, it is weaker than requiring an absolute bound on all outcomes.
In this way, Assumption
4
still allows for growth of some of the outcomes.

The second assumption is
non-super-efficiency
, which places lower bounds on the variance of the effect estimator.

Assumption 5

(Non-Superefficient)
.

The variance of the modified Horvitz–Thompson estimator is bounded below as
\Varsym\lparen\hat{\tau}\rparen\geq\Omega\lparen\lambda(\mathcal{H})/n\rparen
.

Theorem
4.3
shows that under the bounded second moment assumption, the variance of the modified Horvitz–Thompson estimator is bounded as
\Varsym\lparen\hat{\tau}\rparen\leq\mathcal{O}\lparen\lambda(\mathcal{H})/n\rparen
.
Assumption
5
posits that the variance is bounded below by the same rate.
While a non-superefficiency assumption is typically superfluous in an i.i.d. sampling based framework, some form of this an assumption is required for valid inference in design-based inference settings
(see e.g.,
5
;
45
;
60
;
32
)
.
For example, Assumption
5
rules out the case where all outcomes are equal to zero so that the estimator is zero with probability 1.
Generally speaking, Assumption
5
is ruling out potential outcomes which collude with the experimental design to achieve exceptionally fast rates of convergence, which are considered to be knife edge cases not relevant to practice.

To better contextualize these additional assumptions, suppose that potential outcomes are drawn i.i.d. from a super-population.
Under standard assumptions on the super-population (e.g. existence of fourth moments), then simple applications of the law of large numbers and second moment method imply that Assumptions
4
and
5
both hold with probability tending to
1
as
n\to\infty
.
We provide the details in Section
E.1
of the Supplementary Materials.

5.1
Variance Estimation

Standard methods for constructing confidence intervals from a point estimator require an estimate of its variance.
Unfortunately, neither unbiased and nor consistent variance estimation is possible within a design based framework.
The reason is that the variance
\Varsym\lparen\hat{\tau}\rparen
contains many products of fundamentally unobservable terms such as
y_{i}(e_{1})\cdot y_{i}(e_{0})
.
Under arbitrary neighborhood interference, there are additional unobservable products of outcomes, including those between neighboring units in the conflict graph.
Given that consistent variance estimation is impossible, experimenters often opt for conservative estimates of the variance, i.e. those that are upwardly biased.
Using these conservative variance estimates results in inferential procedures which are similarly conservative.

The standard approach to constructing a conservative variance estimator is to first construct an estimable upper bound on the variance; that is, one that does not contain products of unobservable terms.
While a variety of estimable variance upper bounds have been proposed in the no-interference setting
(see e.g.,
50
;
43
;
72
;
56
;
1
;
38
;
4
)
, there are considerably fewer methods for obtaining estimable upper bounds under interference.
Aronow and Samii (2013, 2017) provide a general recipe for obtaining variance bounds using the AM-GM inequality, but this has been shown to be overly conservative, especially in interference settings.

29
propose a systematic approach for obtaining variance bounds under exposure mapping models of interference.
However, their approach is computationally expensive and typically does not yield a closed form solution, which poses challenges for ensuring consistent estimation.

We propose a variance bound based on the operator norm of the covariance matrix of inverse probability weighted indicators.
Let
\boldsymbol{V}
be a
2n
-by-
2n
matrix whose entries are indexed by
(i,k),(j,\ell)
with
i,j\in[n]
and
k,\ell\in\{0,1\}
and are given by

\boldsymbol{V}\Big\lparen(i,k),(j,\ell)\Big\rparen=(-1)^{\mathbf{1}[k\neq\ell]}\Covsym\Big\lparen\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(i,k)}]}{\Pr\lparen E_{(i,k)}\rparen},\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(j,\ell)}]}{\Pr\lparen E_{(j,\ell)}\rparen}\Big\rparen

and let
\lambda(\boldsymbol{V})
be its largest eigenvalue.
The variance bound is given by

n\cdot\mathrm{VB}\triangleq\lambda(\boldsymbol{V})\cdot\Bigg\{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}y_{i}(e_{1})^{2}+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}y_{i}(e_{0})^{2}\Bigg\}\kern 5.0pt.

The variance bound
\mathrm{VB}
contains only squared individual outcomes, which are each observed with positive probability.
The principle behind our construction of this variance bound is the same principle behind our analysis:
the effect estimator is linear in the observed outcomes so that the variance is a quadratic form in the potential outcomes.
Thus, the operator norm of this quadratic form yields an upper bound.
Moreover, this upper bound does not feature problematic unobservable terms which cannot be estimated.
While Theorem
4.1
derives the upper bound
\lambda(\boldsymbol{V})\leq 12.5\cdot\lambda(\mathcal{H})
, we can use the largest eigenvalue directly for the purposes of variance estimation.
Using the exact eigenvalue has the additional benefit that the variance bound is sharp, i.e. equality holds for some potential outcomes.

Proposition 5.1
.

The variance bound is conservative:
\Varsym\lparen\hat{\tau}\rparen\leq\mathrm{VB}
.
Moreover, equality holds for potential outcomes which align with the leading eigenvector of
\boldsymbol{V}
.

In order to estimate the variance bound, we propose a Horvitz–Thompson style estimator.
The variance estimator takes a similar form as the effect estimator:

\displaystyle n\cdot\widehat{\mathrm{VB}}=\lambda(\boldsymbol{V})\cdot\Bigg\lparen\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}Y_{i}^{2}\Bigg\{\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(i,1)}]}{\Pr\lparen E_{(i,1)}\rparen}+\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(i,0)}]}{\Pr\lparen E_{(i,0)}\rparen}\Bigg\}\Bigg\rparen\kern 5.0pt.

In addition to the observed outcomes
Y_{1}\dots Y_{n}
and intervention
Z
, the variance estimator
\widehat{\mathrm{VB}}
requires knowledge of the probability of desired exposure events
\Pr\lparen E_{i,k}\rparen
.
As shown in Lemma
3.3
, these probabilities have simple closed form expressions which may be easily computed.
This is different from most experimental designs under network interference, which require estimating design probabilities through Monte Carlo simulation.

The proposition below shows that the variance estimator is an unbiased estimate of the variance bound and that the expected variance estimator has the same
\mathcal{O}\lparen\lambda(\mathcal{H})/n\rparen
rates as the true variance, which ensures that the width of confidence intervals will inherit corresponding rates.
We remark that the Aronow-Samii variance bound would be much larger, typically scaling as
\Omega\lparen d_{\max}(\mathcal{H})/n\rparen
.
This would effectively lose the benefit of the improved rates of the Conflict Graph Design.

Proposition 5.2
.

Under Assumption
1
, the variance estimator is unbiased for the variance bound:
\Esym[\widehat{\mathrm{VB}}]=\mathrm{VB}
.
Under Assumptions
1
-
2
, the expected variance bound has the rate:
\Esym[\widehat{\mathrm{VB}}]=\mathcal{O}\lparen\lambda(\mathcal{H})/n\rparen
.

We now seek to bound the error of the variance estimator to the variance bound.
The rate guaranteed by Theorem
4.3
together with the non-superefficiency assumption (Assumption
5
) ensure that the variance goes down at a rate of
\Theta\lparen\lambda(\mathcal{H})/n\rparen
.
Thus, the most relevant error of the variance estimator is that after a
n/\lambda(\mathcal{H})
normalization.

Proposition 5.3
.

Under Assumptions
1
and
4
,

the additive error between the normalized variance estimator and the variance bound scales as

\Big\lvert\frac{n}{\lambda(\mathcal{H})}\cdot\widehat{\mathrm{VB}}-\frac{n}{\lambda(\mathcal{H})}\cdot\mathrm{VB}\Big\rvert=\mathcal{O}_{p}\Bigg\lparen\sqrt{\frac{\lambda(\mathcal{H})}{n}\ }\Bigg\rparen\kern 5.0pt.

Just as the point estimator converges as a rate of
\mathcal{O}\lparen\lambda(\mathcal{H})/n\rparen
, so too does the normalized variance estimator.
Thus, the
\lambda(\mathcal{H})=o\lparen n\rparen
condition on the graph is sufficient for both consistent point estimation and consistent variance estimation.
Proposition
5.3
, non-superefficiency, and the continuous mapping theorem imply that the variance estimator is stable, i.e.
\mathrm{VB}/\widehat{\mathrm{VB}}\xrightarrow{p}1
.

5.2
Confidence Intervals

In this section, we propose two types of confidence intervals for the aggregated treatment effect and analyze their asymptotic coverage.
For a given level
\alpha\in(0,1]
, we define the intervals

\displaystyle C_{\textrm{Cheb}}(\alpha)

\displaystyle=\hat{\tau}\pm\frac{1}{\sqrt{\alpha}}\cdot\sqrt{\widehat{\mathrm{VB}}}

\displaystyle C_{\textrm{Wald}}(\alpha)

\displaystyle=\hat{\tau}\pm\Phi^{-1}(1-\alpha/2)\cdot\sqrt{\widehat{\mathrm{VB}}}\kern 5.0pt,

where
\Phi^{-1}:(0,1)\to\mathbb{R}
is the quantile function of a standard normal.
The first interval is based on a Chebyshev bound while the second interval is a standard Wald-type interval based on a normal approximation.
By Proposition
5.2
, the expected width of both intervals is
\mathcal{O}\lparen\sqrt{\lambda(\mathcal{H})/n}\rparen
; however, the dependence on
\alpha
will be different.
The Chebyshev intervals will be wider than the Wald type intervals and this difference becomes more pronounced for smaller
\alpha
.
For example, at level
\alpha=0.05
we have that
\frac{1}{\sqrt{\alpha}}\approx 4.47
and
\Phi^{-1}(1-\alpha/2)\approx 1.96
so that the Chebyshev interval is roughly
2.28
times larger than the Wald type interval.
However, we shall see below that the narrower Wald-type intervals require stronger conditions on the underlying graph in order to achieve asymptotic coverage.

We begin by demonstrating asymptotic coverage of the Chebyshev-based intervals.

Theorem 5.4
.

Under Assumptions
1
-
5
, the Chebyshev intervals asymptotically cover at the nominal rates: for each
\alpha\in(0,1]
,
\liminf_{n\to\infty}\Pr\lparen\tau\in C_{\textrm{Cheb}}(\alpha)\rparen\geq 1-\alpha
.

The Chebyshev intervals require relatively weak assumptions on the underlying network.
This is because the interval itself is based on a finite sample tail bound which holds for any random variable and so the only substantive requirement is that the variance estimator converges appropriately.
As we saw in the previous section, this does not require stronger conditions on the underlying network than what is required for consistent point estimation.

Wald-type intervals are based on a normal approximation and so they are only appropriate when the standardized estimator is asymptotically normal.
Theorem
5.5
below provides sufficient conditions for a CLT of the standardized effect estimator under the Conflict Graph Design, which are stronger than the conditions for consistent point estimation.
The proof uses Stein’s method via the dependency graph technique
(
57
)
, which has been commonly employed in network causal inference
(see e.g.,
14
;
51
;
5
;
30
)
.

Theorem 5.5
.

Suppose that for the maximum degree
d_{\textrm{max}}
we have
d_{\textup{max}}(\mathcal{H})=o(n^{1/9})
.
Then, under Assumptions
1
-
5
, the standardized estimator is asymptotically normal under the Conflict Graph Design:
(\hat{\tau}-\tau)/\sqrt{\Varsym\lparen\hat{\tau}\rparen}\xrightarrow{d}\mathcal{N}(0,1)
.

Corollary 5.6
.

Under Assumptions
1
-
5
and
d_{\textup{max}}(\mathcal{H})=o(n^{1/9})
, the Wald intervals asymptotically cover at the nominal rates: for each
\alpha\in(0,1]
,
\liminf_{n\to\infty}\Pr\lparen\tau\in C_{\textrm{Wald}}(\alpha)\rparen\geq 1-\alpha
.

The condition that the maximum degree of the conflict graph is asymptotically bounded as
d_{\max}(\mathcal{H})=o\lparen n^{1/9}\rparen
required by Theorem
5.5
and Corollary
5.6
for a CLT and asymptotic coverage of Wald-type intervals is substantially stronger than what is required for consistency of point estimation and variance estimation.
This is due, in some part, to fundamental limitations of the proof technique underlying Theorem
5.5
, i.e. Stein’s method via a dependency graph.
However, we believe that stronger conditions on the underlying network are necessary for a standard CLT to hold.
The following proposition demonstrates that even when the conditions for consistency are met, the standardized estimator may not converge in distribution.

Proposition 5.7
.

Suppose the causal estimand is the direct effect.
There exists a sequence of graphs with
\lambda(\mathcal{H})=\Theta\left(\sqrt{n}\right)
and a sequence of outcome functions
y^{(n)}
satisfying Assumptions
1
-
5
, such that the standardized estimator
(\hat{\tau}-\tau)/\sqrt{\Varsym\lparen\hat{\tau}\rparen}
does not converge in distribution under the Conflict Graph Design.

The sequence of graphs alluded to in Proposition
5.7
is based on a simple construction, where for even and odd
n
we choose graphs from two different sequences.
For even
n
, we select a star graph and for odd
n
we select a graph that is formed by starting from a clique on
\sqrt{n}
nodes and attaching to each node a separate
\sqrt{n}
-node clique.
Along the two subsequences, the standardized estimator converges to two different limiting distributions, thus the overall sequence does not converge.

The issue here is that even when the largest eigenvalue is modest (i.e.
\lambda(\mathcal{H})=\mathcal{O}\lparen\sqrt{n}\rparen
), the maximum degree could vary between
\Theta\lparen\sqrt{n}\rparen
and
\Theta\lparen n\rparen
.
These variations in the maximum degree can actually translate into variations in the limiting distributions, which is at the heart of this illustration.

This counter example demonstrates that constructing Wald type intervals through standard techniques requires either stronger assumptions than point estimation or a different approach all together.

Whether experimenters ought to use Chebyshev intervals or Wald-type intervals is an intricate question.
On the one hand, the Chebyshev interval is appropriate whenever the graph is sufficiently well behaved so that precise estimation of the aggregate effect is possible.
On the other hand, while Wald-type intervals seem only to be applicable for a restricted class of graphs, they are narrower and thus more desirable.
At the present moment, we recommend that experimenters run numerical simulations using realistically generated outcomes to determine whether the Wald type intervals may cover at the nominal rates for their specific experiment.

6
Numerical Simulations

In this section, we present numerical simulations to evaluate the proposed methods.
We focus our simulations on the global average treatment effect and the direct treatment effect, given that they are the most well-studied in the literature.
In our simulations, we vary the sample size from
n=500
to
n=3,000
.

We construct networks from a preferential attachment model
(
8
;
19
)
, which has been used to model the World Wide Web, genetic networks, and social networks to name a few.
A network on
n
vertices is sampled from the preferential attachment model given parameters
r
and
m
by the following iterative procedure: the graph is initialized to have
m
nodes with no edges.
At each iteration
t=m+1,\dots n
, the
t
th node is added to the network and attached to
m
nodes, which are selected randomly and proportional to
\textrm{deg}_{t}(i)^{r}
.
In this way, new nodes are more likely to connect with nodes which already have many connections, a preference that increases with the parameter
r
.
In our simulations, we set
m=4
and we selected the exponent parameters
r=1.0
for Global Average Treatment Effect and
r=1.5
for the Direct Treatment Effect.
The motivation for selecting these values of
r
was to create problem instances which displayed the following properties: (i)
\lambda(\mathcal{H})\ll n
so that consistency is possible and (ii)
\lambda(\mathcal{H})\ll d_{\textrm{max}}(\mathcal{H})
so that the problem instance is challenging.

For both estimands of interest, we construct potential outcomes according to the arbitrary neighborhood interference model, i.e. Assumption
1
.
Potential outcomes are constructed by randomly sampling the “control exposure” outcomes as
y_{i}(e_{0})
and the “treatment exposure” outcome
y_{i}(e_{1})
as

y_{i}(e_{0})=\alpha_{i,1}\quad\text{and}\quad y_{i}(e_{1})=\alpha_{i,2}\cdot\sqrt{\textrm{deg}_{G}(i)}\kern 5.0pt,

where the coefficients are sampled independently across units as
\alpha_{i,1}\sim\mathcal{N}(1,1)
and
\alpha_{i,2}\sim\mathcal{N}(2,1)
.
Constructing outcomes in this way creates outcome heterogeneity, providing a challenging problem instance where nodes with high degree also have large individual treatment effects.
This construction of the outcomes also ensures the second moments will be bounded, i.e. Assumption
2
holds.
Although the network and outcomes are constructed randomly, this random construction occurs only once and the reported statistics (e.g. variance, coverage, etc) are conditioned on this single random construction of networks and outcomes.

Our simulations feature both standard Horvitz–Thompson estimators and the modified Horvitz–Thompson estimator introduced in Section
3.4
.
As discussed in Section
3.4
, the probabilities of desired exposure used in the modified Horvitz–Thompson estimator can be computed exactly.
However, the probabilities of actual exposure used in the standard Horvitz–Thompson estimator require Monte Carlo simulation across all designs, for which we use
k=10,000
draws from the designs.
These
k=10,000
random draws are also used to report the statistical properties of the designs e.g. variance, interval widths, coverage, etc.
Due to space constraints, we focus on the variance of the experimental designs here in the main body and report on the inferential procedures and practical recommendations in the Supplementary Material.

(a)

Global Average Treatment Effect

(b)

Direct Treatment Effect

Figure 3
:

Comparison of the variance of various experimental designs.
Figure
3(a)
compares RGCR and Conflict Graph Design for estimating the Global Average Treatment Effect.
Figure
3(b)
shows the Independent Set Design and Conflict Graph Design for estimating the Direct Treatment Effect.

Global Average Treatment Effect:

In addition to the Conflict Graph Design for the global average treatment effect, we implemented 1-Hop and 3-Net Randomized Graph Cluster Randomization (RGCR) designs with spectral weighting
(
66
)
, which are current state-of-the-art experimental designs for this effect.
Figure
3(a)
shows a plot of the variance for each design from which we see several findings.
The modified Horvitz–Thompson estimator achieves a slightly lower variance under the Conflict Graph Design than its standard counterpart, though both are comparable.
More interesting is that the RGCR designs achieve variances which are roughly 4 times lower than that of the Conflict Graph Design.
This improvement of RGCR over CGD is not a general phenomenon: in Section
A.5
of the supplement, we construct families of graphs where RGCR incurs an exponentially worse rate than the CGD.
Nevertheless, we feel that this empirical observation may be insightful for future development of experimental designs.

Direct Treatment Effect:

In addition to the Conflict Graph Design for the direct treatment effect, we implemented the Independent Set design of
42
.
Among the few designs targeted for estimating the direct effect, we chose this one as it had been most extensively evaluated through numerical simulations.
Figure
3(b)
shows a plot of the variance for each design, which demonstrates two findings.
First, the modified and standard Horvitz–Thompson estimators are comparable under the Conflict Graph Design.
We also find that the Conflict Graph Design achieves a variance which is roughly between 3-7 times lower than that of the Independent Set Design.
This is because the Independent Set Design will assign treatment exposure to high degree nodes with low probability, thereby incurring high variance as these are precisely the nodes which have large outcomes.
We found that this difference becomes more pronounced for larger values of the exponent
r
.

7
Conclusion

In this work, we have presented the Conflict Graph Design and a modified Horvitz—Thompson estimator which are constructed to estimate a single experimenter-specified causal effect in a network experiment to high precision.
We have shown that the methods attain
\Varsym\lparen\hat{\tau}\rparen\leq\mathcal{O}\lparen\lambda(\mathcal{H})/n\rparen
rates, which depend not only on the underlying network but also the causal effect.
This general approach to experiment design yields improved rates for the most well-studied causal effects as well as new results for causal effects which have received less attention in the literature on experiment design.
We have also proposed confidence intervals which feature similar rates and provided sufficient conditions for their asymptotic validity.
Finally, we believe that the operator norm analysis carried out in this work will prove to be a useful technique for analyzing future methods under interference.

The most intriguing open question raised by this work is whether the
\lambda(\mathcal{H})/n
rate is optimal or whether it can be improved.
Broadly speaking, resolving questions of optimality would require the development of lower bounds on rates of estimation in network experiments.
This is an especially challenging task given the lack of well-developed efficiency theory for design-based inference, which stands in stark contrast to the mature efficiency theory developed within a super-population framework
(see e.g.,
44
)
.
Another open question is the development of tighter Wald-type intervals in the
\lambda(\mathcal{H})=o\lparen n\rparen
consistency regime, where Proposition
5.7
shows that the standardized estimator will generally not have a limiting distribution.
We believe this requires the modification or development of specific theoretical tools beyond the usual application of Stein’s method in causal inference under interference.

The Conflict Graph Design and the modified Horvitz–Thompson estimator have certain limitations which may also form the basis for future work.
For example, these methods do not use covariate information, which is often available to the experimenter.
The AIPW estimator, which uses a fitted regression model to improve precision, is the most common way to use covariate information to improve precision.
Understanding when such adjusted estimators can improve precision in network experiments is an open question
(see e.g.
27
)
.
However, we suspect that such improvements will be in efficiency (i.e. constant terms) rather than the rates, which are primarily driven by the experimental design.
A second limitation is that the proposed methods are designed to estimate a single causal effect to high precision.
It will typically be ill advised to run the Conflict Graph Design for a primary estimand when multiple secondary estimands are also of interest, due to possible positivity violations.
It is an interesting open problem is to construct a network experimental design to increase the precision of multiple causal effect estimators simultaneously.
It seems likely to us that this would involve a trade-off: the more estimands that are considered, the worse the rate of estimation for each of them must be.

Appendix

Appendix A
Additional Results and Discussion

A.1
Interference and Mediation

In this section, we draw some comparisons between two subfields of causal inference: network interference and mediation analysis.
Broadly speaking, these two fields are distinct from each other.
At the same time, they share many of the same terminology such as “direct effects” and “fundamental unobservability”.
This is unfortunate because the underlying problems and solutions referenced by these shared terms differ substantially between the two fields.
The goal of this section is to clarify these differences.

In the context of mediation analysis, the well-studied natural direct and indirect effects are defined as

\textrm{NDE}=\Esym[Y(1,M(0))-Y(0,M(0))]\quad\text{and}\quad\textrm{NIE}=\Esym[Y(1,M(1))-Y(1,M(0))]\kern 5.0pt.

For a comprehensive treatment of mediation, we refer the reader to the excellent textbook of
67
.
In the context of mediation analysis, the term “fundamental unobservability” refers to the fact that neither of the cross-world counterfactuals
Y(1,M(0))
and
Y(0,M(1))
can be observed in the data if
M(1)\neq M(0)
.
This issue persists even in a randomized experiment because it is impossible to realize treatment
Z=1
and mediator
M=M(0)
simultaneously.
This means that without further assumptions, the natural direct and indirect effects are not identifiable, i.e. no unbiased estimator exists.
The typical solution is to posit exclusion restrictions or cross-world independence assumptions so that unbiased estimation of the causal effect is possible.

Following
5
, the majority of the subsequent literature on interference has focused on estimating contrastive effects, defined as:

\tau=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}y_{i}(e_{1})-y_{i}(e_{0})\kern 5.0pt.

In the context of our paper, the term “fundamental unobservability” refers to the fact that there are pairs of potential outcomes
y_{i}(e_{k})
and
y_{j}(e_{\ell})
which cannot be
observed simultaneously
.
For example, the two potential outcomes
y_{i}(e_{1})
and
y_{i}(e_{0})
for a single unit are not simultaneously observable because unit
i
cannot receive both exposures
e_{1}
and
e_{0}
simultaneously.
Under network interference, potential outcomes
y_{i}(e_{1})
and
y_{j}(e_{0})
between neighboring units
i
and
j
will not be simultaneously observable if there is no intervention
\boldsymbol{z}
which results in unit
i
receiving exposure
e_{1}
and unit
j
receiving exposure
e_{0}
.
In this work, we introduce the conflict graph for the purpose of encoding this “fundamental observability”, i.e. the conflict graph has edges between pairs of units with simultaneously unobservable potential outcomes.

In the context of network experiments, this problem of fundamental unobservability is not one of identification: indeed, randomization alone is enough to ensure that the contrastive effect can be unbiasedly estimated.
Instead, the fundamental unobservability introduces a problem of
precision
.
If two potential outcomes between two units cannot be simultaneously observed, then this means that their corresponding terms in an estimator will necessarily be highly correlated.
The solution to this problem is not to place additional assumptions (e.g. exclusion restrictions, cross-world independence, etc), but rather to carefully construct the randomization of treatment so that the effect estimator is as precise as possible.
In this work, we have shown that under the Conflict Graph Design, it is possible to construct an unbiased effect estimator whose variance is of order
\lambda(\mathcal{H})/n
.

In summary, the term “fundamental unobservability” references different problems—with different solutions— in the contexts of mediation and interference.
In mediation analysis, cross-world outcomes are not directly observable without further assumptions, which represents a problem of identification.
In network interference, pairs of potential outcomes are not simultaneously observable, which only poses a problem for precision.
While the two fields are largely distinct, connections between them serve as a fruitful area for research.
Initial works in this direction include
68
and
37
.

A.2
Extension to Contrastive Marginal Effects

In this section, we demonstrate how the Conflict Graph Design may be used to estimate the more general class of contrastive marginal casual effects.
All the results in this section may be viewed as strict generalizations of the main results presented in the paper for the class of contrastive effects.

A.2.1
Marginal Contrastive Effects

We begin by defining the class of marginal contrastive effects.
Suppose that for each unit
i\in[n]
, the experimenter specifies two contrasting distributions
\mathcal{D}_{i,0}
and
\mathcal{D}_{i,1}
over intervention vectors
\boldsymbol{z}\in\{0,1\}^{n}
.
We define the corresponding
contrastive marginal effect
as

\tau=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Esym_{#1}\big[y_{i}(\boldsymbol{z})\big]-\Esym_{#1}\big[y_{i}(\boldsymbol{z})\big]\kern 5.0pt.

In other words, the contrastive marginal effect is the difference in the expected potential outcomes under the contrasting distributions
\mathcal{D}_{i,1}
and
\mathcal{D}_{i,0}
, averaged over all units in the experiment.
We view the contrasting distributions as being defined separately from the experimental design.
For example, the class of contrastive effects studied in the main paper correspond to the case where each of the contrasting distributions places all its mass on one treatment assignment.

(a)

Exposure
e_{1}

(b)

Exposure
e_{2}

(c)

Exposure
e_{3}

(d)

Exposure
e_{4}

Figure 4
:
Four distinct exposures which all correspond to “50% of neighbors are treated”

Certain causal questions may lend themselves more naturally to the class of contrastive marginal effects.
For example, consider the question of “what is the effect of having 50% treated neighbors”?
The central difficulty of writing this as a contrastive effect of the form
\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}y_{i}(e_{1})-y_{i}(e_{0})
is that under the arbitrary neighborhood interference model, “50% treated neighbors
” does not correspond to a unique exposure
e_{1}
.
Indeed, there will typically be many exposures that may satisfy this condition (see Figure
4
) and it is not clear which one we ought to use as
e_{1}
.
One approach forward is to re-interpret the causal question as implicitly marginalizing over all the interventions
z
in which “50% of neighbors are treated” is true.
In other words, we may consider the marginal contrastive effect obtained by choosing the contrastive distributions as follows:
D_{i,1}
is uniform on the set of assignments
\{\boldsymbol{z}:\frac{1}{N(i)}\sum_{j\in N(i)}\boldsymbol{z}_{j}=1/2\}
and
D_{i,0}
places all mass on assignment
\boldsymbol{z}=\boldsymbol{0}
.
This example shows that this broader class of estimands is more expressive.

This definition of marginalized contrastive treatment effect is independent of the arbitrary neighborhood interference model.
However, under the arbitrary neighborhood interference model, the class of marginal contrastive effects may equivalently be defined with respect to distributions on exposures, i.e.

\tau=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Esym_{#1}\big[y_{i}(e_{1})\big]-\Esym_{#1}\big[y_{i}(e_{0})\big]\kern 5.0pt.

Without loss of generality, we will continue to work with the definition which uses contrastive distributions over treatment assignments.

A.2.2
Generalized Conflict Graph

The conflict graph introduced in the main paper depended on both the underlying network and the contrastive effect.
In this section, we generalize that definition to be appropriate for the class of marginalized contrastive effect.
We begin by defining what it means for two units to be in conflict:

Definition 3

(Conflicting Units)
.

Two experimental units
i,j\in[n]
are in
conflict
if there exists treatment assignments
\boldsymbol{z}_{i}
in the support of
\mathcal{D}_{i,k}
and
\boldsymbol{z}_{j}
in the support of
\mathcal{D}_{j,\ell}
such that there is no assignment vector
\boldsymbol{z}
such that
d_{i}(\boldsymbol{z})=d_{i}(\boldsymbol{z}_{i})
and
d_{j}(\boldsymbol{z})=d_{i}(\boldsymbol{z}_{j})
.

Following the main paper, we define the
conflict graph

\mathcal{H}
to be a graph with units
i\in[n]
as vertices with an edge between units
i
and
j
if they are in conflict.
As before, the conflict graph depends both on the underlying network and the causal effect.

A.2.3
Generalized Conflict Graph Design and Estimator

The generalized Conflict Graph Design is given formally as Algorithm
2
.
The procedure is similar to the original conflict graph design: the “desired exposures” are drawn i.i.d. and conflicts are resolved via an importance ordering.
The key difference is that if a unit
i\in[n]
is selected to receive
e_{k}
(and all of its more important neighbors receive ‘*’) then the experimenter will draw a treatment assignment
\boldsymbol{z}\sim\mathcal{D}_{i,k}
, and this will be used to determine treatment given to unit
i
and its neighborhood.

Input:

Importance ordering
\pi
, maximum eigenvalue
\lambda(\mathcal{H})
, conflict graph
\mathcal{H}
and distributions
\mathcal{D}_{i,0},\mathcal{D}_{i,1}
for all
i\in[n]
.

Output:

Random intervention
Z\in\mathcal{Z}=\{0,1\}^{n}

Set sampling parameter
r=2

1

Sample desired exposure variables
U_{1},\dots,U_{n}
independently and identically as

U_{i}\leftarrow\left\{\begin{array}[]{lr}e_{1}&\text{with probability }\frac{1}{r\cdot 2\lambda(\mathcal{H})}\\
e_{0}&\text{with probability }\frac{1}{r\cdot 2\lambda(\mathcal{H})}\\
&\text{with probability }1-\frac{1}{r\cdot\lambda(\mathcal{H})}\end{array}\right.

2

Sample variables
Z^{(i,0)}\sim\mathcal{D}_{i,0}
and
Z^{(i,1)}\sim\mathcal{D}_{i,1}
for all
i\in[n]
independently as

Z^{(i,0)}\sim\mathcal{D}_{i,0}\quad,\quad Z^{(i,1)}\sim\mathcal{D}_{i,1}

3

Initialize the intervention vector
Z\leftarrow\boldsymbol{0}
.

4

for

i=1\dots n

do

5

if

U_{i}=e_{k}\in\{e_{1},e_{0}\}
and
U_{j}=*
for all
j\in\mathcal{N}^{\pi}_{b}(i)

then

6

Update intervention vector: set
Z_{j}=Z^{(i,k)}(j)
for all
j\in\widetilde{N}(i)

7

Algorithm 2

Conflict Graph Design for Contrastive Marginal Effects

The modified Horvitz–Thompson estimator may be derived in a similar way.
We define the desired exposure events as

\displaystyle E_{(i,1)}

\displaystyle=\{U_{i}=e_{1},U_{j}=*\text{ ,for all }j\in\mathcal{N}^{\pi}_{b}(i)\}

\displaystyle E_{(i,0)}

\displaystyle=\{U_{i}=e_{0},U_{j}=*\text{ ,for all }j\in\mathcal{N}^{\pi}_{b}(i)\}\kern 5.0pt.

The estimator is defined as

\hat{\tau}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}Y_{i}\cdot\Big\lparen\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(i,1)}]}{\Pr\lparen E_{(i,1)}\rparen}-\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(i,0)}]}{\Pr\lparen E_{(i,0)}\rparen}\Big\rparen

(2)

A.2.4
Analysis of Bias and Variance

In the setting of marginalized contrastive effects, we will find it helpful to further divide the desired exposure events
E_{(i,0)},E_{(i,1)}
according to which particular exposure will be observed.
Thus, for each unit
i
and exposure
z\in\{0,1\}^{|\tilde{N}(i)|}
, we define the events

\displaystyle E_{(i,1,z)}

\displaystyle=\{U_{i}=e_{1},U_{j}=*\text{ for all }j\in\mathcal{N}^{\pi}_{b}(i),Z^{(i,1)}=z\}

\displaystyle E_{(i,0,z)}

\displaystyle=\{U_{i}=e_{0},U_{j}=*\text{ for all }j\in\mathcal{N}^{\pi}_{b}(i),Z^{(i,0)}=z\}

We begin by first establishing a convenient expression for the probabilities of events
E_{(i,1,z)},E_{(i,0,z)}
.
The proof is very similar to that of Lemma
3.3
.

Lemma A.1
.

Under the Conflict Graph Design, for every node
i
, every contrast
k\in\{0,1\}
and every exposure
z\in\{0,1\}^{|\tilde{N}(i)|}
, we have

\displaystyle\Pr\lparen E_{(i,k,z)}\rparen=\Pr\lparen E_{(i,k)}\rparen\cdot\Pr_{#1}\lparen z\rparen=\frac{1}{2r\lambda(\mathcal{H})}\Big\lparen 1-\frac{1}{r\lambda(\mathcal{H})}\Big\rparen^{|\mathcal{N}_{b}^{\pi}(i)|}\cdot\Pr_{#1}\lparen z\rparen\kern 5.0pt.

Proof.

It has already been established in Lemma
3.3
for the usual Conflict Graph Design that

\Pr\lparen E_{(i,k)}\rparen=\frac{1}{2r\lambda(\mathcal{H})}\Big\lparen 1-\frac{1}{r\lambda(\mathcal{H})}\Big\rparen^{|\mathcal{N}_{b}^{\pi}(i)|}\kern 5.0pt.

This of course continues to hold with the design of Algorithm
2
, since the event is exactly the same.
In order for
E_{(i,k,z)}
to occur, we need
E_{(i,k)}
to occur and also
Z^{(i,k)}=z
. Since
E_{(i,k)}
depends on
U
variables, that are sampled independently of the
Z
variables, we have

\Pr\lparen E_{(i,k,z)}\rparen=\Pr\lparen E_{(i,k)}\rparen\cdot\Pr\lparen Z^{(i,k)}=z\rparen=\Pr\lparen E_{(i,k)}\rparen\cdot\Pr_{#1}\lparen z\rparen\kern 5.0pt.\qed

In the next Lemma, we establish that the estimator
\hat{\tau}
is unbiased, similarly to how we did for the modified Horvitz-Thompson estimator in Proposition
3.5
.

Proposition A.2
.

Under arbitrary neighborhood interference (Assumption
1
), the modified Horvitz–Thompson estimator
\hat{\tau}
is unbiased under the Conflict Graph Design:
\Esym[\hat{\tau}]=\tau
.

Proof.

We have

\displaystyle\Esym[\hat{\tau}]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Esym\Bigg[\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(i,1)}]}{\Pr\lparen E_{(i,1)}\rparen}\cdot Y_{i}-\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(i,0)}]}{\Pr\lparen E_{(i,0)}\rparen}\cdot Y_{i}\Bigg]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\Esym\big[\mathbf{1}[E_{(i,1)}]\cdot Y_{i}\big]}{\Pr\lparen E_{(i,1)}\rparen}-\frac{\Esym\big[\mathbf{1}[E_{(i,0)}]\cdot Y_{i}\big]}{\Pr\lparen E_{(i,0)}\rparen}

(3)

Let us know compute
\Esym\big[\mathbf{1}[E_{(i,k)}]\cdot Y_{i}\big]
. We have

\displaystyle\Esym\big[\mathbf{1}[E_{(i,k)}]\cdot Y_{i}\big]

\displaystyle=\Esym\Big[\sum_{z\in\{0,1\}^{|\tilde{N}(i)|}}\mathbf{1}[E_{(i,k,z)}]\cdot Y_{i}\Big]

\displaystyle=\sum_{z\in\{0,1\}^{|\tilde{N}(i)|}}\Esym\big[\mathbf{1}[E_{(i,k,z)}]\big]\cdot y_{i}(z)

\displaystyle=\sum_{z\in\{0,1\}^{|\tilde{N}(i)|}}\Pr\lparen E_{(i,k)}\rparen\Pr_{#1}\lparen z\rparen\cdot y_{i}(z)\kern 5.0pt,

where in the last step we used Lemma
A.1
.
Plugging this inside (
3
) yields the result.

∎

We can also establish an upper bound on the variance of the modified Horvitz–Thompson estimator under the generalized Conflict Graph Design, similarly to Theorem
4.1
.
The theorem below is nearly identical to Theorem
4.1
, except that the variance is bounded by the expected second moment with respect to the contrastive distributions
\mathcal{D}_{i,1}
and
\mathcal{D}_{i,0}
.
In this way, the magnitude of the potential outcomes is measured with respect to the contrastive distributions.

Theorem A.3
.

Under arbitrary neighborhood interference (Assumption
1
), there exists a constant
C>0
such that the variance of the modified Horvitz–Thompson estimator
\hat{\tau}
under the Conflict Graph Design is bounded as

\Varsym\lparen\hat{\tau}\rparen\leq C\cdot\frac{\lambda(\mathcal{H})}{n}\cdot\Bigg\{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Esym_{#1}[y_{i}(\boldsymbol{z})^{2}]+\Esym_{#1}[y_{i}(\boldsymbol{z})^{2}]\Bigg\}\kern 5.0pt.

Proof.

As we did in the proof of unbiasedness in Lemma
A.2
, it will be convenient to write the estimator in the following form.

\hat{\tau}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{z\in\{0,1\}^{|\tilde{N}(i)|}}\Pr_{#1}\lparen z\rparen\cdot\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(i,1,z)}]}{\Pr\lparen E_{(i,1,z)}\rparen}\cdot y_{i}(z)-\sum_{z\in\{0,1\}^{|\tilde{N}(i)|}}\Pr_{#1}\lparen z\rparen\cdot\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(i,0,z)}]}{\Pr\lparen E_{(i,0,z)}\rparen}\cdot y_{i}(z)

We start by decomposing our estimator and estimand as

\displaystyle\hat{\tau}=

\displaystyle\underbrace{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{z\in\{0,1\}^{|\tilde{N}(i)|}}\Pr_{#1}\lparen z\rparen\cdot\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(i,1,z)}]}{\Pr\lparen E_{(i,1,z)}\rparen}y_{i}(z)}_{\hat{\tau}_{1}}-\underbrace{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{z\in\{0,1\}^{|\tilde{N}(i)|}}\Pr_{#1}\lparen z\rparen\cdot\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(i,0,z)}]}{\Pr\lparen E_{(i,0,z)}\rparen}y_{i}(z)}_{\hat{\tau}_{0}}

\displaystyle\tau=\underbrace{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Esym_{#1}[y_{i}(z)]}_{\tau_{1}}-\underbrace{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Esym_{#1}[y_{i}(z)]}_{\tau_{0}}

Clearly, the proof of Proposition
A.2
shows that
\Esym[\hat{\tau}_{1}]=\tau_{1}
and
\Esym[\hat{\tau}_{0}]=\tau_{0}
. Thus, we have that

\displaystyle\Varsym\lparen\hat{\tau}\rparen

\displaystyle=\Varsym\lparen\hat{\tau}_{1}-\hat{\tau}_{0}\rparen

\displaystyle=\Varsym\lparen\hat{\tau}_{1}\rparen+\Varsym\lparen\hat{\tau}_{0}\rparen-2\Covsym\lparen\hat{\tau}_{1},\hat{\tau}_{0}\rparen

\displaystyle\leq 2\Varsym\lparen\hat{\tau}_{1}\rparen+2\Varsym\lparen\hat{\tau}_{0}\rparen\kern 5.0pt.

In the last step, we used the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality to bound the covariance term and then the AM-GM inequality.
Thus, it suffices to bound the variance of
\hat{\tau}_{1}
and
\hat{\tau}_{0}
separately. We will bound the variance of
\hat{\tau}_{1}
, and a similar bound for
\hat{\tau}_{0}
follows analogously.

We now expand the variance of
\hat{\tau}_{1}
as

\displaystyle\Varsym\lparen\hat{\tau}_{1}\rparen

\displaystyle=\Varsym\Bigg\lparen\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{z\in\{0,1\}^{|\tilde{N}(i)|}}\Pr_{#1}\lparen z\rparen\cdot\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(i,1,z)}]}{\Pr\lparen E_{(i,1,z)}\rparen}y_{i}(z)\Bigg\rparen

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\sum_{z\in\{0,1\}^{|\tilde{N}(i)|}}\sum_{z^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}^{|\tilde{N}(j)|}}C_{(i,j,z,z^{\prime})}\cdot y_{i}(z)y_{j}(z^{\prime})\kern 5.0pt,

(4)

where we defined the covariance terms for every
i,j\in[n]
,
z\in\{0,1\}^{|\tilde{N}(i)|}
and
z^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}^{|\tilde{N}(j)|}
as

C_{(i,j,z,z^{\prime})}=\Pr_{#1}\lparen z\rparen\cdot\Pr_{#1}\lparen z\rparen\cdot\Covsym\Bigg\lparen\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(i,1,z)}]}{\Pr\lparen E_{(i,1,z)}\rparen},\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(j,1,z^{\prime})}]}{\Pr\lparen E_{(j,1,z^{\prime})}\rparen}\Bigg\rparen\kern 5.0pt.

Let us now compute these covariance terms.
We denote by
d(i,j)
the distance between
i,j
in the Conflict Graph
\mathcal{H}
.
Throughout the remainder of the proof, we use
C>0
to denote a (possibly changing) constant which depends only on the choice of
r
in the Conflict Graph Design.

Single Unit
:
First, if
i=j
and
z=z^{\prime}
, we have

\displaystyle C_{(i,i,z,z)}

\displaystyle=\Pr_{#1}\lparen z\rparen^{2}\cdot\Varsym\Big\lparen\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(i,1,z)}]}{\Pr\lparen E_{(i,1,z)}\rparen}\Big\rparen

\displaystyle=\Pr_{#1}\lparen z\rparen^{2}\cdot\Big\lparen\frac{1}{\Pr\lparen E_{(i,1,z)}\rparen}-1\Big\rparen

\displaystyle=\frac{\Pr_{#1}\lparen z\rparen}{\Pr\lparen E_{(i,1)}\rparen}-\Pr_{#1}\lparen z\rparen^{2}

Now suppose
i=j
but
z\neq z^{\prime}
. Clearly, the events
E_{(i,1,z)},E_{(i,1,z^{\prime})}
are disjoint, so the covariance term is

C_{(i,i,z,z^{\prime})}=-\Pr_{#1}\lparen z\rparen^{2}\kern 5.0pt.

Thus, for the terms
i=j
in variance decomposition, we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{z\in\{0,1\}^{|\tilde{N}(i)|}}\sum_{z^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}^{|\tilde{N}(j)|}}C_{(i,j,z,z^{\prime})}\cdot y_{i}(z)y_{i}(z^{\prime})

(5)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Bigg\lparen\sum_{z\in\{0,1\}^{|\tilde{N}(i)|}}\frac{\Pr_{#1}\lparen z\rparen}{\Pr\lparen E_{(i,1)}\rparen}y_{i}(z)^{2}-\Big\lparen\sum_{z\in\{0,1\}^{|\tilde{N}(i)|}}\Pr_{#1}\lparen z\rparen y_{i}(z)\Big\rparen^{2}\Bigg\rparen

\displaystyle\quad\leq\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{z\in\{0,1\}^{|\tilde{N}(i)|}}\frac{\Pr_{#1}\lparen z\rparen}{\Pr\lparen E_{(i,1)}\rparen}y_{i}(z)^{2}

\displaystyle\quad\leq\frac{C\cdot\lambda(\mathcal{H})}{n^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{z\in\{0,1\}^{|\tilde{N}(i)|}}\Pr_{#1}\lparen z\rparen y_{i}(z)^{2}

(Lemma
4.2
)

\displaystyle\quad=\frac{C\cdot\lambda(\mathcal{H})}{n}\cdot\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Esym_{#1}\big[y_{i}(\boldsymbol{z})^{2}\big]\kern 5.0pt.

(6)

Neighboring Units
:
Let us now focus on distinct pairs of units
i,j\in[n]
which are neighbors in the conflict graph
\mathcal{H}
, i.e.
d(i,j)=1
.
For these units, the desired exposure events
E_{(i,1,z)},E_{(j,1,z^{\prime})}
are disjoint.
Thus, the covariance terms for any
z,z^{\prime}
are given as

C_{i,j,z,z^{\prime}}=-\Pr_{#1}\lparen z\rparen\Pr_{#1}\lparen z^{\prime}\rparen\kern 5.0pt.

Thus, the corresponding terms in the variance can be written as

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i\neq j:d(i,j)=1}\sum_{z\in\{0,1\}^{|\tilde{N}(i)|}}\sum_{z^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}^{|\tilde{N}(j)|}}C_{(i,j,z,z^{\prime})}\cdot y_{i}(z)y_{j}(z^{\prime})

(7)

\displaystyle\quad=\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i\neq j:d(i,j)=1}\sum_{z\in\{0,1\}^{|\tilde{N}(i)|}}\sum_{z^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}^{|\tilde{N}(j)|}}\big\lparen-\Pr_{#1}\lparen z\rparen\Pr_{#1}\lparen z^{\prime}\rparen y_{i}(z)y_{j}(z^{\prime})\big\rparen

(8)

\displaystyle\quad=-\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i\neq j:d(i,j)=1}\Big\lparen\sum_{z\in\{0,1\}^{|\tilde{N}(i)|}}\Pr_{#1}\lparen z\rparen y_{i}(z)\Big\rparen\Big\lparen\sum_{z^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}^{|\tilde{N}(i)|}}\Pr_{#1}\lparen z^{\prime}\rparen y_{j}(z^{\prime})\Big\rparen

(9)

\displaystyle\quad=-\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}A_{\mathcal{H}}(i,j)\cdot\Big\lparen\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\Esym_{#1}[y_{i}(\boldsymbol{z})]\Big\rparen\Big\lparen\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\Esym_{#1}[y_{j}(\boldsymbol{z})]\Big\rparen

(10)

\displaystyle\quad\leq\frac{\lambda(\mathcal{H})}{n}\cdot\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Esym_{#1}[y_{i}(\boldsymbol{z})]^{2}

(11)

by definition of operator norm

\displaystyle\quad\leq\frac{\lambda(\mathcal{H})}{n}\cdot\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Esym_{#1}[y_{i}(\boldsymbol{z})^{2}]

(13)

by Jensen’s inequality.

Non-neighboring Units
:
Finally, let us focus on pairs of units
i,j
that are at least distance
2
in the Conflict Graph
\mathcal{H}
.
For any
z,z^{\prime}
, the correlation between events
E_{(i,1,z)},E_{(j,1,z^{\prime})}
is bounded similarly to that of
E_{(i,1)},E_{j,1}
, as the following calculation shows:

\displaystyle C_{i,j,z,z^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\Pr_{#1}\lparen z\rparen\cdot\Pr_{#1}\lparen z\rparen\cdot\Covsym Bigg\lparen\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(i,1,z)}]}{\Pr\lparen E_{(i,1,z)}\rparen},\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(j,1,z^{\prime})}]}{\Pr\lparen E_{(j,1,z^{\prime})}\rparen}Bigg\rparen

\displaystyle=\Pr_{#1}\lparen z\rparen\cdot\Pr_{#1}\lparen z\rparen\cdot\Big\lparen\frac{\Pr\lparen E_{(i,1,z)}\cap E_{(j,1,z^{\prime})}\rparen}{\Pr\lparen E_{(i,1,z)}\rparen\Pr\lparen E_{(j,1,z^{\prime})}\rparen}-1\Big\rparen

\displaystyle=\Pr_{#1}\lparen z\rparen\cdot\Pr_{#1}\lparen z\rparen\cdot\Big\lparen\frac{\Pr_{#1}\lparen z\rparen\cdot\Pr_{#1}\lparen z\rparen\cdot\Pr\lparen E_{(i,1)}\cap E_{(j,1)}\rparen}{\Pr_{#1}\lparen z\rparen\cdot\Pr_{#1}\lparen z\rparen\cdot\Pr\lparen E_{(i,1)}\rparen\cdot\Pr\lparen E_{(j,1)}\rparen}-1\Big\rparen

\displaystyle=\Pr_{#1}\lparen z\rparen\cdot\Pr_{#1}\lparen z\rparen\cdot\Big\lparen\frac{\Pr\lparen E_{(i,1)}\cap E_{(j,1)}\rparen}{\Pr\lparen E_{(i,1)}\rparen\cdot\Pr\lparen E_{(j,1)}\rparen}-1\Big\rparen

\displaystyle\leq C\Pr_{#1}\lparen z\rparen\cdot\Pr_{#1}\lparen z\rparen\cdot\frac{|\mathcal{N}_{b}^{\pi}(i)\cap\mathcal{N}_{b}^{\pi}(j)|}{\lambda(\mathcal{H})}\kern 5.0pt,

where in the final inequality we used Lemma
4.2
for some constant
C
.
Thus, the remaining terms in the variance decomposition may be bounded as

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i\neq j:d(i,j)\geq 2}\sum_{z\in\{0,1\}^{|\tilde{N}(i)|}}\sum_{z^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}^{|\tilde{N}(j)|}}y_{i}(z)y_{j}(z^{\prime})C_{i,j,z,z^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\quad\leq\frac{C}{n^{2}}\sum_{i\neq j:d(i,j)\geq 2}\sum_{z\in\{0,1\}^{|\tilde{N}(i)|}}\sum_{z^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}^{|\tilde{N}(j)|}}\Pr_{#1}\lparen z\rparen\Pr_{#1}\lparen z^{\prime}\rparen|y_{i}(z)y_{j}(z^{\prime})|\frac{|\mathcal{N}(i)\cap\mathcal{N}(j)|}{\lambda(\mathcal{H})}

\displaystyle\quad=\frac{C}{n^{2}}\sum_{i\neq j:d(i,j)\geq 2}\frac{|\mathcal{N}(i)\cap\mathcal{N}(j)|}{\lambda(\mathcal{H})}\Big\lparen\sum_{z\in\{0,1\}^{|\tilde{N}(i)|}}\Pr_{#1}\lparen z\rparen|y_{i}(z)|\Big\rparen\Big\lparen\sum_{z^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}^{|\tilde{N}(i)|}}\Pr_{#1}\lparen z^{\prime}\rparen|y_{j}(z^{\prime})|\Big\rparen

\displaystyle\quad=\frac{C}{n\cdot\lambda(\mathcal{H})}\sum_{i\neq j:d(i,j)\geq 2}\big\lvert\mathcal{N}(i)\cap\mathcal{N}(j)\big\rvert\Big\lparen\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\Esym_{#1}\big[\lvert y_{i}(z)\rvert\big]\Big\rparen\Big\lparen\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\Esym_{#1}\big[\lvert y_{j}(z)\rvert\big]\Big\rparen

\displaystyle\quad\leq\frac{C}{n\cdot\lambda(\mathcal{H})}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}A_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}(i,j)\Big\lparen\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\Esym_{#1}\big[\lvert y_{i}(z)\rvert\big]\Big\rparen\Big\lparen\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\Esym_{#1}\big[\lvert y_{j}(z)\rvert\big]\Big\rparen

\displaystyle\quad\leq\frac{C}{n\cdot\lambda(\mathcal{H})}\cdot\lambda(\mathcal{H})^{2}\cdot\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Esym_{#1}\big[\lvert y_{i}(\boldsymbol{z})\rvert\big]^{2}

by definition of the operator norm

\displaystyle\quad\leq\frac{C\cdot\lambda(\mathcal{H})}{n}\cdot\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Esym_{#1}\big[y_{i}(\boldsymbol{z})^{2}\big]

by Jensen’s inequality.

Thus, by combining the analysis for single units, neighboring units, and non-neighboring units, we have that the variance is bounded as desired, i.e. there exists a constant
C
such that

\Varsym\lparen\hat{\tau}_{1}\rparen\leq C\frac{\lambda(\mathcal{H})}{n}\cdot\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Esym_{#1}[y_{i}(\boldsymbol{z})^{2}]\kern 5.0pt.

Similar arguments can be made for
\Varsym\lparen\hat{\tau}_{2}\rparen
and thus the proof is complete.
∎

Theorem
A.3
is a finite-sample bound that holds for any network.
If we want to interpret this to asymptotically mean that the variance is of order at most
\lambda(\mathcal{H})/n
, then we need to assume that the second moment of the outcomes with respect to the contrastive distributions is asymptotically bounded, which is a generalization of Assumption
2
.

A.3
Concentration of the Number of Exposed Units

In this section, we analyze the concentration properties of the number of units which are assigned to their desired exposure.
Formally, for each exposure
k\in\{0,1\}
, we define the random variable

T_{k}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{1}[E_{(i,k)}]\kern 5.0pt.

T_{k}
is exactly the number of units assigned to their desired exposure
e_{k}
under the Conflict Graph Design.
These are also the number of units that will be used by the modified Horvitz-Thompson estimator.
In this section, we will prove the following theorem, which shows that
T_{k}
concentrates around its expectation
\Esym[T_{k}]
, which is of order
n/\lambda(\mathcal{H})
.

Proposition A.4
.

There exists constants
c
,
C
, and
K
such that the following holds:
Under the Conflict Graph Design, for either exposure
k\in\{0,1\}
and for any
\alpha\in(0,1)
, with probability at least
1-\alpha
it holds that

c\cdot\frac{n}{\lambda(\mathcal{H})}-\sqrt{\frac{1}{\alpha}}\cdot\sqrt{K\cdot\frac{n}{\lambda(\mathcal{H})}}\leq T_{k}\leq C\cdot\frac{n}{\lambda(\mathcal{H})}+\sqrt{\frac{1}{\alpha}}\cdot\sqrt{K\cdot\frac{n}{\lambda(\mathcal{H})}}\kern 5.0pt.

Proposition
A.4
shows that in the consistency regime where
\lambda(\mathcal{H})=o\lparen n\rparen
, we have that
T
concentrates around its mean with probability tending to 1.
In the remainder of this section, we discuss how to prove this result.
Without loss of generality, we focus on
k=1
and drop the corresponding subscript when it is clear from context.

To prove Proposition
A.4
, we will analyze the mean and variance of
T
and then apply Chebyshev’s inequality.
To this end, it will be helpful to recognize that
T
is equivalent to the modified Horvitz–Thompson estimator for a specific choice of potential outcomes.
Consider the potential outcomes
y_{i}(e_{1})=\Pr\lparen E_{(i,1)}\rparen
and
y_{i}(e_{0})=0
.
For this choice of potential outcomes, the Horvitz–Thompson estimator is related to the number of treated units as
T=n\cdot\hat{\tau}
, i.e.

\hat{\tau}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Bigg\{y_{i}(e_{1})\cdot\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(i,1)}]}{\Pr\lparen\mathbf{1}[E_{(i,1)}]\rparen}-y_{i}(e_{0})\cdot\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(i,0)}]}{\Pr\lparen\mathbf{1}[E_{(i,0)}]\rparen}\Bigg\}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{1}[E_{(i,1)}]=\frac{1}{n}\cdot T\kern 5.0pt.

One can use this relationship
T=n\cdot\hat{\tau}
and existing analysis for
\hat{\tau}
in the main body to prove high probability bounds on
T
.

We start by computing the expectation of
T
.
This is straighforward using the lower bounds for the probabilities of exposure as well as linearity of expectation.

Lemma A.5
.

Under the Conflict Graph Design, there exists constants
c
and
C
such that

c\cdot\frac{n}{\lambda(\mathcal{H})}\leq\Esym[T]\leq C\cdot\frac{n}{\lambda(\mathcal{H})}\kern 5.0pt.

Proof.

The proof follows directly from the linearity of expectation and the upper and lower bounds on the probabilities of exposure from Lemma
4.2
.
∎

Next, we upper bound the variance of
T
.
Because
T
is essentially an instance of the Horvitz–Thompson estimator, we can directly use the result of Theorem
4.1
to obtain an upper bound on the variance:

Lemma A.6
.

There exists a constant
K
such that
under the Conflict Graph Design

\Varsym\lparen T\rparen\leq K\cdot\frac{n}{\lambda(\mathcal{H})}\kern 5.0pt.

Thus, combining Lemmas
A.5
and
A.6
, we can show that
T_{k}
concentrates around its expectation using Chebyshev’s inequality.
We give the proof of the main result below.

Proof of Theorem
A.4
.

Using Chebyshev’s inequality and Lemma
A.6
, we have that for any
\alpha\in(0,1)

\displaystyle\Pr\Bigg\lparen\lvert T_{k}-\Esym[T_{k}]\rvert\geq\sqrt{\frac{K(r)n}{\alpha\lambda(\mathcal{H})}}\Bigg\rparen

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\Varsym\lparen T_{k}\rparen}{\frac{K(r)n}{\alpha\lambda(\mathcal{H})}}\leq\alpha\kern 5.0pt.

The result then follows by combining this with the bounds on
\Esym[T_{k}]
from Lemma
A.5
.
∎

A.4
Comparison to Dependency Graph Analysis

In this section, we elaborate on the dependency graph method for analyzing the rates of consistency in network experiments.
Variants of this method have been widely used (either explicitly or implicitly) throughout the literature on interference
(see e.g.
5
;
51
;
30
;
66
)
.
We show that the dependency graph analysis is loose when applied to the Conflict Graph Design, incurring an additional
d_{\max{}}(\mathcal{H}^{2})
factor.

We begin by introducing a commonly used notion of dependency graph.

Definition 4
.

Given a set of random variables
X_{1}\cdots X_{n}
, a graph
\mathcal{D}=(V,E)
with vertices
V=\{1\dots n\}
is a
dependency graph
if the absence of an edge between
i
and
j
implies that random variables
X_{i}
and
X_{j}
are independent.

We denote the degree of a random variable
X_{i}
in the dependency graph as
d_{\mathcal{D}}(i)
.
The maximum degree
d_{\max{}}(\mathcal{D})=\max_{i\in[n]}d_{\mathcal{D}}(i)
of the dependency graph is a measure of the independence of the set of random variables
X_{1}\dots X_{n}
.
Definition
4
describes the simplest form of a dependency graph using only pair-wise independence, which suffices for the purpose of analyzing the variance.
More nuanced forms of dependency graphs involving higher order independence are used in central limit theorems (see e.g. the definition of dependency graph used in the proof of the CLT in Section
E.6
).

We seek to bound the variance of the modified Horvitz–Thompson estimator.
Recall that this estimator can be decomposed into the average of individual estimators, i.e.

\hat{\tau}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}Y_{i}\cdot\Bigg\lparen\frac{\mathbf{1}\big[E_{(i,1)}\big]}{\Pr\big\lparen E_{(i,1)}\big\rparen}-\frac{\mathbf{1}\big[E_{(i,0)}\big]}{\Pr\big\lparen E_{(i,0)}\big\rparen}\Bigg\rparen=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\hat{\tau}_{i}\kern 5.0pt,

where the desired exposure events are defined as

E_{(i,k)}=\Big\{U_{i}=e_{k}\text{ and }U_{j}=*\text{ for all }j\in\mathcal{N}_{b}^{\pi}(i)\Big\}\kern 5.0pt.

We will consider a dependency graph
\mathcal{D}
on the individual estimators
\hat{\tau}_{1}\dots\hat{\tau}_{n}
.
Note that the dependency graph
\mathcal{D}
encodes the independence of the individual estimators, whereas the conflict graph
\mathcal{H}
encodes only fundamental unobservability of pairs of potential outcomes.
This two graphs are distinct and, in fact, will typically not be equal in network experiments.
We return to this point more later in the section.

We will also define
\gamma
to be the inverse of the smallest relevant exposure probability, i.e.

\gamma=\max_{i\in[n],k\in\{0,1\}}\frac{1}{\Pr\lparen E_{(i,k)}\rparen}\kern 5.0pt.

This quantity is relevant for bounding the variance of an individual effect estimator, as shown in the following lemma.
We remark that while we focus on the Conflict Graph Design, the same principle may be used for an arbitrary design.

Lemma A.7
.

Suppose the arbitrary neighborhood interference model holds (Assumption
1
).
Under the Conflict Graph Design, the variance of each individual effect estimator may be bounded as

\Varsym\lparen\hat{\tau}_{i}\rparen\leq\gamma\cdot\Big\lparen y_{i}(e_{1})^{2}+y_{i}(e_{0})^{2}\Big\rparen\kern 5.0pt.

Proof.

Recall that the individual estimator
\hat{\tau}_{i}
is unbiased for the individual effect
\tau_{i}
.
Under the arbitrary neighborhood interference assumption,
the variance may be calculated as

\displaystyle\Varsym\lparen\hat{\tau}_{i}\rparen

\displaystyle=\Esym\big[\big\lparen\hat{\tau}_{i}-\tau_{i}\big\rparen^{2}\big]

\displaystyle=\Esym\Big[\Big\lparen y_{i}(e_{1})\cdot\Big\{\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(i,1)}]}{\Pr\lparen E_{(i,1)}\rparen}-1\Big\}-y_{i}(e_{0})\cdot\Big\{\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(i,0)}]}{\Pr\lparen E_{(i,0)}\rparen}-1\Big\}\Big\rparen^{2}\Big]

\displaystyle=y_{i}(e_{1})^{2}\cdot\Esym\Big[\Big\{\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(i,1)}]}{\Pr\lparen E_{(i,1)}\rparen}-1\Big\}^{2}\Big]+y_{i}(e_{0})^{2}\cdot\Esym\Big[\Big\{\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(i,0)}]}{\Pr\lparen E_{(i,0)}\rparen}-1\Big\}^{2}\Big]

\displaystyle\quad\quad+2y_{i}(e_{1})y_{i}(e_{0})

Calculating these expectations, we have that

\displaystyle=y_{i}(e_{1})^{2}\cdot\Big\lparen\frac{1}{\Pr\lparen E_{(i,1)}\rparen}-1\Big\rparen+y_{i}(e_{0})^{2}\cdot\Big\lparen\frac{1}{\Pr\lparen E_{(i,0)}\rparen}-1\Big\rparen+2y_{i}(e_{1})y_{i}(e_{0})

Finally, an application of the AM-GM inequality to the third term yields

\displaystyle\leq y_{i}(e_{1})^{2}\cdot\frac{1}{\Pr\lparen E_{(i,1)}\rparen}+y_{i}(e_{0})^{2}\cdot\frac{1}{\Pr\lparen E_{(i,0)}\rparen}

\displaystyle\leq\gamma\cdot\big\lparen y_{i}(e_{1})^{2}+y_{i}(e_{0})^{2}\big\rparen\kern 5.0pt,

where the final inequality used the definition of
\gamma
.
∎

The following proposition is the general form of the dependency graph analysis.
Although we focus on the Conflict Graph Design, the idea can be more generally applied to an arbitrary design
(
5
;
32
)
.
The key idea is that Cauchy-Schwarz and AM-GM inequalities are applied to covariance terms which are not guaranteed to be zero by the dependency graph, and Lemma
A.7
to bound the individual variances.

Proposition A.8

(Dependency Graph Analysis)
.

Suppose the arbitrary neighborhood interference model holds (Assumption
1
).
Under the Conflict Graph Design, the variance of the modified Horvitz–Thompson estimator is bounded as

\Varsym\lparen\hat{\tau}\rparen\leq\frac{d_{\max{}}(\mathcal{D})}{n}\cdot\gamma\cdot\Big\{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}y_{i}(e_{1})^{2}+y_{i}(e_{0})^{2}\Big\}\kern 5.0pt.

Proof.

We begin by decomposing the variance of the sum into the sum of the relevant covariance terms.
We use the notation
i\sim_{\mathcal{D}}j
to denote that units
i
and
j
are adjacent in the dependency graph
\mathcal{D}
.

\displaystyle\Varsym\lparen\hat{\tau}\rparen

\displaystyle=\Varsym\big\lparen\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\hat{\tau}_{i}\big\rparen

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\Covsym\lparen\hat{\tau}_{i},\hat{\tau}_{j}\rparen

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mathbf{1}[i\sim_{\mathcal{D}}j]\cdot\Covsym\lparen\hat{\tau}_{i},\hat{\tau}_{j}\rparen\kern 5.0pt,

where the last line follows by definition of the edges in the dependency graph.
In particular, we have that
\mathbf{1}[i\sim_{\mathcal{D}}j]\cdot\Covsym\lparen\hat{\tau}_{i},\hat{\tau}_{j}\rparen=\Covsym\lparen\hat{\tau}_{i},\hat{\tau}_{j}\rparen
because the absence of an edge means that the variables are independent and thus the covariance is zero.
Using the Cauchy-Schwarz and AM-GM inequalities, we have that

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mathbf{1}[i\sim_{\mathcal{D}}j]\cdot\sqrt{\Varsym\lparen\hat{\tau}_{i}\rparen\cdot\Varsym\lparen\hat{\tau}_{j}\rparen}

(Cauchy-Schwarz)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mathbf{1}[i\sim_{\mathcal{D}}j]\cdot\frac{1}{2}\Big\lparen\Varsym\lparen\hat{\tau}_{i}\rparen+\Varsym\lparen\hat{\tau}_{j}\rparen\Big\rparen

(AM-GM)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}d_{\mathcal{D}}(i)\cdot\Varsym\lparen\hat{\tau}_{i}\rparen\kern 5.0pt,

where the last equality follows by collecting terms and using the definition of the degrees in the dependency graph.
Now, we bound each of the degrees by the maximum degree and apply the result of Lemma
A.7
to bound the individual variances to obtain

\displaystyle\leq\frac{d_{\max{}}(\mathcal{D})}{n}\cdot\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Varsym\lparen\hat{\tau}_{i}\rparen

(def of
d_{\max{}}(\mathcal{D})
)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{d_{\max{}}(\mathcal{D})}{n}\cdot\gamma\cdot\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}y_{i}(e_{1})^{2}+y_{i}(e_{0})^{2}\kern 5.0pt,

(Lemma
A.7
)

which completes the proof.
∎

Proposition
A.8
contains the general dependency graph analysis.
In order to compare this to the operator norm analysis, we need to determine the values of the inverse probabilities
\gamma
and the maximum degree of the dependency graph
d_{\max{}}(\mathcal{D})
.
This is done in the following corollary.

Corollary A.9

(CGD Dependency Graph Analysis)
.

Suppose the arbitrary neighborhood interference model holds (Assumption
1
).
There exists a constant
C>0
such that the variance of the modified Horvitz–Thompson estimator under the Conflict Graph Design is bounded as

\Varsym\lparen\hat{\tau}\rparen\leq C\cdot\frac{\lambda(\mathcal{H})}{n}\cdot d_{\max{}}(\mathcal{H}^{2})\cdot\Big\{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}y_{i}(e_{1})^{2}+y_{i}(e_{0})^{2}\Big\}\kern 5.0pt,

where
\mathcal{H}^{2}
is the graph obtained by connecting all nodes which are at most distance two away in the conflict graph
\mathcal{H}
and
d_{\max{}}(\mathcal{H}^{2})
is its maximum degree.

Proof.

First, we use Lemma
4.2
to bound the inverse probabilities by
\gamma\leq C\cdot\lambda(\mathcal{H})
.
Next, we recognize that two individual effect estimators are independent under the Conflict Graph Design if and only if they are greater than two hops away in the conflict graph
\mathcal{H}
.
Thus,
\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{H}^{2}
.
∎

Corollary
A.9
contains the complete dependency graph analysis applied to the Conflict Graph Design.
The key difference between the dependency graph analysis and the operator norm analysis is that the former incurs an additional
d_{\max{}}(\mathcal{H}^{2})
factor.
This factor
d_{\max{}}(\mathcal{H}^{2})
will typically be orders of magnitude larger than
\lambda(\mathcal{H})
.

For example, consider the estimation of the Global Average Treatment Effect (GATE) where the underlying graph is denoted as
G
.
Then, the additional factor
d_{\max{}}(\mathcal{H}^{2})
is at most
d_{\max{}}(G)^{4}
, and this bound will be tight for most regular graphs.
This is exactly the extra term appearing in the analysis of
66
, because they employ the dependency graph method.
In general, the dependency graph method will incur polynomial dependencies on the maximum degree in the graph
G
.
This is because the dependency graph cannot distinguish small correlations from large ones.
In contrast, using the operator norm analysis, we obtain refined bounds on the correlations depending on the distance between nodes in the graph, which yields an overall better analysis of the rates.

Illustrative Example

Consider the direct treatment effect
\tau=(1/n)\sum_{i=1}^{n}y_{i}(e_{1})-y_{i}(e_{0})
, where
e_{1}
is the exposure that you are treated and your neighbors receive control and
e_{0}
is the exposure that you and all your neighbors receive control.
Suppose that the network
G
is the star graph and that treatment is assigned according the Conflict Graph Design.

(a)

Original Network
G

(b)

Conflict Graph
\mathcal{H}

(c)

Dependency Graph
\mathcal{D}

Figure 5
:

Figure
5(a)
depicts a star graph as the original underlying network
G
.
Figure
5(b)
depicts the conflict graph
\mathcal{H}
for the direct effect, which in this case is the original graph
G
.
Figure
5
depicts the dependency graph
\mathcal{D}
which is the complete graph, as the treatment assignment of each node is correlated under the CGD.

Figure
5
shows the underlying graph
G
, the conflict graph
\mathcal{H}
, and the dependency graph
\mathcal{D}
for this example.
The conflict graph
\mathcal{H}
is simply the original graph
G
because each leaf is in conflict with the central node, but pairs of leaf nodes are not themselves in conflict, i.e. any pair of estimand-relevant exposures can be simultaneously observed for leaf nodes.
On the other hand, the dependency graph
\mathcal{D}
is fully connected: the central node’s exposure is of course correlated with each leaf’s exposure, but in fact each pair of leaves has correlated exposures through the central node, their common neighbor.
For this reason, the dependency graph analysis yields a much more pessimistic analysis of the variance:

\underbrace{\Varsym\lparen\hat{\tau}\rparen\leq\frac{d_{\max}(\mathcal{D})}{n}\cdot\gamma}_{\textrm{Dependency Graph Analysis}}=\underbrace{\mathcal{O}\lparen\sqrt{n}\rparen}_{\textrm{Star Graph}}\quad\text{while}\quad\underbrace{\Varsym\lparen\hat{\tau}\rparen\leq\frac{\lambda(\mathcal{H})}{n}}_{\textrm{Operator Norm Analysis}}=\underbrace{\mathcal{O}\Big\lparen\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\Big\rparen}_{\textrm{Star Graph}}

In each of the steps above, the first inequality is due to the proof technique and the equality is simply a calculation for this illustrative example.
Note that the dependency graph analysis cannot show that the estimator is consistent, whereas the operator norm analysis establishes consistency at a
n^{-1/4}
rate.

A.5
Comparison with
66

In the simulations presented in Section
B
, we observed that the Randomized Graph Cluster Randomization designs (RGCR) of
66
achieved lower variance than the Conflict Graph Design.
This prompts the question of whether the particular selection of graphs for these simulations favors the RGCR designs, or whether these designs also achieve
\lambda(\mathcal{H})/n
rates in general.
We resolve this question in this section, by showing that the RGCR design does not achieve the
\lambda(\mathcal{H})/n
rate in general.
In particular, we construct a family of graphs
\{G_{n}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
where the 1-hop-max RGCR design with Horvitz-Thompson estimator performs
exponentially worse
than the Conflict Graph Design with the modified Horvitz-Thompson estimator.

Recall that the GATE is defined as

\tau=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Big\lparen y_{i}(\boldsymbol{1})-y_{i}(\boldsymbol{0})\Big\rparen

We first state the main result that we will prove in this section. We use
\Esym_{#1}[\cdot],\Esym_{#1}[\cdot]
to denote expectation with respect to the Conflict Graph Design and the 1-hop-max design, respectively.
We will denote by
\hat{\tau}_{\operatorname{1-hm}}
the estimator used in
66
together with 1-hop-max design.
This is given by the standard Horvitz-Thompson estimator for the GATE and takes the form

\hat{\tau}_{\operatorname{1-hm}}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}Y_{i}\cdot\Big\lparen\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(i,1)}]}{\Pr\lparen E_{(i,1)}\rparen}-\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(i,0)}]}{\Pr\lparen E_{(i,0)}\rparen}\Big\rparen\kern 5.0pt,

where the events
E_{(i,1)}
and
E_{(i,0)}
are simply the events that a node receives the all treatment or the all control exposure, i.e.

\displaystyle E_{(i,1)}=\{z\in\{0,1\}^{n}:z_{i}=1,z_{j}=1\forall j\in N(i)\}

(15)

\displaystyle E_{(i,0)}=\{z\in\{0,1\}^{n}:z_{i}=0,z_{j}=0\forall j\in N(i)\}

We note that the same notation has been used in Section
3.4
to define the desired exposure events used in the modified Horvitz-Thompson estimator used with the Conflict Graph Design.
We emphasize that for the purposes of this section, the notation
E_{(i,1)},E_{(i,0)}
will always be defined as in (
15
).

We also note that
\hat{\tau}_{\operatorname{CGD}}
will denote the modified Horvitz-Thompson estimator that is used together with the Conflict Graph Design, as defined in Section
3.4
.
As in Theorem
4.3
, we will assume that the second moments of the potential outcomes are bounded.

Theorem A.10
.

Let
\tau
be the Global Average Treatment Effect. There is a sequence
G_{n}
of graphs with maximum degree
d_{n}=\Theta\lparen n^{1/6}\rparen
, with a triangular sequence
y_{i}^{(n)}
of potential outcome functions satisfying Assumptions
1
and
2
, such that the following hold

\displaystyle\Esym_{#1}[\big\lparen\tau-\hat{\tau}_{\operatorname{CGD}}\big\rparen^{2}]^{1/2}=\mathcal{O}\lparen n^{-1/6}\rparen

(16)

while

\displaystyle\Esym_{#1}[\big\lparen\tau-\hat{\tau}_{\operatorname{1-hm}}\big\rparen^{2}]^{1/2}=\Omega\Big\lparen\frac{1}{\log^{2}n}\Big\rparen\kern 5.0pt.

(17)

This implies that the rate achieved by the 1-hop-max design is exponentially worse than that achieved by the Conflict Graph Design in this family of graphs.
This shows that the 1-hop-max design does not achieve comparable performance to the Conflict Graph Design for all graphs. In particular, the simulations in Section
B
involve graphs that do not reflect the worst-case performance of the 1-hop-max design.

For completeness, we have also experimentally verified this difference in performance between these two designs. In Figure
6
, we compare the variance of estimating the GATE using the 1-hop-max design and the Conflict Graph Design on graphs from the family
G_{n}
that is defined in the proof of Theorem
A.10
.
We observe that the Conflict Graph Design converges at a polynomial rate, while the convergence of the 1-hop-max design is barely noticeable even for graphs with millions of nodes. This is consistent with the predictions of Theorem
A.10
.

Figure 6
:
Comparison of the variance achieved by the 1-hop-max and the conflict graph designs, as a functions of the number of nodes
n
.

In the rest of the section, we will establish a variety of useful lemmas and will present the proof of Theorem
A.10
in the end.
We start by describing the 1-hop-max design from
66
.
We will use the notation
B_{l}(i)
from
66
to denote the nodes that are at distance at most
l
from
i
, including
i
itself. Note that
B_{1}(i)=N(i)\cup\{i\}=\tilde{N}(i)
.

For a given graph
G
, the 1-hop-max design starts by computing the weights for each node in the graph, which are the entries of the leading eigenvector
w\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
of the adjacency matrix of
G^{2}
.
The algorithm then works in two phases. In the first phase, it samples a random variable
X_{i}\sim\beta(w_{i},1)
independently for each node
i
.
In the second phase, it assigns each node
i
a cluster label
c_{i}
, which is the identity of the node in
B_{1}(i)
with the largest
X_{j}
value (breaking ties arbitrarily).
After all nodes have been assigned a cluster label, the algorithm assigns treatment or control to each cluster independently with probability
1/2
. We provide the pseudocode of the 1-hop-max design in Algorithm
3
.

Input:

Graph
G=(V,E)
, weights
\boldsymbol{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
(leading eigenvector of adjacency matrix of
G^{2}
)

Output:

Random intervention
Z\in\mathcal{Z}=\{0,1\}^{n}

for

i\in V

do

1

Sample
X_{i}\sim\beta(w_{i},1)
independently

for

i\in V

do

2

c_{i}\leftarrow\argmax_{j\in B_{1}(i)}X_{j}

//
break ties arbitrarily

for

each distinct cluster label
k
in
\{c_{i}:i\in V\}

do

3

Sample
Y_{k}\sim\operatorname{Bernoulli}(1/2)
independently

for

each
j
with
c_{j}=k

do

4

Z_{j}\leftarrow Y_{k}

Algorithm 3

Weighted 1-hop-max Clustering

The key difficulty when analyzing the performance of the 1-hop-max design comes from the fact that the clusters are random and do not necessarily contain the entire neighborhood of a node. It is even possible that a node
j
is assigned cluster
c_{j}=i
, but the node
i
is assigned to a different cluster.
To make these calculations easier, it would help if there is some kind of uniformity in the graph.
We now describe the class of graphs we will be considering.

We define the following family
G_{n}
of graphs, indexed by the number of nodes
n
. For each
n
, there is an associated parameter
d_{n}
that is used to define the graph.
The graph is depicted in Figure
7
and we provide a formal description below:

The vertex set
V
is partitioned as

V=\{l,r\}\cup U\cup\big\lparen\cup_{i\in U}(L_{i}\cup R_{i})\big\rparen\cup C\kern 5.0pt,

where
|U|=d_{n},|L_{i}|=|R_{i}|=d_{n}/2,|C|=d_{n}^{3}
.
We denote
L=\cup_{i\in U}L_{i}
and
R=\cup_{i\in U}R_{i}
. From the above it follows that
|L|=|R|=d_{n}^{2}/2
.
The graph has the following edges.

1.

There is an edge between
l
(respectively
r
) and every node in
L
(respectively
R
) .

2.

Every node
i\in U
is connected to all nodes in
L_{i}\cup R_{i}
.

3.

Every node
i\in L\cup R
is connected to
d
distinct nodes in
C
. Call
C_{i}
the neighbors of
i
in
C
.

Thus, the graph
G_{n}
has total number of vertices
n=2+d_{n}+d_{n}^{2}+d_{n}^{3}=d_{n}^{3}+o\lparen d_{n}^{3}\rparen
, thus we have
d_{n}=n^{1/3}+o\lparen n^{1/3}\rparen
.

Intuitively, the structure of
G_{n}
is similar to a three-level tree, but where the root is also connected to the second level.
The reason for this is that the nodes in
L\cup R
will end up in the cluster of
l
or
r
with sufficiently high probability. In turn, this will correlate the exposure events for the nodes in
U
.

\displaystyle U
\displaystyle R_{d}
\displaystyle\Bigr\}d^{2}
nodes
\displaystyle\Bigr\}d
nodes
\displaystyle L_{1}
\displaystyle\Bigr\}d^{3}
nodes
\displaystyle C
\displaystyle R_{1}
\displaystyle L_{d}
\displaystyle L=L_{1}\cup\cdots\cup L_{d}
\displaystyle R=R_{1}\cup\cdots\cup R_{d}
\displaystyle l
\displaystyle r

Figure 7
:
The graph
G^{(n)}
used in the proof of Theorem
A.10
. It contains a subset of nodes
U
, that each have
d
neighbors, where half of the neighbors are also connected to a hub node
l
and the other half to a hub node
r
. Each of the neighbors of nodes in
U
is also connected to
d
distinct nodes in
C
. The proof will proceed by showing that the exposures of nodes in
U
are highly correlated under the 1-hop-max design, leading to high variance for the Horvitz-Thompson estimator.

We start by listing some important properties about the spectrum of this graph. In particular, we are interested in the maximum eigenvalue of the adjacency matrix, as well as in the relative magnitudes of the entries in the leading eigenvector, since these are used in the 1-hop-max design. For simplicity,
we will omit the dependence on
n
in the notation of the graph and the degree in the remainder of this section.

Lemma A.11
.

Let
G
be the graph in the family defined earlier with
n
nodes, where
d=n^{1/3}(1+o(1))
. Then, the following properties hold:

1.

The maximum eigenvalue of the adjacency matrix of
G^{2}
is
\lambda(G^{2})=d^{2}/2+d/2+O(1)
.

2.

If
w\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
is the leading eigenvector of the adjacency matrix of
G^{2}
with unit norm, then we have for all
i
,

w_{i}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\cdot\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\Theta(1)&\text{ if }i\in L\cup R\cup\{l,r\}\\
\Theta(1/d)&\text{ if }i\in U\\
\Theta(1/d^{2})&\text{ if }i\in C\end{array}\right.

Proof.

Let us denote by
\lambda
the maximum eigenvalue of the adjacency matrix of
G^{2}
for simplicity.
First of all, note that due to symmetry, all nodes in
L\cup R\cup\{l,r\}
have the same entry in the leading eigenvector, which we denote by
x_{L}
. Similarly, all nodes in
U
have the same entry
x_{U}
and all nodes in
C
have the same entry
x_{C}
.
Also,
l,r
have the same entry
x_{l}
.
In
G^{2}
, nodes
l,r
are connected to all nodes in
U
, as well as
d^{3}/2
nodes in
C
each. A node in
U
is connected to
l,r
, all nodes in
L_{i}\cup R_{i}
and
d^{2}
nodes in
C
. A node in
L_{i}
(respectively
R_{i}
) is connected to
l
(respectively
r
), one node in
U
, all other nodes in
L
(respectively
R
),
d/2
nodes in
R
and
d
nodes in
C
. Finally, a node in
C
is connected to either
l
or
r
, one node in
U
, one node in
L
or
R
and
d-1
other “sibling” nodes in
C
.
We can therefore write the conditions for the leading eigenvector as the following system of equations:

\displaystyle\lambda x_{l}

\displaystyle=\frac{d^{2}}{2}x_{L}+dx_{U}+\frac{d^{3}}{2}x_{C}

(18)

\displaystyle\lambda x_{L}

\displaystyle=\big\lparen\frac{d^{2}}{2}+\frac{d}{2}-1\big\rparen x_{L}+x_{l}+x_{U}+dx_{C}

\displaystyle\lambda x_{U}

\displaystyle=2x_{l}+dx_{L}+d^{2}x_{C}

\displaystyle\lambda x_{C}

\displaystyle=x_{L}+x_{U}+x_{l}+(d-1)x_{C}

By solving this system, we arrive at the following polynomial equation for
\lambda
:

\displaystyle 4\lambda^{4}-(2d^{2}+6d-8)\lambda^{3}-(6d^{2}+22d-4)\lambda^{2}+(d^{5}+3d^{4}-6d^{3}-6d^{2}-20d)\lambda

\displaystyle+(4d^{5}-16d^{3}+12d^{2}-8d)=0

We can determine the asymptotic behavior of the roots of this polynomial by using the method of dominant balance (see e.g. Chapter 7 in
11
). In particular, suppose
\lambda=\alpha d^{\gamma}+o\lparen d^{\gamma}\rparen
for some
\alpha,\gamma>0
. Plugging this into the polynomial and keeping only the dominant terms, we get

4\alpha^{4}d^{4\gamma}-2\alpha^{3}d^{2+3\gamma}-6\alpha^{2}d^{2+2\gamma}+\alpha d^{5+\gamma}+4d^{5}=0

To have a balance, we need at least two terms to have the same exponent, otherwise one term will dominate the others for large
d
. Note that if
\gamma>2
, the first term
d^{4\gamma}
is strictly dominating all the other terms, thus we must have
\gamma\leq 2
. Let’s try
\gamma=2
, since we care about the largest root.
Then, the dominant terms are the first and second terms
4\alpha^{4}d^{8}
and
-2\alpha^{3}d^{8}
. Balancing these two terms gives
\alpha=1/2
. Thus, we have found one root with asymptotic behavior
\lambda=d^{2}/2+o\lparen d^{2}\rparen
. To compute the second order term, we can assert that
\lambda=d^{2}/2+\beta d^{\delta}+o\lparen d^{\delta}\rparen
for some
\beta,\delta
with
\delta<2
. Plugging this back into the polynomial and keeping only the dominant terms, we get

2\beta d^{6+\delta}-\frac{3}{2}\beta d^{6+\delta}-\frac{3}{4}d^{7}-\frac{3}{2}d^{6}+\frac{1}{2}d^{7}+4d^{5}=0

The only way to have a balance of the leading order terms is to have
\delta=1
. Balancing the terms of order
d^{7}
gives

2\beta-\frac{3}{2}\beta-\frac{3}{4}+\frac{1}{2}=0\kern 5.0pt,

which implies
\beta=1/2
. Thus, we have established that
\lambda=d^{2}/2+d/2+o\lparen d\rparen
.

Finally, to establish the scaling of the entries of the leading eigenvector, we can plug in the value of
\lambda
back into the system of equations (
18
) and solve for the ratios between
x_{l},x_{L},x_{U},x_{C}
. Specifically, we can start from the fourth equation in (
18
) to express
x_{C}
in terms of
x_{L},x_{U},x_{l}
. By the asymptotics on
\lambda
that we just established, we get

x_{C}=\frac{2}{d^{2}}(1+o(1))\big\lparen x_{L}+x_{U}+x_{l}\big\rparen\kern 5.0pt.

Now we plug this value to the third equation, which gives

x_{U}=\frac{8}{d^{2}}(1+o(1))x_{l}+\frac{2}{d}(1+o(1))x_{L}\kern 5.0pt.

Thus, the
x_{U}
term is orders of magnitude smaller than both
x_{l},x_{L}
. Finally, plugging these estimates in the first equation yields

\frac{d^{2}}{2}(1+o(1))x_{l}=\frac{d^{2}}{2}x_{L}+\frac{8}{d}(1+o(1))x_{l}+2(1+o(1))x_{L}+2d(1+o(1))(x_{l}+x_{L})

which implies that
x_{L}=(1+o(1))x_{l}
. Thus, we get

x_{U}=\frac{2}{d}(1+o(1))x_{l}\quad\text{ and }\quad x_{C}=\frac{4}{d^{2}}(1+o(1))x_{l}\kern 5.0pt.

Normalizing for unit norm gives the desired result.
∎

We are now ready to analyze the performance of the 1-hop-max design on this family of graphs.
To analyze the variance of the estimator
\hat{\tau}_{\operatorname{1-hm}}
,
we will first require some technical lemmas about Beta and exponential distributions.
The first is about the distribution of the minimum of a subset of exponential random variables.

Lemma A.12
.

Let
T_{1},\ldots,T_{n}
be independent random variables where
T_{i}\sim\operatorname{Exp}(w_{i})
, where
\operatorname{Exp}(\lambda)
is the exponential distribution with parameter
\lambda
and density function
x\mapsto\lambda e^{-\lambda x}
for
x>0
. Then,

\Pr\lparen T_{1}<\min_{i>1}T_{i}\rparen=\frac{w_{1}}{\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}}

Proof.

We use the well-known fact that a random variable
T
follows
\operatorname{Exp}(w)
distribution if and only if
X=e^{-T}
follows
\beta(w,1)
distribution. Thus, under the transformation
X_{i}=e^{-T_{i}}
, the probability can be rewritten as

\Pr\lparen X_{1}>\max_{i>1}X_{i}\rparen

and we can use Theorem 4.10 from
66
to conclude the proof.
