∎

The second technical lemma is a generalization about the rank statistics of independent random variables that follow the exponential distribution.

Lemma A.13
.

Let
T_{1},\ldots,T_{n}
be independent random variables where
T_{i}\sim\operatorname{Exp}(w_{i})
, where
\operatorname{Exp}(\lambda)
is the exponential distribution with parameter
\lambda
and density function
x\mapsto\lambda e^{-\lambda x}
for
x>0
.
Furthermore, suppose that
w_{1}\leq\rho\cdot\min_{i>1}w_{i}
, for some
\rho>0
.
Let

T_{(1)},\ldots,T_{(n)}

be the variables
T_{1},\ldots,T_{n}
ordered from smallest to largest, with
T_{(1)}
being the smallest and
T_{(n)}
the largest.
Then, for any
r\in[n]
,

\Pr\lparen T_{1}\leq T_{(r)}\rparen\leq\rho\cdot\frac{r}{n-r+1}\kern 5.0pt.

Proof.

We use the memoryless property of the exponential distribution, which asserts that for a random variable
Y
that follows an exponential distribution and any
s,t>0
, we have

\Pr\lparen Y>s+t|Y>s\rparen=\Pr\lparen Y>t\rparen\kern 5.0pt.

Imagine we sample each
T_{i}
by using an exponential “clock”, where the clock fires once at a random time
T_{i}
and
\Pr\lparen T_{i}\leq t\rparen=1-e^{-w_{i}t}
is given as in the exponential distribution.
Then, we can imagive we have
n
independent clocks, one associated to each random variable
T_{i}
.
We will say
T_{i}
fires if its associated clock fires.
The time is partitioned in rounds. The first round is the time until the earliest clock fires, the second round is the time between the earliest and second earliest clocks etc. Let
A_{t}
be the event that the clock assigned to
T_{1}
fires in one of the first
t
rounds, which equivalently says that
1
appears in one of the first
t
positions in the ordering. Then, the statement of the lemma is about upper bounding
\Pr\lparen A_{r}\rparen
.
By the law of total probability, we can write

\Pr\lparen A_{r}\rparen=\Pr\lparen A_{1}\rparen+\Pr\lparen A_{2}|A_{1}^{c}\rparen+\ldots\Pr\lparen A_{r}|A_{r-1}^{c}\rparen\kern 5.0pt.

Let us bound
\Pr\lparen A_{j}|A_{j-1}^{c}\rparen
for each
j
.
This is the event that
T_{1}
fires in the
j
-th round. To analyze that, let us first condition on the fact that the remaining clocks after the
j-1
-th round form the subset
L_{j}
and
1\in L_{j}
. Conditioned on that event, by the memoryless property of the exponential distribution, we know that the law of the variables in
L_{j}
after the start of the
j
-th round is still the same law of an exponential distribution. Thus, by Lemma
A.12
, the probability that
1
is the clock that fires in the
j
-th round, conditioned on the subset
L_{j}
, is

\frac{w_{1}}{\sum_{i\in L_{j}}w_{i}}\leq\rho\cdot\frac{1}{|L_{j}|}=\rho\cdot\frac{1}{n-j+1}

Since this holds for all possible conditionings of the subset
L_{j}
, we conclude that

\Pr\lparen A_{j}|A_{j-1}^{c}\rparen\leq\rho\cdot\frac{1}{n-j+1}

Therefore,

\Pr\lparen A_{r}\rparen\leq\rho\cdot\sum_{j=1}^{r}\frac{1}{n-j+1}\leq\frac{\rho\cdot r}{n-r+1}

∎

The third lemma involves Beta distributions and will be useful when we calculate joint probabilities of exposure.

Lemma A.14
.

Suppose we have an index set
V=\{i,j\}\cup A\cup B\cup C
, where
A,B,C
are pairwise disjoint. For every
i\in V
, we sample independently
X_{i}\sim\beta(w_{i},1)
, where
w_{i}>0
is some positive weight. For a subset
R\subseteq V
, denote
w_{R}=\sum_{k\in R}w_{k}
. Then

\Pr\lparen X_{i}>\max_{k\in A\cup B}X_{k}\textrm{ and }X_{j}>\max_{k\in B\cup C}X_{k}\rparen=\frac{w_{i}w_{j}}{w_{V}}\Big\lparen\frac{1}{w_{i}+w_{A}+w_{B}}+\frac{1}{w_{j}+w_{B}+w_{C}}\Big\rparen

Proof.

If
R
is an arbitrary subset, a well-known property of Beta distributions (see Theorem 4.10 in
66
) is that
\max_{k\in R}X_{k}\sim\beta(w_{R},1)
.
Thus, we can rewrite the target probability as

\Pr\lparen X_{i}>\max(Y_{A},Y_{B})\textrm{ and }X_{j}>\max(Y_{B},Y_{C})\rparen\\
=\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{1}w_{i}w_{j}x_{i}^{w_{i}-1}x_{j}^{w_{j}-1}\Pr\lparen Y_{A}<x_{i}\rparen\Pr\lparen Y_{C}<x_{j}\rparen\Pr\lparen Y_{B}<\min(x_{i},x_{j})\rparen dx_{i}dx_{j}

where
Y_{A}\sim\beta(w_{A},1),Y_{B}\sim\beta(w_{B},1),Y_{C}\sim\beta(w_{C},1)
. Using the convenient expression for the distribution function of a Beta distribution, we have

\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{1}w_{i}w_{j}x_{i}^{w_{i}-1}x_{j}^{w_{j}-1}\Pr\lparen Y_{A}<x_{i}\rparen\Pr\lparen Y_{C}<x_{j}\rparen\Pr\lparen Y_{B}<\min(x_{i},x_{j})\rparen dx_{i}dx_{j}\\
=\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{1}w_{i}w_{j}x_{i}^{w_{i}-1}x_{j}^{w_{j}-1}x_{i}^{w_{A}}x_{j}^{w_{C}}\min(x_{i},x_{j})^{w_{B}}dx_{i}dx_{j}

Splitting the region
[0,1]^{2}
into subregions
x_{i}<x_{j}
and
x_{i}\geq x_{j}
, we can calculate

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{x_{j}}w_{i}w_{j}x_{i}^{w_{i}-1}x_{j}^{w_{j}-1}x_{i}^{w_{A}}x_{j}^{w_{C}}x_{i}^{w_{B}}dx_{i}dx_{j}

\displaystyle\quad=\frac{w_{i}w_{j}}{w_{i}+w_{A}+w_{B}}\int_{0}^{1}x_{j}^{w_{j}+w_{C}+w_{i}+w_{A}+w_{B}-1}dx_{j}

\displaystyle\quad=\frac{w_{i}w_{j}}{(w_{i}+w_{A}+w_{B})w_{V}}

Similarly

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{x_{i}}w_{i}w_{j}x_{i}^{w_{i}-1}x_{j}^{w_{j}-1}x_{i}^{w_{A}}x_{j}^{w_{C}}x_{j}^{w_{B}}dx_{i}dx_{j}=\frac{w_{i}w_{j}}{(w_{j}+w_{B}+w_{C})w_{V}}

Overall, this yields

\Pr\lparen X_{i}>\max_{k\in A\cup B}X_{k}\textrm{ and }X_{j}>\max_{k\in B\cup C}X_{k}\rparen=\frac{w_{i}w_{j}}{w_{V}}\Big\lparen\frac{1}{w_{i}+w_{A}+w_{B}}+\frac{1}{w_{j}+w_{B}+w_{C}}\Big\rparen\qed

We now establish a few crucial properties of the 1-hop-max design.
In particular, the outcomes in the proof of Theorem
A.10
will be chosen so that they are supported on nodes in
U
. Thus, we need to establish an upper bound on the probability that a given node in
U
gets the all treatment exposure.
First, we recall a lower bound on the probability of exposure from
66
.

Lemma A.15

(Theorem 4.11 in
66
)
.

Let
G
be a graph, on which we run the 1-hop-max design. Then, for each node
i
in
G
, we have

\Pr\lparen E_{(i,1)}\rparen\geq\frac{1}{1+\lambda(G^{2})}\kern 5.0pt,

where
\lambda(G^{2})
is the maximum eigenvalue of the adjacency matrix of
G^{2}
.

We would like to establish an upper bound on the probability of exposure that is of the same order
\mathcal{O}\lparen 1/d^{2}\rparen
for any
i\in U
. Intuitively, the reason is that if
X_{i}
does not dominate the values of its neighbors, then all neighbors will likely be assigned to different clusters, due to the local tree-like structure of the graph around
i
. This means that for
i
to get the all treatment exposure, all these clusters must be assigned treatment, which happens with exponentially small probability. The details are given below.

Lemma A.16
.

Let
G
be the graph with
n
nodes described in the beginning of the section. Suppose we run the 1-hop-max design on this graph. Then, for any
i\in U
,

\Pr\lparen E_{(i,1)}\rparen=\mathcal{O}\Big\lparen\Big\{\frac{\log d}{d}\Big\}^{2}\Big\rparen\kern 5.0pt.

Proof.

By construction, we know that the degree of a node
i\in U
in
G
is
d
.
For an integer
r\in[d]
, define the events

\displaystyle F_{r,L}:=\{\text{There are at least }r\text{ nodes }j\in L_{i}\text{ such that }X_{j}>X_{l}\}

(19)

\displaystyle F_{r,R}:=\{\text{There are at least }r\text{ nodes }j\in R_{i}\text{ such that }X_{j}>X_{r}\}

(20)

In other words, if
F_{r,L}
(respectively
F_{r,R}
)occurs, then there are at least
r
neighbors of
i
in
L_{i}
(respectively
R_{i}
) with larger values of
X
than
l
(respectively
r
).
We will first show that at least one of
F_{r,L},F_{r,R}
holds with high probability.
First of all, it will be convenient to use the fact that if a variable
X
follows
\beta(w,1)
distribution, then
T=-\log X
follows
\textrm{Exp}(w)
distribution. Let us define the variables
T_{i}=-\log X_{i}
for all
i
.
Then,
F_{r,L}^{c}
is the event that
l
appears among the top
r
values of
\{X_{j},j\in L_{i}\}\cup\{X_{l}\}
, or equivalently, among the
r
smaller values of
\{T_{j},j\in L_{i}\}\cup\{T_{l}\}
.
By property
2
of Lemma
A.11
, we know that
w_{l}=\mathcal{O}\lparen\min_{j\in L_{i}}w_{j}\rparen
.
Thus, by Lemma
A.13
, we have that

\Pr\lparen F_{r,L}^{c}\rparen=\mathcal{O}\Big\lparen\frac{r}{d-r+1}\Big\rparen

The same logic yields

\Pr\lparen F_{r,R}^{c}\rparen=\mathcal{O}\Big\lparen\frac{r}{d-r+1}\Big\rparen

Notice that the events
F_{r,L}
and
F_{r,R}
depend on disjoint subsets of variables, thus are independent.
This implies that

\Pr\lparen F_{r,L}^{c}\cap F_{r,R}^{c}\rparen=\Pr\lparen F_{r,L}^{c}\rparen\cdot\Pr\lparen F_{r,R}^{c}\rparen=\mathcal{O}\Big\lparen\Big\lparen\frac{r}{d-r+1}\Big\rparen^{2}\Big\rparen\kern 5.0pt.

We now define the following events

\displaystyle G_{r,L}=\{\text{There are at least }r\text{ nodes }j\in L_{i}\text{ such that }X_{j}>X_{i}\}

\displaystyle G_{r,R}=\{\text{There are at least }r\text{ nodes }j\in R_{i}\text{ such that }X_{j}>X_{i}\}

By property
2
we know that
w_{i}=\mathcal{O}\lparen\min_{j\in L_{i}}w_{j}/d\rparen
. Thus, applying Lemma
A.13
, we get

\displaystyle\max\big\lparen\Pr\lparen G_{r,L}^{c}\rparen,\Pr\lparen G_{r,R}^{c}\rparen\big\rparen=\mathcal{O}\Big\lparen\frac{r}{d(d-r+1)}\Big\rparen

Thus, using the union bound we have that

\Pr\lparen(F_{r,L}^{c}\cap F_{r,R}^{c})\cup G_{r,L}^{c}\cup G_{r,R}^{c}\rparen=\mathcal{O}\Big\lparen\frac{r}{d(d-r+1)}\Big\rparen+\mathcal{O}\Big\lparen\Big\lparen\frac{r}{d-r+1}\Big\rparen^{2}\Big\rparen

(21)

Now, consider the complement event, i.e.

A:=\big\lparen(F_{r,L}^{c}\cap F_{r,R}^{c})\cup G_{r,L}^{c}\cup G_{r,R}^{c}\big\rparen^{c}=(F_{r,L}\cup F_{r,R})\cap G_{r,L}\cap G_{r,R}

If
A
holds, this means that either in the left or in the right set of neighbors of
i
, there are at least
r
of them that have bigger values than both
X_{i}
and
X_{l}
or
X_{r}
.
Let
S_{i,L}\subseteq L_{i}
be the set of left neighbors with larger value, i.e.

S_{i,L}:=\{j\in L_{i}:X_{j}>\max\lparen X_{i},X_{l}\rparen\}\kern 5.0pt.

We define the set
S_{i,R}
analogously for the neighbors on the right side.
Notice that for every node
j\in S_{i,L}
we have
c_{j}\neq i,l
, since
X_{j}>\max(X_{i},X_{l})
.
Also, by construction, the only common neighbors of
j,k\in L_{i}
are
i,l
. Thus, we conclude that every node in
S_{i,L}
will be assigned to a separate cluster.
Now, let us condition on the set
S_{i,L}
. Since each cluster is treated independently with probability
1/2
, the probability that all nodes in
S_{i,L}
receive treatment is
(1/2)^{|S_{i,L}|}
.
Therefore, we just proved that

\Pr\lparen E_{(i,1)}|S_{i,L}\rparen\leq\Big\lparen\frac{1}{2}\Big\rparen^{|S_{i,L}|}\kern 5.0pt.

Now, we suppose that
A
holds. By the preceding discussion, this implies
|S_{i,L}|\geq r
or
|S_{i,R}|\geq r
.
Without loss of generality, let’s assume that
|S_{i,L}|\geq r
. Since the above bound on the probability holds for any given
S_{i,L}
that we condition on, we conclude that

\Pr\lparen E_{(i,1)}\cap F_{r,L}\cap G_{r,L}\cap G_{r,R}\rparen\leq\Big\lparen\frac{1}{2}\Big\rparen^{r}\kern 5.0pt.

Exactly the same logic applies for the right side
R
, so using a union bound we get

\Pr\lparen E_{(i,1)}\cap(F_{r,L}\cup F_{r,R})\cap G_{r,L}\cap G_{r,R}\rparen\leq\Big\lparen\frac{1}{2}\Big\rparen^{r-1}\kern 5.0pt.

(22)

Thus, combining inequalites (
22
) and (
21
) we get for every
r

\Pr\lparen E_{(i,1)}\rparen=\mathcal{O}\Bigg\lparen\Bigg\lparen\frac{1}{2}\Bigg\rparen^{r-1}+\frac{r}{d(d-r+1)}+\Big\lparen\frac{r}{d-r+1}\Big\rparen^{2}\Bigg\rparen

Choosing
r=3\log d
in the above yields the desired result.
∎

We next show a lower bound for the joint probability of two nodes in
U
to receive the all treatment exposure.
This is where we crucially need to use the extra nodes
l,r
. Without them, this probability would be of order
O(1/d^{4})
. However, with nodes
l,r
connected to all nodes in
L_{i},R_{i}
respectively, for all
i\in U
, it turns out the answer is
\Theta(1/d^{2})
.

Lemma A.17
.

Let
i,j\in U
, where
U
is as in Lemma
A.11
. Let
E_{(i,1)}
and
E_{(j,1)}
be the events that nodes
i
and
j
receive the all treatment exposure under the 1-hop-max design on
G_{n}
. Then, we have

\Pr\lparen E_{(i,1)}\cap E_{(j,1)}\rparen=\Omega\Big\lparen\frac{1}{d^{2}}\Big\rparen\kern 5.0pt.

Proof.

For an arbitrary subset of nodes
R
, we abuse notation and denote by
N(R)
the union of neighbors of nodes in
R
.
Define the events

\displaystyle H_{L}=\{X_{l}>\max(X_{i},X_{j},\max_{k\in L_{i}\cup L_{j}\cup N(L_{i})\cup N(L_{j})}X_{k})\}

\displaystyle H_{R}=\{X_{r}>\max(X_{i},X_{j},\max_{k\in R_{i}\cup R_{j}\cup N(R_{i})\cup N(R_{j})}X_{k})\}

We claim that if
H_{L}
(respectively
H_{R}
) holds, then all nodes in
L_{i}\cup L_{j}
(respectively
R_{i}\cup R_{j}
) will be assigned to the cluster
l
(respectively
r
). To see this, note that for
k\in L_{i}
, the possible clusters it could be assigned to are all their neighbors and itself. By the definition of the event
H_{L}
,
X_{l}
has the largest value among all neighbors of
k
and
k
itself, so
c_{k}=l
. Similarly we argue that if
k\in L_{j}
and
H_{L}
holds then
c_{k}=l
. Also, analogously we can argue for
H_{R}
.
Thus, if
H_{L},H_{R}
both hold, then all nodes in
L_{i}\cup L_{j}
will be in the cluster of
l
and all nodes in
R_{i}\cup R_{j}
in the cluster of
r
.
Thus, conditioned on that event, the probability that all nodes in
L_{i}\cup L_{j}\cup R_{i}\cup R_{j}
receive treatment is at least
1/4
. The nodes
i,j
might be in different clusters, so the probability that they are both also assigned treatment is again at least
1/4
.
Overall, this shows that

\Pr\lparen E_{(i,1)}\cap E_{(j,1)}\rparen\geq\frac{1}{16}\Pr\lparen H_{L}\cap H_{R}\rparen

Thus, the claim reduces to showing that
\Pr\lparen H_{L}\cap H_{R}\rparen=\Omega\lparen 1/d^{2}\rparen
.
Notice that the event
H_{L}\cap H_{R}
can be written exactly in the form of Lemma
A.14
,
i=l,j=r
and

\displaystyle A=\lparen L_{i}\cup L_{j}\cup N(L_{i})\cup N(L_{j})\rparen\setminus\{i,j,l,r\}

\displaystyle C=\lparen R_{i}\cup R_{j}\cup N(R_{i})\cup N(R_{j})\rparen\setminus\{i,j,l,r\}

\displaystyle B=\{i,j\}

Since the 2-hop neighborhoods of
i,j
are tree-like, it is clear that
A,B,C
are pairwise disjoint.
Thus, applying Lemma
A.14
yields

\displaystyle\Pr\lparen H_{L}\cap H_{R}\rparen=\frac{w_{l}w_{r}}{w_{l}+w_{r}+w_{i}+w_{j}+\sum_{k\in N(i)\cup N(j)\cup(N(N(i))\cup N(N(j)))\setminus\{i,j,l,r\}}w_{k}}

\displaystyle\cdot\left(\frac{1}{w_{l}+w_{i}+w_{j}+\sum_{k\in\cup L_{i}\cup L_{j}\cup(N(L_{i})\cup N(L_{j}))\setminus\{i,j,l,r\}}w_{k}}\right.

\displaystyle+\left.\frac{1}{w_{r}+w_{i}+w_{j}+\sum_{k\in\cup R_{i}\cup R_{j}\cup(N(R_{i})\cup N(R_{j}))\setminus\{i,j,l,r\}}w_{k}}\right)

We now use property
2
of Lemma
A.11
. This says that for
k,r\in N(i)\cup N(j)\cup\{l,r\}
we have
w_{k}/w_{r}=\Theta\lparen 1\rparen
and for
k\in N(i)\cup N(j)\cup\{l,r\}
and
r\in N(N(i))\cup N(N(j))\setminus\{l,r\}

w_{k}/w_{r}=\Theta\lparen d^{2}\rparen
, wince
r
belongs to the bottom layer of the graph
C
.
Finally, by construction we have that
|N(N(i))|,|N(N(j))|=\mathcal{O}\lparen d^{2}\rparen
. Putting all of this together, we conclude that

\Pr\lparen H_{L}\cap H_{R}\rparen=\frac{\Theta\lparen d^{2}\rparen}{\Theta\lparen d^{2}\rparen}\Big\lparen\frac{1}{\Theta\lparen d^{2}\rparen}+\frac{1}{\Theta\lparen d^{2}\rparen}\Big\rparen=\Theta\Big\lparen\frac{1}{d^{2}}\Big\rparen\kern 5.0pt.\qed

We now have all the necessary tools for the proof of our main result of the section.

Proof of Theorem
A.10
.

We will use the sequence of graphs
G_{n}
constructed in the beginning of the section, where
d_{n}=(1+o(1))n^{1/3}
.
Let us first discuss the performance of the Conflict Graph Design on this sequence of graphs. By Lemma
A.11
we have that
\lambda(G_{n}^{2})=O(d_{n}^{2})
.
Thus, if the potential outcomes satisfy Assumption
2
, then by Theorem
4.1
, the mean-squred error of the modified Horvitz-Thompson estimator
\hat{\tau}_{\operatorname{CGC}}
is bounded as

\Esym_{#1}[\big\lparen\tau-\hat{\tau}_{\operatorname{CGD}}\big\rparen^{2}]=\mathcal{O}\Big\lparen\frac{\lambda(G_{n}^{2})}{n}\Big\rparen=\mathcal{O}\Big\lparen\frac{d^{2}}{n}\Big\rparen\kern 5.0pt,

which implies (
16
).
We now turn to analyzing the performance of the standard Horvitz-Thompson estimator
\hat{\tau}_{\operatorname{1-hm}}
under the 1-hop-max design on the sequance of graphs
G_{n}
.

We begin by specifying the sequence of potential outcomes that we will be considering. We will focus on nodes in
U
, so only the outcomes for these nodes will be non-zero. Also, we will focus on the all treatment exposure only, so the all control exposure outcomes will be zero for all nodes.
Specifically, for each node
i\in U
, we set
y_{i}(\boldsymbol{1})=\sqrt{n/d}
.
In other words,

y_{i}^{(n)}(\boldsymbol{0})=0\quad,\quad y_{i}^{(n)}(\boldsymbol{1})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\sqrt{\frac{n}{d}}&\text{ , if }i\in U\\
0&\text{ , otherwise}\end{array}\right.

Clearly Assumption
2
is satisfied for this choice of outcomes, since

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Big\lparen y_{i}(\boldsymbol{1})^{2}+y_{i}(\boldsymbol{0})^{2}\Big\rparen

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in U}\frac{n}{d}=1\kern 5.0pt.

As stated in
66
, the estimator
\hat{\tau}_{\operatorname{1-hm}}
is unbiased for the GATE
\tau
. Thus, to understand the mean-squared error, it suffices to analyze the variance of
\hat{\tau}_{\operatorname{1-hm}}
.
We start by writing the variance of the estimator as

\displaystyle\Varsym\lparen\hat{\tau}_{\operatorname{1-hm}}\rparen

\displaystyle=\Varsym\Big\lparen\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(i,1)}]}{\Pr\lparen E_{(i,1)}\rparen}Y_{i}\Big\rparen

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\Covsym\Big\lparen\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(i,1)}]}{\Pr\lparen E_{(i,1)}\rparen}Y_{i},\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{j,1}]}{\Pr\lparen E_{j,1}\rparen}Y_{j}\Big\rparen

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i\in U}\sum_{j\in U}\Bigg\lparen\frac{\Pr\lparen E_{(i,1)}\cap E_{j,1}\rparen}{\Pr\lparen E_{(i,1)}\cdot\Pr\lparen E_{j,1}\rparen\rparen}-1\Bigg\rparen\cdot y_{i}(\boldsymbol{1})y_{j}(\boldsymbol{1})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{dn}\sum_{i\in U}\sum_{j\in U}\Bigg\lparen\frac{\Pr\lparen E_{(i,1)}\cap E_{j,1}\rparen}{\Pr\lparen E_{(i,1)}\cdot\Pr\lparen E_{j,1}\rparen\rparen}-1\Bigg\rparen\kern 5.0pt.

Now, using the bounds on the probabilities of exposure from Lemmas
A.17
and
A.16
, we have that for each
i,j\in U
with
i\neq j
,

\frac{\Pr\lparen E_{(i,1)}\cap E_{j,1}\rparen}{\Pr\lparen E_{(i,1)}\cdot\Pr\lparen E_{j,1}\rparen\rparen}-1=\Omega\Big\lparen\frac{\frac{1}{d^{2}}}{\Big\lparen\frac{\log^{2}d}{d^{2}}\Big\rparen^{2}}\Big\rparen=\Omega\Big\lparen\frac{d^{2}}{\log^{4}d}\Big\rparen\kern 5.0pt.

If
i=j
, then similarly we get from Lemma
A.16

\frac{1}{\Pr\lparen E_{(i,1)}\rparen}-1=\Omega\Big\lparen\frac{d^{2}}{\log^{2}d}\Big\rparen=\Omega\Big\lparen\frac{d^{2}}{\log^{4}d}\Big\rparen

Thus, we conclude that

\Varsym\lparen\hat{\tau}_{\textrm{standard}}\rparen=\frac{1}{dn}\cdot d^{2}\cdot\Omega\Big\lparen\frac{d^{2}}{\log^{4}d}\Big\rparen=\Omega\Big\lparen\frac{d^{3}}{n\log^{4}d}\Big\rparen\kern 5.0pt.

Using the fact that
d_{n}=(1+o(1))n^{1/3}
and taking the limit
n\to\infty
in the above implies (
17
).
∎

Appendix B
Additional Simulation Results

In this section, we present additional discussion of the simulation results.
We focus here on reporting aspects of the inferential procedures.
For all simulations, we report intervals for the
95\%
confidence level.

See Section
6
for the detailed discussion of the networks that are used in the simulation.
In these extended simulations, we consider two types of outcomes:

•

Large Outliers
:
y_{i}(e_{0})=\alpha_{i,1}
and
y_{i}(e_{1})=\alpha_{i,2}\cdot\sqrt{\textrm{deg}_{G}(i)}

•

Medium Outliers
:
y_{i}(e_{0})=\alpha_{i,1}
and
y_{i}(e_{1})=\alpha_{i,2}\cdot\lparen\textrm{deg}_{G}(i)\rparen^{1/4}

where the coefficients are sampled independently across units as
\alpha_{i,1}\sim\mathcal{N}(1,1)
and
\alpha_{i,2}\sim\mathcal{N}(2,1)
.
In each of these settings, the potential outcome under control exposure
e_{0}
is kept roughly constant while the potential outcome under treatment exposure
e_{1}
is proportional to some power of the unit’s degree in the network.
By the construction of the preferential attachment network, one can verify that the Large Outliers construction ensures boundedness of the second moment (Assumption
2
)
but the fourth moment (Assumption
4
) will generally not be bounded.
On the other hand, the Medium Outliers construction ensures boundedness of 4th moments but not necessarily boundedness of higher moments.
In the sense that moment conditions reflect outlier behavior, these two outcome settings reflect different outlier behavior.

Recall that boundedness of the second moment was required for
\sqrt{n/\lambda(\mathcal{H})}
-consistency (Assumption
2
) and that boundedness of the fourth moment (Assumption
4
) was a sufficient condition both for consistent variance estimation and the Central Limit Theorem, which played a key role in the construction of valid confidence intervals.
Thus, we may anticipate that while the Large Outliers setting allowed for consistent point estimation, the inferential procedures will only work for Medium Outliers.

Recall that the Central Limit Theorem (Theorem
5.5
) also required a strong condition on the maximum degree of the conflict graph, i.e.
d_{\textrm{max}}(\mathcal{H})=o\lparen n^{1/9}\rparen
.
We feel that this reflects a limitation of the proof technique rather than a necessary condition for a CLT.
In the numerical simulations, the assumption
d_{\textrm{max}}(\mathcal{H})=o\lparen n^{1/9}\rparen
is far from holding and so these simulations may be viewed as a “stress test” of the inferential procedures when this assumption is not met.
On the other hand, the fourth moment condition seems both necessary and sufficient for obtaining precise variance estimators at the given rates (Proposition
5.3
).
For this reason, we vary the moments of the outcomes.

Throughout our simulations, we examine the following confidence intervals:

•

Wald (exact
\Varsym
)
: A Wald confidence interval where the exact variance is used. This is an oracle confidence interval because the exact variance is not known to the experimenter. This reflects the behavior of the “true” standardized estimator.

•

Wald (exact
\mathrm{VB}
)
: A Wald confidence interval where the exact variance bound is used. This is an oracle confidence interval because the exact variance bound is not known to the experimenter. This reflects the behavior of the “variance bound” standardized estimator.

•

Wald (
\widehat{\mathrm{VB}}
)
: A Wald confidence interval using the conservative variance estimator, proposed in Section
5.2
.

•

Chebyshev (exact
\Varsym
)
: A Chebyshev confidence interval where the exact variance is used. This is an oracle confidence interval because the exact variance is not known to the experimenter.

•

Chebyshev (exact
\mathrm{VB}
)
: A Chebyshev confidence interval where the exact variance bound is used. This is an oracle confidence interval because the exact variance bound is not known to the experimenter.

•

Chebyshev (
\widehat{\mathrm{VB}}
)
: A Chebyshev confidence interval using the conservative variance estimator, proposed in Section
5.2
.

B.1
Direct Treatment Effect

Let us first consider the direct treatment effect.
We begin by examining the inferential methods in the presence of Large Outliers.

(a)

Expected CI Widths

(b)

Coverage

Figure 8
:
Confidence Interval Statistics for Direct Treatment Effect in the Large Outliers setting.

Figure
8
contains a plot of the expected interval widths and the coverage as
n
grows.
In terms of interval widths, we see what is to be expected: namely, that the Chebyshev-types widths are larger than the Wald-type widths and within these classes, intervals based on the variance bound are larger than intervals based on the exact variance.
We see that the Chebyshev intervals cover above the nominal rate, which is to be expected.
The oracle Wald intervals cover slightly above the nominal rate while the actual Wald intervals cover significantly below the nominal rate.

(a)

Standardized Estimator Histogram

(b)

Variance of the ratio
\widehat{\mathrm{VB}}/\mathrm{VB}

Figure 9
:
Additional interval statistics for Direct Treatment Effect in the Large Outliers setting.

Our goal now is to explain the behavior of the Wald-type intervals.
We begin by looking at the histogram of the standardized estimator
\lparen\hat{\tau}-\tau\rparen/{\sqrt{\Varsym\lparen\hat{\tau}\rparen}}
at
n=3,000
samples, contained in Figure
9(a)
.
Although the histograms are approximately a standard normal, they are different in two ways:
(1) the standardized effect estimator is slightly more concentrated around 0 for moderate values of the error (e.g. less than 2 in absolute value) and (2) far away from 0, the right tails are thicker than a standard normal.

We attribute these irregularities to the high degree nodes, which also have large outcomes.
We know that such behavior can theoretically occur even when the estimator is consistent.
See Proposition
5.7
and its proof in Section
E.8
for more rigorous discussion.
Roughly speaking, the high degree nodes contribute significantly to the standardized error when assigned to treatment exposure, but this is a low probability event.
Informally speaking, this results in the standardized error distribution being narrower than
\mathcal{N}(0,1)
around 0, but having little peaks far away from 0, though these are typically vanishing in large samples.
This phenomenon explains the histogram we see in Figure
8(b)
and also why the oracle Wald intervals over-cover ever so slightly.

This does not explain, however, why the actual Wald intervals severely undercover.
To understand this better, we turn our attention to the variance estimator,
\widehat{\mathrm{VB}}
.
The usual Slutsky-style arguments for asymptotic coverage require that
\widehat{\mathrm{VB}}/\mathrm{VB}
converges in probability to 1.
While it is (strictly speaking) not a necessary condition, examining the variance of the ratio
\widehat{\mathrm{VB}}/\mathrm{VB}
will help us understand whether this concentration is happening.
As we see in Figure
9(b)
, this variance is not decreasing with the sample size.
We believe that this is the explanation for the severe under-coverage of the Wald intervals.
In particular, we believe that the variance is hard to estimate because of the presence of Large Outliers, i.e. unbounded fourth moments.
This simulation provides numerical evidence to affirm that the bounded fourth moments really are necessary for the statistical validity of the proposed inferential methods.

(a)

Expected CI Widths

(b)

Coverage

Figure 10
:
Additional interval statistics for Direct Treatment Effect in the Medium Outliers setting.

Thus, we are motivated to investigate the statistical validity of the proposed inferential methods when the fourth moments are bounded.
See Figure
10
for the expected width and coverage of the intervals for the Medium Outlier setting as
n
grows.
We see that the widths behave as expected: Chebyshev intervals are wider than Wald intervals and within these classes the intervals using variance bound is larger than those using the true variance.
We see that the coverage is now behaving as expected as well.
At around
n=1,000
, the Wald intervals cover at the nominal level.
As before, the Chebyshev intervals over-cover at all included sample sizes.

(a)

Standardized Estimator Histogram

(b)

Variance of the ratio
\widehat{\mathrm{VB}}/\mathrm{VB}

Figure 11
:
Consistency Statistics for Direct Treatment Effect in the Medium Outliers setting.

Let us confirm that both ingredients of the inferential procedure are working.
In Figure
11(a)
we see that the histogram of the standardized error is approximately normal.
There is but the slightest hint of a bump in the right tail, reflecting the presence of a few high degree nodes.
Moreover, we see in Figure
11(b)
that the variance of the ratio
\widehat{\mathrm{VB}}/\mathrm{VB}
is roughly decreasing so that convergence of the variance estimator appears to be happening.
There are some bumps in this deceasing plot, but we attribute this to the random generation of the network.

Figure 12
:
Comparison of the variance of estimating DTE between the various experimental designs in the Medium Outliers setting.

But how does the Conflict Graph Design behave relative to the benchmark Independent Set Design?
Figure
12
shows that in the setting of Medium Outliers, the Independent Set Design achieves a lower variance than the Conflict Graph Design with modified or standard Horvitz–Thompson estimators.
This improvement is by a factor of about 2-3.
For this simulation set-up, the Conflict Graph Design achieves improved variance in the presence of outliers but the benchmark design achieves lower variance when fewer outliers are present.

B.2
Global Average Treatment Effect

We now consider the Global Average Treatment Effect.
Roughly speaking, the findings above remain more or less the same: the Wald-type intervals under-cover due to statistical fluctuations in the variance estimators arising from large outliers.
As the outliers are decreased, the coverage are trending towards the nominal levels.
The main difference here is that even in the medium outlier setting, the sample size does not appear large enough for the coverage to approach the nominal levels.

(a)

Expected CI Widths

(b)

Coverage

(c)

Standardized Estimator Histogram

(d)

Variance of the ratio
\widehat{\mathrm{VB}}/\mathrm{VB}

Figure 13
:
Confidence Interval Statistics for Global Average Treatment Effect in the Large Outliers setting.

Figure
13
shows the statistical summaries of the inferential procedures under the Large Outliers setting.
While the oracle interval widths look as expected (e.g. Chebyshev is larger than Wald), the widths of the actual intervals display a certain peculiarity: the actual intervals have a width that is closer to the actual variance than the variance bound.
Moreover, the actual intervals severely under-cover while the oracle Wald interval (exact variance) covers at the nominal level.
Both of these peculiarities can be explain in the same way as before; namely, the variance estimator has too much statistical fluctuation.
This is corroborated in two ways.
First, Figure
13(c)
that shows the standardized estimator is approximately normal, albeit with a right skew due to high degree nodes with large outcomes.
Even so, the tail approximation obtained from a normal works sufficiently well in the sense that the oracle Wald interval covers at the nominal rate.
Second, Figure
13(d)
demonstrates that
\widehat{\mathrm{VB}}/\mathrm{VB}
has variance which is not decreasing sufficiently fast.
We conjecture that this is again due to the Large Outliers, i.e. the fourth moments are not bounded.

(a)

Expected CI Widths

(b)

Coverage

(c)

Standardized Estimator Histogram

(d)

Variance of the ratio
\widehat{\mathrm{VB}}/\mathrm{VB}

Figure 14
:
Additional interval statistics for Global Average Treatment Effect in the Medium Outliers setting.

To test this explanation, we investigate the performance of the inferential procedures in the setting of Medium Outliers.
Figure
14
shows the relevant statistical summaries.
The figures look roughly the same as in the case of Large Outliers, except that the coverage is slightly better, though still under-covering.
This under-coverage of the Wald intervals was initially puzzling to us because our simulation results indicate that both (1) the normal approximation is relatively good (2) the variance estimator has statistical fluctuation which is decreasing with the sample size.
The remaining explanation is that the statistical fluctuation, while decreasing, is not small enough for
\widehat{\mathrm{VB}}
to be a sufficiently good approximation of
\mathrm{VB}
for the purposes of coverage of the interval.

To test this explanation, we turn to our theoretical predictions.
In the proof of coverage for the Wald-style intervals (Corollary
5.6
), we show that the following holds in the asymptotic limit:

\Pr\lparen\tau\in C_{\textrm{Wald}}(\alpha)\rparen\approx 1-2\cdot\Bigg\lparen 1-\Phi_{\sigma}\Big\lparen\Phi_{1}^{-1}(1-\alpha/2)\cdot\sqrt{1-\epsilon}\Big\rparen\Bigg\rparen\quad\text{where}\quad\Big\lvert\frac{\widehat{\mathrm{VB}}}{\mathrm{VB}}-1\Big\rvert\approx\epsilon

and
\Phi_{\sigma}^{-1}
is the normal quantile function with
\sigma^{2}=\Varsym\lparen\hat{\tau}\rparen/\mathrm{VB}
to reflect the increase due to the variance bound.
Proposition
5.3
shows that under the fourth moment condition on the outcomes, the quantity
\epsilon
will be on the order
\mathcal{O}_{p}\lparen\sqrt{\lambda(\mathcal{H})/n}\rparen
.
Thus, we derived the predicted coverage rate:

\textup{Pr}_{\textup{theory}}(\alpha)\triangleq 1-2\cdot\Bigg\lparen 1-\Phi_{\sigma}\Big\lparen\Phi_{1}^{-1}(1-\alpha/2)\cdot\sqrt{1-c\cdot\frac{\lambda(\mathcal{H})}{n}}\Big\rparen\Bigg\rparen\kern 5.0pt,

where
c
is a constant that we will choose.
Our goal was then to test whether this predicted coverage rate matched the true coverage rate of our method, for some value of
c
.

(a)

Direct Treatment Effect

(b)

Global Average Treatment Effect

Figure 15
:
Comparison of theoretically predicted coverage with true coverage in the Medium Outliers setting.

Figure
15
plots the predicted coverage against the true coverage of the Wald intervals for the setting of Medium Outliers in both effects.
We see in both figures that the predicted coverage probability matches the true coverage probability of the Wald intervals quite well, for the chosen constants.
Figure
15(a)
confirms that the coverage for the Direct Treatment Effect is at the nominal levels.
At the same time, Figure
15(b)
confirms that the coverage for the Global Average Treatment Effect is not yet at the nominal levels.

So what explains this difference in coverage between the two effects considered in the simulation?
In short, it is due to the difference in the rate of convergence of the variance estimators.
This difference is driven by the difference in the rates
\sqrt{\lambda(\mathcal{H})/n}
between the two settings.
Recall that the Conflict Graph for the direct effect is
\mathcal{H}=G
while the Conflict Graph for the Global Average Treatment Effect is the two-hop graph, i.e.
\mathcal{H}=G^{2}
.

Figure 16
:
Comparison of
\lambda(\mathcal{H})/n
rates between the graphs used for DTE and GATE estimation.

Figure
16
demonstrates this difference in the rates.
Because
\lambda(\mathcal{H})/n
converges to zero much faster for the direct treatment effect, the asymptotics kick in faster than for the Global Average Treatment Effect.
This example highlights that more global effects are indeed more difficult to estimate than more local effects.
This should be taken into account when conducting power analyses before running an experiment.

Figure 17
:
Comparison of the variance of estimating GATE between the various experimental designs in the Medium Outliers setting.

Figure
17
shows that in the presence of fewer outliers, the RGCR designs continue to achieve smaller variance than the Conflict Graph Design.

B.3
Practical Considerations

The simulation exercise reveals several practical considerations.
First, that although the Conflict Graph Design achieves improved rates of convergence, it is always worthwhile to benchmark against alternative designs.
Second, that the performance of a design against
worst-case
potential outcomes satisfying bounded second moment may not reflect the performance of a design against
typical
potential outcomes.
As always, pre-experiment simulation studies are recommended.

From a methodological perspective, we see that the biggest benefit may come from devising new inferential methods that work in the presence of larger outliers.
This is especially important when the outliers may also have high degrees in the graph.
It remains an open question to offer a more systematic treatment of inference in these high-degree regimes.
Until then, coverage is typically guaranteed by Chebyshev interval and this may be a safer bet for many practitioners.
Moreover, the theoretical approximations for coverage probabilities
\textup{Pr}_{\textup{theory}}(\alpha)
derived above may be useful for power calculations until more rigorous methods are made available.

Appendix C
Conflict Graph Design: Preliminary Lemmas

In this Section, we collect the proofs of some preliminary lemmas about the Conflict Graph Design and the modified Horvitz-Thompson estimator.
Let’s start by introducing some notation that will be helpful for the remainder of this Appendix. For a given conflict graph
\mathcal{H}
and importance ordering
\pi
, we say that unit
i
is a
parent
of unit
j
, or
j
is a
child
of
i
, if and only if
i\in\mathcal{N}_{b}^{\pi}(j)
.

C.1
Desired Exposures (Lemma
3.2
)

We start by proving Lemma
3.2
, which says that if event
E_{(i,k)}
holds for a unit
i
, then it will receive the desired exposure in the final treatment assignment.

Lemma
3.2
.

Under the event
E_{(i,k)}
, we have that
d_{i}(Z)=d_{i}(\boldsymbol{z}_{i,(k)})
.

Proof.

Suppose
E_{(i,k)}
occurs. Then, the condition in Line
2
will be satisfied for a unit
i
.
Then, Line
2
will be executed, which will cause the algorithm to assign individual assignments to unit
i
and its neighbors so that it receives the desired exposure. In other words, at the end of the iteration corresponding to
i
, we have
\forall j\in\widetilde{N}(i):Z_{j}=\boldsymbol{z}_{i,(k)}(j)
.

We claim that the algorithm will not “over-write” these individual assignments later in the execution.
To see this, first observe that only neighbors of
i
in the conflict graph could potentially cause such a change.
Indeed, if a unit
r
is not a neighbor of
i
in the conflict graph, then if Line
2
was executed for
r
, it would set
\forall j\in\widetilde{N}(r):Z_{j}=\boldsymbol{z}_{r,(k)}(j)
. Since
r
and
j
are not neighbors in the conflict graph, by definition this means that no entries of the neighborhood
\widetilde{N}(i)
of
i
would be overwritten by a different value.

Let us now focus on a node
r\in\mathcal{N}(i)
. If
r
is more important than
i
, meaning
r\in\mathcal{N}_{b}^{\pi}(i)
, then
event
E_{(i,k)}
ensures that Line
2
will not be executed for
r
, since
U_{r}=*
. Thus, it will not affect the neighborhood of
i
.
If
r
is less important than
i
in
\pi
, meaning
i\in\mathcal{N}_{b}^{\pi}(r)
, then
again event
E_{(i,k)}
ensures that Line
2
will not be executed for
r
, since
U_{i}\neq*
. Thus, it will also not affect the neighborhood of
i
.
Intuitively, less important units “yield” to the desires of their more important neighbors.

Overall, this shows that in the final treatment vector
Z
the assignment in
\widetilde{N}(i)
will not be modified after
i
. Thus, we still have
\forall j\in\widetilde{N}(i):Z_{j}=\boldsymbol{z}_{i,(k)}(j)
and the proof is complete.
∎

C.2
Expressions for Desired Exposure Probabilities (Lemma
3.3
)

In this Section, we provide simple expressions for the desired exposure probabilities
\Pr\lparen E_{(i,k)}\rparen
used in the effect estimator.
These closed form expressions will help us bounding the variance of the effect estimator.
Because the desired exposure probabilities have these simple closed forms, the modified Horvitz–Thompson estimator does not require any computationally expensive procedure to compute these probabilities, e.g. Monte Carlo simulations.

Lemma
3.3
.

For unit
i\in[n]
and exposure
k\in\{0,1\}
, the probability of
E_{(i,k)}
is

\Pr\big\lparen E_{(i,k)}\big\rparen=\frac{1}{r\cdot 2\lambda(\mathcal{H})}\cdot\Big\lparen 1-\frac{1}{r\cdot\lambda(\mathcal{H})}\Big\rparen^{\lvert\mathcal{N}_{b}^{\pi}(i)\rvert}

For a pair of distinct units
i\neq j\in[n]
and exposures
k,\ell\in\{0,1\}
, the probability of the intersection of desired exposure events is
\Pr\lparen E_{(i,k)}\cap E_{(j,\ell)}\rparen=0
if
i
and
j
are adjacent in
\mathcal{H}
and otherwise

\Pr\big\lparen E_{(i,k)}\cap E_{(j,\ell)}\big\rparen=\Big\lparen\frac{1}{r\cdot 2\lambda(\mathcal{H})}\Big\rparen^{2}\cdot\Big\lparen 1-\frac{1}{r\cdot\lambda(\mathcal{H})}\Big\rparen^{\lvert\mathcal{N}_{b}^{\pi}(i)\cup\mathcal{N}_{b}^{\pi}(j)\rvert}\kern 5.0pt.

Proof.

We start by computing the probability of event
E_{(i,k)}
. By definition, and since the
U_{i}
variables are independent, we have

\displaystyle\Pr\lparen E_{(i,k)}\rparen

\displaystyle=\Pr\Big\lparen U_{i}=e_{k},U_{j}=*\forall j\in\mathcal{N}_{b}^{\pi}(i)\Big\rparen

\displaystyle=\Pr\Big\lparen U_{i}=e_{k}\Big\rparen\prod_{j\in\mathcal{N}_{b}^{\pi}(i)}\Pr\Big\lparen U_{j}=*\Big\rparen

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2r\lambda(\mathcal{H})}\Big\lparen 1-\frac{1}{r\lambda(\mathcal{H})}\Big\rparen^{|\mathcal{N}_{b}^{\pi}(i)|}\,,

which proves the first claim.

For a pair of units
i,j
that are connected by an edge in
\mathcal{H}
, if
E_{(i,k)},E_{(j,l)}
both hold, then
U_{i}\neq*,U_{j}\neq*
. As we already observed in the proof of Lemma
3.2
, either
i\in\mathcal{N}_{b}(j)
or
j\in\mathcal{N}_{b}(i)
occur.
Suppose
i\in\mathcal{N}_{b}(j)
without loss of generality. Then,
E_{(j,l)}
dictates that
U_{i}=*
since
i
is a parent of
j
, which is a contradiction. Thus,
E_{(i,k)}\cap E_{(j,l)}=\emptyset
.

Finally, suppose
i
and
j
are not adjacent in
\mathcal{H}
.
Then, we can write

\displaystyle\Pr\Big\lparen E_{(i,k)}\cap E_{(j,l)}\Big\rparen

\displaystyle=\Pr\Big\lparen U_{i}=e_{k},U_{j}=e_{l},U_{r}=*\forall r\in\mathcal{N}_{b}^{\pi}(i),U_{s}=*\forall s\in\mathcal{N}_{b}^{\pi}(j)\Big\rparen

\displaystyle=\Pr\Big\lparen U_{i}=e_{k},U_{j}=e_{l},U_{r}=*\forall r\in\mathcal{N}_{b}^{\pi}(i)\cup\mathcal{N}_{b}^{\pi}(j)\Big\rparen

\displaystyle=\Pr\Big\lparen U_{i}=e_{k}\Big\rparen\Pr\Big\lparen U_{j}=e_{l}\Big\rparen\prod_{r\in\mathcal{N}_{b}^{\pi}(i)\cup\mathcal{N}_{b}^{\pi}(j)}\Pr\Big\lparen U_{r}=*\Big\rparen

\displaystyle=\Big\lparen\frac{1}{2r\lambda(\mathcal{H})}\Big\rparen^{2}\cdot\Big\lparen 1-\frac{1}{r\lambda(\mathcal{H})}\Big\rparen^{\lvert\mathcal{N}_{b}^{\pi}(i)\cup\mathcal{N}_{b}^{\pi}(j)\rvert}\kern 5.0pt.

∎

C.3
Effect Estimator is Unbiased (Proposition
3.5
)

Next, using Lemma
3.2
, we prove that our estimator is unbiased.

Proposition
3.5
.

Under arbitrary neighborhood interference (Assumption
1
), the

modified Horvitz–Thompson
estimator is unbiased under the Conflict Graph Design:
\Esym[\hat{\tau}]=\tau
.

Proof.

By Lemma
3.2
, we know that under the event
E_{(i,k)}
, then
d_{i}(Z)=d_{i}(\boldsymbol{z}_{i,(k)})
.
By Assumption
1
, this implies that
Y_{i}=y_{i}(Z)=y_{i}(\boldsymbol{z}_{i,(k)})
.
Thus, we always have the identity
Y_{i}\mathbf{1}[E_{(i,k)}]=y_{i}(\boldsymbol{z}_{i,(k)})\mathbf{1}[E_{(i,k)}]
for
k\in\{0,1\}
.
Furthermore, by Lemma
3.3
we have
\Pr\lparen E_{(i,k)}\rparen>0
, for all
i\in[n]
and
k\in\{0,1\}
.
The above observations allow us to write

\displaystyle\Esym[\hat{\tau}]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Esym\Bigg[Y_{i}\left(\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(i,1)}]}{\Pr\lparen E_{(i,1)}\rparen}-\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(i,0)}]}{\Pr\lparen E_{(i,0)}\rparen}\right)\Bigg]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(y_{i}(\boldsymbol{z}_{i,(1)})\frac{\Esym\big[\mathbf{1}[E_{(i,1)}]\big]}{\Pr\lparen E_{(i,1)}\rparen}-y_{i}(\boldsymbol{z}_{i,(0)})\frac{\Esym\big[\mathbf{1}[E_{(i,0)}]\big]}{\Pr\lparen E_{(i,0)}\rparen}\right)

\displaystyle=\tau

∎

C.4
Eigenvector Importance Ordering (Proposition
3.1
)

In this section, we prove that the eigenvector ordering
\pi_{\textup{eig}}
defined in Section
3.2
is an importance ordering.
For continuity, the proof is stated in terms of conflict graphs; however, the proof works for arbitrary graphs more generally.

Proposition
3.1
.

The eigenvector ordering
\pi_{\textup{eig}}
is an importance ordering.

Proof.

Assume that the conflict graph
\mathcal{H}
is connected.
We will see at the end of the proof how the general graphs can be handled in terms of connected components.
Because
\mathcal{H}
is connected, the Perron Frobenius theorem states the eigenvector
\boldsymbol{v}
corresponding to the largest eigenvalue can be chosen to have positive entries, i.e.
\boldsymbol{v}(i)>0
for all
i\in[n]
.

Recall that
\pi_{\textup{eig}}
is an importance ordering if

\lvert\mathcal{N}_{b}^{\pi_{\textup{eig}}}(i)\rvert\leq\lambda(\mathcal{H})\quad\text{where}\quad\mathcal{N}_{b}^{\pi_{\textup{eig}}}(i)=\Big\{j\in\mathcal{N}(i):\pi_{\textup{eig}}(j)<\pi_{\textup{eig}}(j)\Big\}\kern 5.0pt.

Let us also define the neighbors which come after unit
i
in the ordering as

\mathcal{N}_{a}^{\pi_{\textup{eig}}}(i)=\Big\{j\in\mathcal{N}(i):\pi_{\textup{eig}}(j)>\pi_{\textup{eig}}(j)\Big\}\kern 5.0pt,

which are the children of unit
i\in[n]
.
Let us now suppress the dependence on
\pi_{\textup{eig}}
for notational convenience.
Using the definition of the eigenvector
\boldsymbol{v}(i)
and the fact that each vertex has a self-loop, we have that

\lambda(\mathcal{H})\boldsymbol{v}(i)=\boldsymbol{v}(i)+\sum_{j\in\mathcal{N}(i)}\boldsymbol{v}(j)=\boldsymbol{v}(i)+\sum_{j\in\mathcal{N}_{b}(i)}\boldsymbol{v}(j)+\sum_{j\in\mathcal{N}_{a}(i)}\boldsymbol{v}(j)\geq\boldsymbol{v}(i)+\big\lvert\mathcal{N}_{b}(i)\big\rvert\boldsymbol{v}(i)

(23)

The last inequality follows since by definition
\boldsymbol{v}(j)\geq\boldsymbol{v}(i)
for all
j\in\mathcal{N}_{b}(i)
.
From the assumption that
\mathcal{H}
is connected, we have that
\boldsymbol{v}(i)>0
for all
i
so that we can divide both sides of (
23
) with
\boldsymbol{v}(i)
.
Rearranging terms in this way yields that

\big\lvert\mathcal{N}_{b}(i)\big\rvert\leq\lambda(\mathcal{H})-1\kern 5.0pt.

This establishes that if
\mathcal{H}
is connected, then the eigenvector ordering
\pi_{\textup{eig}}
is an importance ordering.

Now, suppose
\mathcal{H}
is not connected.
Let
\mathcal{H}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{H}_{k}
be the connected components, with corresponding eigenvector orderings
\pi_{\textup{eig}}^{(1)},\ldots,\pi_{\textup{eig}}^{(k)}
.
Each
\mathcal{H}_{i}
contains
s_{i}
nodes.
In that case, the eigenvector ordering
\pi_{\textup{eig}}
for the entire graph
\mathcal{H}
is defined as the “concatenation” of these orderings, i.e.

\pi_{\textup{eig}}(i)=\sum_{j=1}^{r_{i-1}}s_{j}+\pi_{\textup{eig}}^{(r_{i})}(i),\text{ where }i\in\mathcal{H}_{r_{i}}

We have already shown that each
\pi_{\textup{eig}}^{(i)}
is an importance ordering for the corresponding
\mathcal{H}_{i}
.
Since there are no edges between components, the defining property of importance ordering is not affected for each component.
In other words, for each node
i
,
\mathcal{N}_{b}(i)
remains the same in
\pi_{\textup{eig}}
and
\pi_{\textup{eig}}^{(r_{i})}
, where
r_{i}
is the component that contains
i
.
This concludes the claim in the general case.
∎

C.5
Sequential Degree Importance Ordering

We present a second construction of an importance ordering based on sequentially removing minimum degree vertices in the conflict graph.
We refer to this construction as the
sequential degree ordering

\pi_{\textup{deg}}
and the formal definition appears in Algorithm
4
.
The ordering is constructed in reverse order over the iterations of the algorithm.
In the first iteration, a vertex of minimum degree in
\mathcal{H}
is placed last in the ordering.
We then remove this minimum degree vertex and its edges from
\mathcal{H}
.
This process repeats so that the vertex of minimum degree is continually added to the lowest available position in the ordering.
Even though this was not stated in terms of importance orderings, the key ideas for the proof can be found in
(
71
)
. We give the details for completeness.

Input:

Conflict graph
\mathcal{H}
.

Output:

Sequential degree ordering
\pi_{\textup{deg}}

Initialize graph
\mathcal{H}^{(1)}=\mathcal{H}

1

Initialize empty ordering
\pi_{\textup{deg}}(i)=\emptyset
for all
i\in[n]

2

for

i=1\dots n

do

3

Let
j
be a minimum degree node in
\mathcal{H}^{(i)}
, breaking ties arbitrarily.

4

Update ordering
\pi_{\textup{deg}}(j)=n-i+1

5

Construct
\mathcal{H}^{(i+1)}
by removing node
j
and its edges from
\mathcal{H}^{(i)}
.

6

Algorithm 4

Sequential-Degree-Ordering

Proposition C.1

(
71
)
.

The sequential degree ordering
\pi_{\textup{deg}}
is an importance ordering.

Proof.

For each
i\in[n]
, we know that it is chosen in order
\pi_{\textup{deg}}(i)
. This means that at the time
i
is selected by the algorithm, there are
n-\pi_{\textup{deg}}(i)
nodes that have already been selected as less important. Let
A_{i}
denote this set of units, and
B_{i}=[n]\setminus A_{i}\setminus\{i\}
. To prove that
\pi_{\textup{deg}}
is an importance ordering, we need to argue that
i
has at most
\lambda(\mathcal{H})-1
neighbors in
B_{i}
. This exactly corresponds to the degree of
i
in the induced subgraph
\mathcal{H}^{n-\pi_{\textup{deg}}(i)+1}
. By definition of Algorithm
4
,
i
is the node of minimum degree in the induced subgraph
\mathcal{H}^{n-\pi_{\textup{deg}}(i)+1}
. We know that the minimum degree in
\mathcal{H}^{n-\pi_{\textup{deg}}(i)+1}
is at most
\lambda(\mathcal{H})^{(i)}-1
, the maximum eigenvalue of the adjacency matrix of the graph
\mathcal{H}^{n-\pi_{\textup{deg}}(i)+1}
(we have to subtract
1
because of self-loops). Since this adjacency matrix is a principal submatrix of
\boldsymbol{A}_{\mathcal{H}}
, by restricting only to rows and columns that lie in
B_{i}\cup\{i\}
, by the Cauchy Interlacing Theorem we have that
\lambda(\mathcal{H})^{(i)}\leq\lambda(\mathcal{H})
. We conclude that the degree of
i
in
\mathcal{H}^{n-\pi_{\textup{deg}}(i)+1}
is at most
\lambda(\mathcal{H})-1
, which completes the proof.
∎

The notion of importance ordering has some interesting connections to techniques from Spectral Graph Theory. In particular, as we observed, the fact that

\pi_{\textup{deg}}
is an importance ordering essentially follows from the proof of Wilf’s Theorem
(
71
)
, which states that the chromatic number of a graph
G
is at most
\lambda(G)+1
, where as usual
\lambda(G)
is the maximum eigenvalue of the adjacency matrix. Indeed, an importance ordering can be used to construct a coloring of the graph. In
71
, a coloring is constructed by iterating over all nodes in the order dictated by
\pi_{\textup{deg}}
, starting from the most important one. For each node
i
, there are at most
\lambda(G)
neighbors that are more important, which have already been colored. Thus, there is always at least one color that
i
can be assigned to, without violating the validity of the coloring.
In this way, the proof that the eigenvector ordering
\pi_{\textup{eig}}
is an importance ordering provides an alternative proof of Wilf’s theorem, which may be of independent interest.

Appendix D
Analysis of Effect Estimator

In this section, we will prove Theorem
4.1
which bounds the finite sample variance of the effect estimator under the Conflict Graph Design.
We restate the theorem below for convenience.

Theorem
4.1
.

Suppose Assumption
1
holds.
Under the Conflict Graph Design, the finite sample variance of the modified Horvitz–Thompson estimator is bounded as

\Varsym\lparen\hat{\tau}\rparen\leq\frac{12.5\cdot\lambda(\mathcal{H})}{n}\cdot\Big\lparen\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}y_{i}(e_{1})^{2}+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}y_{i}(e_{0})^{2}\Big\rparen\kern 5.0pt.

We also restate the Conflict Graph Design here for reference.

See
1

We first present some important Lemmas that will be useful for the proof of Theorem
4.1
. Finally, in Section
D.4
we present the proof of the statement.

D.1
Expression for Covariance Terms

It will be convenient for the analysis of
\Varsym\lparen\hat{\tau}\rparen
to obtain closed form expressions for the terms that appear in the variance.
This is done in the following Lemma.

Lemma D.1
.

Suppose we run the Conflict Graph Design with some importance ordering
\pi
and constant
r>0
with
r\lambda(\mathcal{H})>1
.
For each unit
i\in[n]
and contrastive exposure
k\in\{0,1\}
, the variance of each probability weighted indicator can be expressed as

\Varsym\Big\lparen\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(i,k)}]}{\Pr\lparen E_{(i,k)}\rparen}\Big\rparen=\frac{2r\lambda(\mathcal{H})}{\Big\lparen 1-\frac{1}{r\lambda(\mathcal{H})}\Big\rparen^{|\mathcal{N}_{b}^{\pi}(i)|}}-1\kern 5.0pt.

Furthermore, for any unit
i
we have

\Covsym\Big\lparen\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(i,0)}]}{\Pr\lparen E_{(i,0)}\rparen},\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(i,1)}]}{\Pr\lparen E_{(i,1)}\rparen}\Big\rparen=-1\kern 5.0pt.

For each pair of distinct units
i\neq j\in[n]
and contrastive exposure
k,\ell\in\{0,1\}
, the covariance between probability weighted indicators may be written as:

\displaystyle\Covsym\Big\lparen\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(i,k)}]}{\Pr\lparen E_{(i,k)}\rparen},\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(j,\ell)}]}{\Pr\lparen E_{(j,\ell)}\rparen}\Big\rparen

\displaystyle=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lr}-1&\text{ if }d_{\mathcal{H}}(i,j)=1\\
\Big\lparen 1-\frac{1}{r\lambda(\mathcal{H})}\Big\rparen^{-|\mathcal{N}_{b}^{\pi}(i)\cap\mathcal{N}_{b}^{\pi}(j)|}-1&\text{ if }d_{\mathcal{H}}(i,j)\geq 2\end{array}\right.

Proof.

The proof follows immediately from the expressions of the desired exposure probabilities provided in Lemma
3.3
. In particular, for every unit
i\in[n]
,

\Varsym\Big\lparen\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(i,k)}]}{\Pr\lparen E_{(i,k)}\rparen}\Big\rparen=\frac{1}{\Pr\lparen E_{(i,k)}\rparen}-1=\frac{2r\lambda(\mathcal{H})}{\Big\lparen 1-\frac{1}{r\lambda(\mathcal{H})}\Big\rparen^{|\mathcal{N}_{b}^{\pi}(i)|}}-1\kern 5.0pt.

Since
E_{(i,0)}\cap E_{(i,1)}=\emptyset
, we have that

C_{(i,0),(i,1)}=\Covsym\Big\lparen\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(i,0)}]}{\Pr\lparen E_{(i,0)}\rparen},\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(i,1)}]}{\Pr\lparen E_{(i,1)}\rparen}\Big\rparen=-\Esym\Big[\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(i,0)}]}{\Pr\lparen E_{(i,0)}\rparen}\Big]\Esym\Big[\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(i,1)}]}{\Pr\lparen E_{(i,1)}\rparen}\Big]=-1\kern 5.0pt.

Finally, let
i\neq j
be distinct units. If
d_{\mathcal{H}}(i,j)=1
, then
i
and
j
are adjacent in
\mathcal{H}
, which means by Lemma
3.3
that

\Covsym\Big\lparen\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(i,k)}]}{\Pr\lparen E_{(i,k)}\rparen},\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(j,\ell)}]}{\Pr\lparen E_{(j,\ell)}\rparen}\Big\rparen=-\Esym\Big[\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(i,k)}]}{\Pr\lparen E_{(i,k)}\rparen}\Big]\Esym\Big[\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(j,\ell)}]}{\Pr\lparen E_{(j,\ell)}\rparen}\Big]=-1\kern 5.0pt.

If
d_{\mathcal{H}}(i,j)\geq 2
, then again by Lemma
3.3

\displaystyle\Covsym\Big\lparen\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(i,k)}]}{\Pr\lparen E_{(i,k)}\rparen},\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(j,\ell)}]}{\Pr\lparen E_{(j,\ell)}\rparen}\Big\rparen

\displaystyle=\frac{\Pr\lparen E_{(i,k)}\cap E_{(j,\ell)}\rparen}{\Pr\lparen E_{(i,k)}\rparen\Pr\lparen E_{(j,\ell)}\rparen}-1

\displaystyle=\frac{\Big\lparen\frac{1}{2r\lambda(\mathcal{H})}\Big\rparen^{2}\cdot\Big\lparen 1-\frac{1}{r\lambda(\mathcal{H})}\Big\rparen^{\lvert\mathcal{N}_{b}^{\pi}(i)\cup\mathcal{N}_{b}^{\pi}(j)\rvert}}{\Big\lparen\frac{1}{2r\lambda(\mathcal{H})}\Big\rparen^{2}\cdot\Big\lparen 1-\frac{1}{r\lambda(\mathcal{H})}\Big\rparen^{\lvert\mathcal{N}_{b}^{\pi}(i)\rvert+\lvert\mathcal{N}_{b}^{\pi}(j)\rvert}}-1

\displaystyle=\Big\lparen 1-\frac{1}{r\lambda(\mathcal{H})}\Big\rparen^{-|\mathcal{N}_{b}^{\pi}(i)\cap\mathcal{N}_{b}^{\pi}(j)|}-1\kern 5.0pt.\qed

Lemma
D.1
shows that the covariance between units depends on their distance in the conflict graph
\mathcal{H}
. This will prove useful when bounding the variance, as we will be able to group correlations between nodes according to their distance.

D.2
Bounding the Covariance Terms (Lemma
4.2
)

An important step when bounding
\Varsym\lparen\hat{\tau}\rparen
will be to obtain precise bounds on the covariance terms that appear. An important feature of the Conflict Graph Design is that we can provide such bounds, which will depend on the relative topology of
i
and
j
in the conflict graph.
Indeed, Lemma
4.2
provides a characterization of the covariance terms.
Here, we prove a stronger version of Lemma
4.2
with better constants that depend on the parameter
r
as well. The proof follows by using the expression for the covariance terms provided by Lemma
D.1
, together with several linearization arguments.

Lemma
4.2
*
.

Suppose we run the Conflict Graph Design with some importance ordering
\pi
and constant
r>1
.
For each unit
i\in[n]
and contrastive exposure
k\in\{0,1\}
, the variance of each probability weighted indicator is bounded as

\Varsym\Big\lparen\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(i,k)}]}{\Pr\lparen E_{(i,k)}\rparen}\Big\rparen\leq\frac{2r}{(1-1/(2r))^{2}}\lambda(\mathcal{H})\kern 5.0pt.

For each pair of distinct units
i\neq j\in[n]
and contrastive exposure
k,\ell\in\{0,1\}
, the covariance between probability weighted indicators may be characterized according to their distance in
\mathcal{H}
:

\displaystyle\Covsym\Big\lparen\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(i,k)}]}{\Pr\lparen E_{(i,k)}\rparen},\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(j,\ell)}]}{\Pr\lparen E_{(j,\ell)}\rparen}\Big\rparen

\displaystyle=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lr}-1&\text{ if }d_{\mathcal{H}}(i,j)=1\\
0&\text{ if }d_{\mathcal{H}}(i,j)\geq 3\end{array}\right.

\displaystyle\Bigg\lvert\Covsym\Big\lparen\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(i,k)}]}{\Pr\lparen E_{(i,k)}\rparen},\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(j,\ell)}]}{\Pr\lparen E_{(j,\ell)}\rparen}\Big\rparen\Bigg\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\frac{-2\ln(1-1/(2r))}{(1-1/(2r))^{2}}\cdot\frac{\big\lvert\mathcal{N}(i)\cap\mathcal{N}(j)\big\rvert}{\lambda(\mathcal{H})}\quad\text{for }d_{\mathcal{H}}(i,j)=2\kern 5.0pt.

Proof.

For event
E_{(i,k)}
, by the expression from Lemma
3.3
we have that

\Varsym\Big\lparen\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(i,k)}]}{\Pr\lparen E_{(i,k)}\rparen}\Big\rparen=\frac{1}{\Pr\lparen E_{(i,k)}\rparen}-1\leq\frac{2r\lambda(\mathcal{H})}{\Big\lparen 1-\frac{1}{r\lambda(\mathcal{H})}\Big\rparen^{|\mathcal{N}_{b}^{\pi}(i)|}}\leq\frac{2r\lambda(\mathcal{H})}{\Big\lparen 1-\frac{1}{r\lambda(\mathcal{H})}\Big\rparen^{\lambda(\mathcal{H})}}\kern 5.0pt.

The last inequality follows since
\pi
is an importance ordering and thus
|\mathcal{N}_{b}^{\pi}(i)|\leq\lambda(\mathcal{H})-1<\lambda(\mathcal{H})
.
We distinguish cases according to whether the conflict graph has a non-self edge or not.
If it doesn’t, then the matrix
\boldsymbol{A}_{\mathcal{H}}
is the identity and
\lambda(\mathcal{H})=1
. Then, the variance bound takes the form

\frac{2r}{1-\frac{1}{r}}\lambda(\mathcal{H})\leq\frac{2r}{(1-1/(2r))^{2}}\lambda(\mathcal{H})\kern 5.0pt.

If
\mathcal{H}
contains at least one non-self edge, then clearly
\lambda(\mathcal{H})\geq 2
.
In that case, we show that the following function is decreasing with
\lambda
.

f(\lambda)=\Big\lparen 1-\frac{1}{r\lambda}\Big\rparen^{-\lambda+1}\kern 5.0pt.

Since
\lambda(\mathcal{H})\geq 2
, the first part of the Lemma will follow immediately.
To prove monotonicity, we examine the first derivative.

f^{\prime}(\lambda)=\Big\lparen-\ln\big\lparen 1-1/(r\lambda)\big\rparen-\frac{1}{r\lambda-1}\Big\rparen f(\lambda)<0\kern 5.0pt.

The above follows from the inequality
-\ln(1-1/x)\leq 1/(x-1)
for all
x>1
.

Moving to the second part of the Lemma, for
i,j
with
d_{\mathcal{H}}(i,j)=1
the claim has already been established in Lemma
D.1
. For
i,j
with
d_{\mathcal{H}}(i,j)\geq 2
, the claim is non-trivial only if
\mathcal{H}
contains at least one non-self edge. Thus, in the sequel we assume that is the case, which implies in particular that
\lambda(\mathcal{H})\geq 2
. We would like to prove that for a suitable constant
C=C(r)>0

\displaystyle\Covsym\Big\lparen\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(i,k)}]}{\Pr\lparen E_{(i,k)}\rparen},\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(j,\ell)}]}{\Pr\lparen E_{(j,\ell)}\rparen}\Big\rparen=\Big\lparen 1-\frac{1}{r\lambda(\mathcal{H})}\Big\rparen^{-|\mathcal{N}_{b}^{\pi}(i)\cap\mathcal{N}_{b}^{\pi}(j)|}-1\leq C(r)\frac{|\mathcal{N}_{b}^{\pi}(i)\cap\mathcal{N}_{b}^{\pi}(j)|}{\lambda(\mathcal{H})}\,,

where the first equality comes from Lemma
D.1
.
Consider the function

g(x)=\Big\lparen 1-\frac{1}{r\lambda(\mathcal{H})}\Big\rparen^{-x}-1-C(r)\frac{x}{\lambda(\mathcal{H})}\kern 5.0pt.

We will show that for all
0<x<\lambda(\mathcal{H})
we have
g(x)\leq 0
for a suitable constant
C(r)
. Then the result would follow, since

|\mathcal{N}_{b}^{\pi}(i)\cap\mathcal{N}_{b}^{\pi}(j)|\leq\lambda(\mathcal{H})-1<\lambda(\mathcal{H})
(since
\pi
is an importance ordering).
Again by taking derivative with respect to
x
, we get

g^{\prime}(x)=-\ln\Big\lparen 1-\frac{1}{r\lambda(\mathcal{H})}\Big\rparen\Big\lparen 1-\frac{1}{r\lambda(\mathcal{H})}\Big\rparen^{-x}-\frac{C(r)}{\lambda(\mathcal{H})}\kern 5.0pt.

Since
x\leq\lambda(\mathcal{H})
, in order to have
g(x)\leq 0
for all
x
, it suffices to pick a constant
C(r)
such that

-\lambda(\mathcal{H})\ln\Big\lparen 1-\frac{1}{r\lambda(\mathcal{H})}\Big\rparen\Big\lparen 1-\frac{1}{r\lambda(\mathcal{H})}\Big\rparen^{-\lambda(\mathcal{H})}\leq C(r)\kern 5.0pt.

Indeed, the left hand side is bounded by a constant for all
\lambda(\mathcal{H})\geq 2
, and it attains it’s maximum value when
\lambda(\mathcal{H})=2
. Thus, it suffices to pick

C(r)=\frac{-2\ln(1-1/(2r))}{(1-1/(2r))^{2}}\kern 5.0pt,

and the proof is complete by noticing that
|\mathcal{N}_{b}^{\pi}(i)\cap\mathcal{N}_{b}^{\pi}(j)|\leq|\mathcal{N}(i)\cap\mathcal{N}(j)|
.
∎

D.3
Linear Algebra Preliminaries

Our approach to bounding the variance relies on the operator norm approach.
As such, we will need to bound the largest eigenvalue of a block matrix in terms of the eigenvalues of the blocks.
We now state and prove two lemmas that allows us to do so.
Both will be useful once we break down the variance in different terms.

Lemma D.2
.

Let
\boldsymbol{D}^{(1)},\boldsymbol{D}^{(2)},\boldsymbol{D}^{(3)}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
be diagonal matrices.
Then, the operator norm of the block matrix

\begin{pmatrix}\boldsymbol{D}^{(1)}&\boldsymbol{D}^{(3)}\\
\boldsymbol{D}^{(3)}&\boldsymbol{D}^{(2)}\end{pmatrix}

is at most
\max_{i\in[n]}(\max(|\boldsymbol{D}^{(1)}_{ii}|,|\boldsymbol{D}^{(2)}_{ii}|))+\max_{i\in[n]}|\boldsymbol{D}^{(3)}_{ii}|
.

Proof.

We can write the block matrix as a sum

\begin{pmatrix}\boldsymbol{D}^{(1)}&\boldsymbol{D}^{(3)}\\
\boldsymbol{D}^{(3)}&\boldsymbol{D}^{(2)}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\boldsymbol{D}^{(1)}&0\\
0&\boldsymbol{D}^{(2)}\end{pmatrix}+\begin{pmatrix}0&\boldsymbol{D}^{(3)}\\
\boldsymbol{D}^{(3)}&0\end{pmatrix}

Clearly, the operator norm of the first matrix is
\max_{i\in[n]}(\max(|\boldsymbol{D}^{(1)}_{ii}|,|\boldsymbol{D}^{(2)}_{ii}|))
and of the second matrix
\max_{i\in[n]}|\boldsymbol{D}^{(3)}_{ii}|
. The result follows from the subadditivity of operator norm.
∎

Lemma D.3
.

Suppose
\boldsymbol{S}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
is a symmetric matrix and
\boldsymbol{S}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{2n\times 2n}
is the following block matrix

\boldsymbol{S}^{\prime}=\begin{pmatrix}\boldsymbol{S}&-\boldsymbol{S}\\
-\boldsymbol{S}&\boldsymbol{S}\end{pmatrix}

Then

\lVert\boldsymbol{S}^{\prime}\rVert=2\lVert\boldsymbol{S}\rVert

Proof.

Since
\boldsymbol{S}
is symmetric, let
\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{n}
be the eigenvalues of
\boldsymbol{S}
with corresponding eigenvectors
\mathbf{x}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. We can easily find the eigendecomposition of
\boldsymbol{S}^{\prime}
based on the one of
\boldsymbol{S}
. Indeed, for each
\mathbf{x}_{i}
, consider the vector
\mathbf{y}_{i}=(\mathbf{x}_{i},\mathbf{x}_{i})\in\mathbb{R}^{2n}
. We can check that
\mathbf{y}_{i}
is an eigenvector of
\boldsymbol{S}^{\prime}
with eigenvalue
0
.
Also, let’s consider the vector
\mathbf{y}^{\prime}_{i}=(\mathbf{x}_{i},-\mathbf{x}_{i})
. Then, we can check that
\mathbf{y}^{\prime}_{i}
is an eigenvector of
\boldsymbol{S}^{\prime}
with eigenvalue
2\lambda_{i}
. Also, all these vectors are pairwise orthogonal. Thus, the spectrum of
\boldsymbol{S}^{\prime}
is the spectrum of
\boldsymbol{S}
multiplied by
2
and with an added eigenvalue
0
of multiplicity
n
. The result follows.
∎

D.4
Finite Sample Variance Analysis (Theorem
4.1
)

We are now ready to prove Theorem
4.1
, which constitutes the main result of the paper.

Proof of Theorem
4.1
.

To begin our analysis, let us first decompose the modified Horvitz–Thompson estimator.
Observe that the estimator may be written as the average of individual treatment effect estimators,

\hat{\tau}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\hat{\tau}_{i}\quad\text{where}\quad\hat{\tau}_{i}=y_{i}(e_{1})\cdot\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(i,1)}]}{\Pr\lparen E_{(i,1)}\rparen}-y_{i}(e_{0})\cdot\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(i,0)}]}{\Pr\lparen E_{(i,0)}\rparen}\kern 5.0pt,

where we have used the arbitrary neighborhood exposure assumption (Assumption
1
), as observed in the proof of Proposition
3.5
, to write the estimator in terms of the (deterministic) potential outcomes
y_{i}(e_{1})
and
y_{i}(e_{0})
.
The only random parts of the estimator are therefore the inverse probability weighted indicators.
Thus, the variance of the estimator may be decomposed as the sum of individual covariance terms of the individual inverse probability weighted indicators:

n\cdot\Varsym\lparen\hat{\tau}\rparen=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\{0,1\}\\
\ell\in\{0,1\}\end{subarray}}(-1)^{k-\ell}y_{i}(e_{k})y_{j}(e_{\ell})\Covsym\Bigg\lparen\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(i,k)}]}{\Pr\lparen E_{(i,k)}\rparen},\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(j,\ell)}]}{\Pr\lparen E_{(j,\ell)}\rparen}\Bigg\rparen\kern 5.0pt.

(24)

In order to analyze the variance, we must characterize these covariance terms.
Throughout this section, we shall use the shorthand that for a pair of units
i,j\in[n]
and contrastive exposures
k,\ell\in\{0,1\}
, the covariance terms are denoted as

C_{(i,k),(j,\ell)}\triangleq\Covsym\Big\lparen\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(i,k)}]}{\Pr\lparen E_{(i,k)}\rparen},\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(j,\ell)}]}{\Pr\lparen E_{(j,\ell)}\rparen}\Big\rparen\kern 5.0pt.

A crucial observation here is that expression (
24
) can be seen as a quadratic form of the potential outcomes.
In particular, let us define
\boldsymbol{\alpha}\in\mathbb{R}^{2n}
to be the vector that contains all
2n
relevant potential outcomes that determine the effect
\tau
, as follows:

\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{i}=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}y_{i}(e_{0})&\text{if }0\leq i\leq n,\\
\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}y_{i-n}(e_{1})&\text{if }n+1\leq i\leq 2n.\end{cases}

(25)

We define matrices
\boldsymbol{V}^{(kl)}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
for
k,l\in\{0,1\}
as

\boldsymbol{V}_{ij}^{(kl)}=(-1)^{k+l}C_{(i,k),(j,l)}\kern 5.0pt.

(26)

Then, (
24
) can also be written in the following matrix form:

\Varsym\lparen\tau\rparen=\frac{1}{n}\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\top}\underbrace{\begin{pmatrix}\boldsymbol{V}^{(00)}&\boldsymbol{V}^{(01)}\\
\boldsymbol{V}^{(10)}&\boldsymbol{V}^{(11)}\end{pmatrix}}_{\boldsymbol{V}}\boldsymbol{\alpha}\kern 5.0pt.

(27)

We now notice that

\lVert\boldsymbol{\alpha}\rVert_{2}^{2}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}y_{i}(e_{1})^{2}+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}y_{i}(e_{0})^{2}\kern 5.0pt.

Thus, we have that

\Varsym\lparen\hat{\tau}\rparen=\frac{1}{n}\boldsymbol{\alpha}^{\top}\boldsymbol{V}\boldsymbol{\alpha}\leq\frac{1}{n}\cdot\sup_{\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}}\frac{\boldsymbol{x}^{\top}\boldsymbol{V}\boldsymbol{x}}{\lVert\boldsymbol{x}\rVert_{2}^{2}}\cdot\lVert\boldsymbol{\alpha}\rVert_{2}^{2}=\frac{1}{n}\cdot\lVert\boldsymbol{V}\rVert\cdot\Bigg\lparen\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}y_{i}(e_{1})^{2}+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}y_{i}(e_{0})^{2}\Bigg\rparen\kern 5.0pt.

(28)

In the above,
\lVert\boldsymbol{V}\rVert
is the operator norm of
\boldsymbol{V}
.
Because
\boldsymbol{V}
is symmetric and positive semidefinite, the operator norm is equal to the largest eigenvalue of
\boldsymbol{V}
, denoted by
\lambda_{\textrm{max}}(\boldsymbol{V})
.
Thus, proving Theorem
4.1
is equivalent to bounding
\lambda_{\textrm{max}}(\boldsymbol{V})
by a multiple of
\lambda(\mathcal{H})
.
We focus on this task in the remainder of the proof.

The strategy we will employ will be to split
\boldsymbol{V}
into a sum of matrices. Each matrix has entries only for pairs
i,j
of nodes at a particular distance
0
,
1
or
2
away in the graph. This is justified by Lemma
D.1
, which shows that the values of the entries depend on the distance of the nodes in the conflict graph
\mathcal{H}
. Notice that if
d_{\mathcal{H}}(i,j)\geq 3
, then the events
E_{(i,k)},E_{(j,\ell)}
are independent, so
C_{(i,k),(j,\ell)}=0
.
This greatly simplifies the task of bounding the variance.
Hence, we split each matrix
\boldsymbol{V}^{(kl)}
into the sum of three matrices, which contain the entries of
\boldsymbol{V}
for pairs of units at distance
0,1
or
2
away. According to the previous observations, we can write

\boldsymbol{V}=\underbrace{\begin{pmatrix}\boldsymbol{T}^{(0)}&-I\\
-I&T^{0}\end{pmatrix}}_{\boldsymbol{R}^{(0)}}+\underbrace{\begin{pmatrix}\boldsymbol{T}^{(1)}&-\boldsymbol{T}^{(1)}\\
-\boldsymbol{T}^{(1)}&\boldsymbol{T}^{(1)}\end{pmatrix}}_{\boldsymbol{R}^{(1)}}+\underbrace{\begin{pmatrix}\boldsymbol{T}^{(2)}&-\boldsymbol{T}^{(2)}\\
-\boldsymbol{T}^{(2)}&\boldsymbol{T}^{(2)}\end{pmatrix}}_{\boldsymbol{R}^{(2)}}

(29)

In the above, we have defined for
k\in\{0,1,2\}

\boldsymbol{T}^{(k)}_{ij}=\begin{cases}C_{ij}&\text{if, }d_{\mathcal{H}}(i,j)=k\\
0&\text{if, }d_{\mathcal{H}}(i,j)\neq k\end{cases}\quad

The above means
\boldsymbol{T}^{(k)}
contains only entries for pairs of nodes at distance
k
.
By the subadditivity of the operator norm, we have

\lambda_{\textrm{max}}(R)\leq\lambda_{\textrm{max}}(\boldsymbol{R}^{(0)})+\lambda_{\textrm{max}}(\boldsymbol{R}^{(1)})+\lambda_{\textrm{max}}(\boldsymbol{R}^{(2)})

(30)

We now proceed to bound the operator norms of
\boldsymbol{R}^{(0)},\boldsymbol{R}^{(1)}
and
\boldsymbol{R}^{(2)}
, which will result in our final bound of the variance.
In order to bound the operator norm of these three matrices, we need to establish bounds for the entries
C_{(i,k),(j,l)}
that are contained in these three matrices.
These are established in Lemma
4.2
*.
With these bounds in hand, we are ready to analyze the finite sample variance of the estimator under the design.

For
\boldsymbol{R}^{(0)}
, we notice that all four sub-blocks are diagonal matrices. Thus, by simple linear algebra, the maximum eigenvalue of
\boldsymbol{R}^{(0)}
can be bounded by the maximum element of these diagonal matrices.
Indeed, we can apply Lemma
D.2
with
\boldsymbol{D}^{(1)}=\boldsymbol{D}^{(2)}=\boldsymbol{T}^{0}
,
\boldsymbol{D}^{(3)}=-\boldsymbol{I}
, which gives

\lVert\boldsymbol{R}^{(0)}\rVert\leq 1+\max\big\lparen\max_{i\in[n]}(|C_{(i,0),(i,0)}|)\big\rparen\leq 1+\frac{2r}{(1-1/(2r))^{2}}\lambda(\mathcal{H})\kern 5.0pt.

In the last inequality we used Lemma
4.2
*.
Notice that the bound in
C_{ii}
comes from
lower bounding the probabilities
\Pr\lparen E_{i}^{k}\rparen
for all
i\in[n]
and
k\in\{0,1\}
. Intuitively, this means that all units receive the
desired exposures with high-enough probability, and this indeed shows up in the variance calculation.

Let us now handle correlations between nodes that are distance
1
away in
\mathcal{H}
. We notice that
\boldsymbol{T}^{(1)}
is equal to the negative adjacency matrix
\boldsymbol{A}_{\mathcal{H}}
of the conflict graph
\mathcal{H}
, meaning
\boldsymbol{T}^{(1)}=-\boldsymbol{A}_{\mathcal{H}}
.
Thus, the eigenvalues of
\boldsymbol{T}^{(1)}
are in 1-1 correspondence to the ones of
A_{\mathcal{H}}
with just a sign reversal.
Some simple linear algebra on the block matrix can now be used to relate the maximum eigenvalue of
\boldsymbol{R}^{(1)}
with that of
\boldsymbol{T}^{(1)}
.
Indeed, we can apply Lemma
D.3
with
S=\boldsymbol{T}^{(1)}
, which will give us

\lambda_{\textrm{max}}(\boldsymbol{R}^{(1)})=2\lambda_{\textrm{max}}(-A_{\mathcal{H}})\leq 2\lambda(\mathcal{H})\kern 5.0pt.

In the last inequality, we used the fact that
A_{\mathcal{H}}
is an adjacency matrix, and its largest in absolute value eigenvalue is equal to its largest eigenvalue
\lambda(\mathcal{H})

(
62
)
.

Finally, let’s bound the operator norm of
\boldsymbol{R}^{(2)}
, which contains pairs of nodes that are at distance
2
away in
H
. We will try to relate the entries of
\boldsymbol{T}^{(2)}
to the number of paths of length
2
between two nodes.
Indeed, by Lemma
4.2
*,

\boldsymbol{T}^{(2)}_{ij}\leq\frac{-2\ln(1-1/(2r))}{(1-1/(2r))^{2}}\frac{|\mathcal{N}_{b}^{\pi}(i)\cap\mathcal{N}_{b}^{\pi}(j)|}{\lambda(\mathcal{H})}\leq\frac{|\mathcal{N}^{\pi}(i)\cap\mathcal{N}^{\pi}(j)|}{\lambda(\mathcal{H})}\kern 5.0pt.

Now, notice that for any pair of nodes
i,j
that are at distance
2
in
\mathcal{H}
, the number of paths between
i
and
j
of length
2
is exactly

|\mathcal{N}^{\pi}(i)\cap\mathcal{N}^{\pi}(j)|
.
On the other hand, we know that the matrix
\boldsymbol{A}_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}
contains for every pair
i,j
the number of paths between
i
and
j
of length
2
.
Thus, for any
i,j
, we have established the entrywise bound

\boldsymbol{T}^{(2)}_{ij}\leq\frac{-2\ln(1-1/(2r))}{(1-1/(2r))^{2}}\frac{1}{\lambda(\mathcal{H})}(\boldsymbol{A}_{\mathcal{H}}^{2})_{ij}\kern 5.0pt.

Since both
\boldsymbol{T}^{(2)}
and
\boldsymbol{A}_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}
are symmetric matrices of nonnegative entries, the Peron-Frobenius Theorem implies monotonicity of the maximum eigenvalue
(
62
)
, from which we conclude that

\displaystyle\lambda_{\textrm{max}}(\boldsymbol{T}^{(2)})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{-2\ln(1-1/(2r))}{(1-1/(2r))^{2}}\frac{\lambda_{\textrm{max}}(A_{\mathcal{H}}^{2})}{\lambda(\mathcal{H})}

\displaystyle=\frac{-2\ln(1-1/(2r))}{(1-1/(2r))^{2}}\frac{\lambda(\mathcal{H})^{2}}{\lambda(\mathcal{H})}

\displaystyle=\frac{-2\ln(1-1/(2r))}{(1-1/(2r))^{2}}\lambda(\mathcal{H})

We can now apply Lemma
D.3
with
S=\boldsymbol{T}^{(2)}
, which yields

\lambda_{\textrm{max}}(\boldsymbol{R}^{(2)})\leq 2\frac{-2\ln(1-1/(2r))}{(1-1/(2r))^{2}}\lambda(\mathcal{H})

Combining the above bounds and using (
30
), we obtain that whenever
\lambda(\mathcal{H})\geq 2
,

\lambda_{\textrm{max}}(R)\leq\Big\lparen\frac{2r}{(1-1/(2r))^{2}}+3+2\frac{-2\ln(1-1/(2r))}{(1-1/(2r))^{2}}\Big\rparen\lambda(\mathcal{H})

Observe that for any
r\in[1.8,2.8]
, this constant is at most
12.5
, which is why we choose
r=2
in the statement of the algorithm.
Moreover, we can optimize the choice of
r>0
to obtain the best constant.
Standard calculations show that this expression is minimized when
r\approx 2.19
and the bound we obtain its
\lambda_{\textrm{max}}(R)\leq 12.08\cdot\lambda(\mathcal{H})
.
∎

D.5
Consistency of Effect Estimator (Theorem
4.3
)

Theorem
4.3
.

Suppose Assumptions
1
and
2
hold.
Under the Conflict Graph Design, the modified Horvitz–Thompson estimator converges in mean square at a
\sqrt{\lambda(\mathcal{H})/n}
-rate:

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\sqrt{\frac{n}{\lambda(\mathcal{H})}}\cdot\Esym\Big[\big\lparen\tau-\hat{\tau}\big\rparen^{2}\Big]^{1/2}<\infty\kern 5.0pt.

Proof.

We suppress the dependence of the sequence of potential outcomes on the index
n
for simplicity.
The proof follows from applying the finite result of Theorem
4.1
.
In particular, since
\hat{\tau}
is unbiased, we have

\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{n}{\lambda(\mathcal{H})}}\cdot\Esym\Big[\big\lparen\tau-\hat{\tau}\big\rparen^{2}\Big]^{1/2}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{n}{\lambda(\mathcal{H})}}\cdot\sqrt{\Varsym\lparen\hat{\tau}\rparen}

\displaystyle\leq 12.08\sqrt{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}y_{i}(e_{0})^{2}+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}y_{i}(e_{1})^{2}}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}\lparen 1\rparen\,,

where in the last step we used Assumption
2
. Taking limits as
n\to\infty
finishes the proof.
∎

D.6
Slow rate example for Standard Horvitz–Thompson Estimator (Theorem
4.5
)

Theorem
4.5
.

Consider the direct treatment effect
\tau
, as defined in Section
2.3
.
There is a sequence
G^{(n)}
of graphs with a triangular sequence
y_{i}^{(n)}
of potential outcome functions satisfying Assumptions
1
-
3
, such that the following holds under the Conflict Graph Design:

\limsup_{n\to\infty}n^{1/4}\cdot\Esym\Big[\big\lparen\tau-\hat{\tau}\big\rparen^{2}\Big]^{1/2}<\infty\quad\text{while}\quad 0<\liminf_{n\to\infty}\sqrt{\log n}\cdot\Esym\Big[\big\lparen\tau-\hat{\tau}_{\textrm{standard}}\big\rparen^{2}\Big]^{1/2}\kern 5.0pt.

Proof.

In the remainder of this Section, we set
r=1
for simplicity in the Conflict Graph Design, since we are not interested in the constants but on rates of convergence.
We start by defining the sequence of graphs
G^{(n)}=(V_{n},E_{n})
.
For every
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we define
V_{n}=\{1\}\cup K_{n}\cup R_{n}
, where
K_{n}
contains
m_{n}=\sqrt{n}/\log n
nodes. Also,
R_{n}
contains
n
nodes. We enumerate the nodes
K_{n}=\{k_{1},\ldots,k_{m_{n}}\}
and
R_{n}=\{r_{1},\ldots,r_{n}\}
. We assume without loss of generality that
n
is a power of
2
, otherwise we can modify the construction by replacing
\sqrt{n}
with
\lceil\sqrt{n}\rceil
and
\log n
with
\lceil\log n\rceil
, without affecting the asymptotics.
Also, notice that overall, we have
n+\sqrt{n}/\log n+1
nodes instead of
n
.
However, since this is
n(1+o(1))
, it will not change the asymptotics of the rates, so we keep the same normalization
1/n
for both estimators
\hat{\tau}
and
\hat{\tau}_{\textrm{standard}}
.

\displaystyle 1
\displaystyle K_{n}
\displaystyle R_{n}
\displaystyle t_{n}
\displaystyle\Bigr\}n
nodes
\displaystyle\Bigr\}m_{n}
nodes

Figure 18
:

Graph
G^{(n)}
that demonstrates a gap between the variance of the modified and the standard Horvitz-Thompson estimators.
Directions of edges indicate the eigenvalue ordering.

We now describe the set of edges
E_{n}
. First of all, there is an edge
(1,r_{i})
between
1
and each of the nodes
r_{i}\in R_{n}
, for
i=1,\ldots,n
.
Also,
R_{n}
is divided in subsets of size
t_{n}=\sqrt{n}\log n=n/m_{n}
each. We have a total of
m_{n}
subsets , let
R_{n}^{(i)}
be the
i
-th subset. We have an edge
(k_{i},r_{j})
for any
i\in[m_{n}]
and
j\in R_{n}^{(i)}
.
We can see pictorially how
G^{(n)}
would look like in Figure
18
.

We focus on the Average Direct Treatment Effect. As we have seen in Section
3.1
, in that case the conflict graph
\mathcal{H}
will be
G^{(n)}
itself, with added self loops for all nodes. Thus, we focus on analyzing the spectral properties of
G^{(n)}
for the remainder of this section. We start by determining the eigenvalue ordering
\pi_{\textup{eig}}
induced by the graph
G^{(n)}
. Since self loops only add an identity matrix to the adjacency, we can analyze the adjacency matrix
A_{n}
of
G^{(n)}
without self loops for simplicity.
Let
\lambda(\mathcal{H})
be the largest eigenvalue of
A_{n}
. To determine the eigenvector
\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
corresponding to
\lambda(\mathcal{H})
, we first notice that due to symmetry, all nodes in
K_{n}
will have the same value in the eigenvector and all nodes in
R_{n}
will also have the same value.
Let
p_{1}
be the entry of
\boldsymbol{v}
for node
1
,
p_{2}
be the entry for nodes in
K_{n}
and
p_{3}
the entry for nodes in
R_{n}
.
Our goal is to solve the linear system

A_{n}\boldsymbol{v}=\lambda(\mathcal{H})\boldsymbol{v}\kern 5.0pt.

Because of symmetry, this system reduces to the following
3
equations

\displaystyle np_{3}

\displaystyle=\lambda(\mathcal{H})p_{1}

\displaystyle t_{n}p_{3}

\displaystyle=\lambda(\mathcal{H})p_{2}

\displaystyle p_{1}+p2

\displaystyle=\lambda(\mathcal{H})p_{3}\kern 5.0pt.

Solving these gives us

\displaystyle p_{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{t_{n}}{n}p_{1}

\displaystyle p_{3}

\displaystyle=\frac{\lambda(\mathcal{H})}{n}p_{1}

\displaystyle\lambda(\mathcal{H})p_{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{n}{\lambda(\mathcal{H})}\big\lparen 1+\frac{t_{n}}{n}\big\rparen p_{1}\kern 5.0pt.

Since the graph is connected,
p_{1}>0
and hence the third equation gives us
\lambda(\mathcal{H})=\sqrt{n(1+t/n)}=\Theta(\sqrt{n})
, since
t_{n}=o(n)
by construction. Thus,
\lambda(\mathcal{H})=o(n)
, so Assumption
3
on the sublinear growth of the largest eigenvalue is satisfied. We also get
p_{2}=(t_{n}/n)p_{1}=(\log n/\sqrt{n})p_{1}
and
p_{3}=(\lambda(\mathcal{H})/n)p_{1}=\Theta(1/\sqrt{n})p_{1}
. Thus, for sufficiently large
n
we have
p_{1}>p_{2}>p_{3}
. This means that in
\pi_{\textup{eig}}
node
1
is the earliest, followed by all nodes in
K_{n}
and then all nodes on
R_{n}
. Thus, we have that for all
k_{i}\in K_{n}
,
\mathcal{N}_{b}^{\pi}(k_{i})=\emptyset
. Also, for each
r_{j}\in R_{n}^{(i)}
we have
\mathcal{N}_{b}^{\pi}(k_{i})=\{k_{i}\}
. Thus, the children of
1
are all nodes in
R_{n}
and the children of each
k_{i}\in K_{n}
are the nodes in
R_{n}^{(i)}
.

Now, let us define the outcome sequence. In particular, we define the following outcomes
