y_{i}^{(n)}(e_{0})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}0&i\notin K_{n}\\
\sqrt{t_{n}}&i\in K_{n}\\
\end{array}\right.\quad\quad y_{i}^{(n)}(e_{1})=0,\forall i\kern 5.0pt.

Intuitively, we only assign non-trivial outcomes on nodes in
K_{n}
, since we are interested in capturing the correlations between them. We see later that this is enough to make the variance of
\hat{\tau}_{\textrm{standard}}
blow up.
Notice that Assumption
2
is satisfied, since

\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i\in K_{n}}(y_{i}^{(n)}(e_{0}))^{2}=\frac{m_{n}t_{n}}{n}=1\kern 5.0pt.

We can thus apply Theorem
4.3
for
\hat{\tau}
to conclude that

\limsup_{n\to\infty}n^{1/4}\cdot\Esym\Big[\big\lparen\tau-\hat{\tau}\big\rparen^{2}\Big]^{1/2}<\infty\kern 5.0pt.

We now focus on the performance of
\hat{\tau}_{\textrm{standard}}
.
To do that, we focus on the nodes
k_{i}\in K_{n}
,
i=1,\ldots,m_{n}
.
We start by calculating the exposure probabilities for these nodes, i.e. the probability
\Pr\lparen d_{k_{i}}(Z)=d_{k_{i}}(\boldsymbol{z}_{i,(0)})\rparen
.
Recall that the standard Horvitz–Thompson uses these proper exposure probabilities rather than the desired exposure probabilities
\Pr\lparen E_{(i,k)}\rparen
used by the modified Horvitz–Thompson estimator.
Let’s analyze the possible outcomes that would lead to
d_{k_{i}}(Z)=d_{k_{i}}(\boldsymbol{z}_{i,(0)})
. This event can be written equivalently as
\{Z_{k_{i}}=0,Z_{r_{j}}=0,\forall r_{j}\in R_{n}^{(i)}\}
.
We now analyze all possible assignments of the
U_{i}
variables that lead to that exposure.

•

U_{k_{i}}=e_{0}
. In that case,
k_{i}
has the desired exposure of all control, and since it has no parents, it will receive this exposure.

•

U_{k_{i}}=*\land U_{1}\neq*
. This corresponds to the case where at least one of the parents of the nodes in
R_{n}^{(i)}
has a desired exposure that is not
*
. In that case,
the nodes in
R_{n}^{(i)}

yield
to the desires of one of their parents, whose desired exposures require all the nodes in
R_{n}^{(i)}
to remain in control.

•

U_{k_{i}}=U_{1}=*,U_{r_{j}}\neq e_{1}\forall r_{j}\in R_{n}^{(i)}
. This corresponds to the case where both parents do not have a desired exposure, meaning that they allow nodes in
R_{n}^{(i)}
to receive their desired exposures. This means that if at least one node in
R_{n}^{(i)}
has
U_{r_{j}}=e_{1}
, then it “desires” to receive the direct treatment exposure, leading to
Z_{r_{j}}=1
. Thus, in order for
k_{i}
to receive the all control exposure, this should not happen for any node in
R_{n}^{(i)}
.

The three events discussed above are pairwise disjoint.
Thus, they help us write the exposure probabilities in the following nice form.

\displaystyle\Pr\lparen d_{k_{i}}(Z)=d_{k_{i}}(z_{i}^{(1)})\rparen

\displaystyle=\Pr\lparen U_{k_{i}}=e_{0}\rparen+\Pr\lparen U_{k_{i}}=*,U_{1}\neq*\rparen

\displaystyle\quad+\Pr\lparen U_{k_{i}}=U_{1}=*,U_{r_{j}}\neq e_{1}\forall r_{j}\in R_{n}^{(i)}\rparen

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\lambda(\mathcal{H})}+\Big\lparen 1-\frac{1}{\lambda(\mathcal{H})}\Big\rparen\frac{1}{\lambda(\mathcal{H})}+\Big\lparen 1-\frac{1}{\lambda(\mathcal{H})}\Big\rparen^{2}\Big\lparen 1-\frac{1}{2\lambda(\mathcal{H})}\Big\rparen^{t_{n}}

\displaystyle=\Theta\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\right)+\Theta\left(\left(1-\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\right)^{\sqrt{n}\log n}\right)

\displaystyle=\Theta\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\right)\kern 5.0pt,

since the second term is
\mathcal{O}\lparen 1/n\rparen
.

The next step would be to calculate the covariance between two different units
k_{i},k_{s}\in K_{n}
.
In particular, the following term will appear in the expanded form of the variance of
\hat{\tau}_{\textrm{standard}}

\displaystyle D_{(k_{i},0),(k_{s},0)}

\displaystyle:=\Covsym\Bigg\lparen\frac{\mathbf{1}[d_{k_{i}}(Z)=d_{k_{i}}(\boldsymbol{z}_{i,(0)})]}{\Pr\Big\lparen d_{k_{i}}(Z)=d_{k_{i}}(\boldsymbol{z}_{k_{i},(0)})\Big\rparen},\frac{\mathbf{1}[d_{k_{s}}(Z)=d_{k_{s}}(\boldsymbol{z}_{i,(0)})]}{\Pr\Big\lparen d_{k_{s}}(Z)=d_{k_{s}}(\boldsymbol{z}_{k_{s},(0)})\Big\rparen}\Bigg\rparen

\displaystyle=\frac{\Pr\Big\lparen d_{k_{i}}(Z)=d_{k_{i}}(\boldsymbol{z}_{k_{i},(0)}),d_{k_{s}}(Z)=d_{k_{s}}(\boldsymbol{z}_{k_{s},(0)})\Big\rparen}{\Pr\Big\lparen d_{k_{i}}(Z)=d_{k_{i}}(\boldsymbol{z}_{k_{i},(0)})\Big\rparen\Pr\Big\lparen d_{k_{s}}(Z)=d_{k_{s}}(\boldsymbol{z}_{k_{s},(0)})\Big\rparen}-1\kern 5.0pt.

To compute the probability in the numerator, let us analyze all ways in which the following event occurs

\left\{d_{k_{i}}(Z)=d_{k_{i}}(\boldsymbol{z}_{k_{i},(0)}),d_{k_{s}}(Z)=d_{k_{s}}(\boldsymbol{z}_{k_{s},(0)})\right\}=\left\{Z_{k_{i}}=Z_{k_{s}}=0,Z_{r}=0,\forall r\in R_{n}^{(i)}\cup R_{n}^{(s)}\right\}\kern 5.0pt.

This event can be broken down in the following disjoint events.

•

U_{1}\neq*,U_{k_{i}}\neq e_{1},U_{k_{s}}\neq e_{1}
. In that case, all children of
k_{i},k_{s}
will receive control in the final assignment, since they are “yielding” to the exposure of their parent
1
. Thus, this event happens with probability

\frac{1}{\lambda(\mathcal{H})}\Big\lparen 1-\frac{1}{2\lambda(\mathcal{H})}\Big\rparen^{2}=\Theta\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\right)\kern 5.0pt.

•

U_{1}=*
. In that case, the events
d_{k_{i}}(Z)=d_{k_{i}}(\boldsymbol{z}_{k_{i},(0)})
and
d_{k_{s}}(Z)=d_{k_{s}}(\boldsymbol{z}_{k_{s},(0)})
are now independent. This event happens with probability

\displaystyle\Pr\lparen U_{1}=*\rparen

\displaystyle\Pr\big\lparen d_{k_{i}}(Z)=d_{k_{i}}(\boldsymbol{z}_{k_{i},(0)})|U_{1}=*\big\rparen\Pr\big\lparen d_{k_{s}}(Z)=d_{k_{s}}(\boldsymbol{z}_{k_{s},(0)})|U_{1}=*\big\rparen

\displaystyle=\Big\lparen 1-\frac{1}{\lambda(\mathcal{H})}\Big\rparen\Theta\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\right)\Theta\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\right)

\displaystyle=\Theta\left(\frac{1}{n}\right)\kern 5.0pt.

For these conditional probabilities we can use the same case analysis as for calculating the propensity scores above to conclude that each one is
\Theta(1/\sqrt{n})
.

Thus, overall we have that

\Pr\Big\lparen d_{k_{i}}(Z)=d_{k_{i}}(\boldsymbol{z}_{k_{i},(0)}),d_{k_{s}}(Z)=d_{k_{s}}(\boldsymbol{z}_{k_{s},(0)})\Big\rparen=\Theta\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\right)\kern 5.0pt.

Thus,

D_{(k_{i},0),(k_{s},0)}=\frac{\Theta(1/\sqrt{n})}{\Theta(1/\sqrt{n})\Theta(1/\sqrt{n})}-1=\Theta(\sqrt{n})\kern 5.0pt.

This shows that the correlation between nodes
k_{i},k_{s}
will have a large contribution to the variance of
\hat{\tau}_{\textrm{standard}}
.

Indeed, for this choice of potential outcomes, we have for the variance of
\hat{\tau}_{\textrm{standard}}

\displaystyle\Varsym\lparen\hat{\tau}_{\textrm{standard}}\rparen

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{k_{i},k_{s}\in K_{n},k_{i}\neq k_{s}}y_{k_{i}}(e_{0})y_{k_{s}}(e_{0})D_{(k_{i},0),(k_{s},0)}

\displaystyle=\frac{t_{n}{m_{n}\choose 2}}{n^{2}}\Theta(\sqrt{n})

\displaystyle=\Theta\left(\frac{m_{n}}{\sqrt{n}}\right)

\displaystyle=\Theta\left(\frac{1}{\log n}\right)\kern 5.0pt.

The first inequality follows by ignoring the individual variance terms of each unit, which are always nonnegative.
Using that the standard Horvitz–Thompson estimator is unbiased and taking the limit
n\to\infty
, we get

\liminf_{n\to\infty}\sqrt{\log n}\cdot\Esym[\lparen\hat{\tau}_{\textrm{standard}}-\tau\rparen^{2}]^{1/2}>0\kern 5.0pt.\qed

Appendix E
Analysis of Inferential Methods

In this section, we provide proofs for results arising in Section
5
regarding the inferential methods.

E.1
Validity of Inference Assumptions under i.i.d. Outcomes

In this Section, we prove a claim that was made in Section
5
, namely that Assumptions
4
and
5
both hold with with high probability if the outcomes are drawn from a distribution with bounded fourth moments.
To be more precise, let us introduce the following assumption about the generation of potential outcomes.

Assumption 6
.

Suppose that the potential outcomes
\{y_{i}(e_{0}),y_{i}(e_{1})\}_{i=1}^{n}
are sampled i.i.d. from a distribution
\mathcal{D}
over
\mathbb{R}^{2}
with the following properties:

•

Mean Zero
: If
(X,Y)\sim\mathcal{D}
then
\Esym[X]=\Esym[Y]=0
.

•

Fourth Moments
: If
(X,Y)\sim\mathcal{D}
then
\Esym[|X|^{4}],\Esym[|Y|^{4}]<\infty
.

•

Not Perfectly Correlated
: If
(X,Y)\sim\mathcal{D}
then

\rho:=\frac{\Covsym\lparen X,Y\rparen}{\sqrt{\Varsym\lparen X\rparen\Varsym\lparen Y\rparen}}<1\kern 5.0pt.

We give here a useful Lemma that bounds some mixed moments of the distribution
\mathcal{D}
by the fourth moments.

Lemma E.1
.

Let
\mathcal{D}
be a distribution satisfying Assumption
6
.
Define

R\triangleq\max\left(\Esym[|X|^{4}],\Esym[|Y|^{4}]\right)\kern 5.0pt,

where
X,Y\sim\mathcal{D}
.
Then,

•

\Esym[(X^{2}-\Esym[X^{2}])^{2}]\leq R

•

\Esym[(Y^{2}-\Esym[Y^{2}])^{2}]\leq R

•

\Esym[(XY-\Esym[XY])^{2}]\leq R

•

\Esym[X^{2}]\Esym[Y^{2}]\leq R
.

Proof.

Since the variance is always bounded by the second moment, we have

\Esym[(X^{2}-\Esym[X^{2}])^{2}]=\Varsym\lparen X^{2}\rparen\leq\Esym[X^{4}]\leq R\kern 5.0pt.

Similarly, we conclude that
\Esym[(Y^{2}-\Esym[Y^{2}])^{2}]\leq R
.
Observe also that

\Esym[(XY-\Esym[XY])^{2}]=\Varsym\lparen XY\rparen\leq\Esym[X^{2}Y^{2}]\leq\sqrt{\Esym[X^{4}]\Esym[Y^{4}]}\leq R\kern 5.0pt,

where in the second to last step we have used the Cauchy-Schwartz inequality.
By Jensen’s inequality we have that
\Esym[X^{2}]\leq\sqrt{\Esym[X^{4}]}
and
\Esym[Y^{2}]\leq\sqrt{\Esym[Y^{4}]}
.
Using this, we obtain that

\Esym[X^{2}]\Esym[Y^{2}]\leq\sqrt{\Esym[X^{4}]}\cdot\sqrt{\Esym[Y^{4}]}\leq R\kern 5.0pt.\qed

Next, we prove that if outcomes are sampled according to Assumption
6
, then with probability tending to
1
as
n\to\infty
, Assumption
4
holds.

Proposition E.2
.

Suppose Assumption
6
holds.
Then, there exists a constant
M>0
, such that for both
k\in\{0,1\}

\lim_{n\to\infty}\Pr\Bigg\lparen\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}y_{i}(e_{k})^{4}>M\Bigg\rparen=0

Proof.

Let
(X,Y)\sim\mathcal{D}
. We will prove the claim for
k=0
, the proof for
k=1
is identical.
By Assumption
6
, denote
R=\Esym[X^{4}]<\infty
and
consider any constant
M>R
. Then, we have

\displaystyle\Pr\Bigg\lparen\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}y_{i}(e_{k})^{4}>M\Bigg\rparen=\Pr\Bigg\lparen\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}y_{i}(e_{k})^{4}-\Esym\Bigg[\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}y_{i}(e_{k})^{4}\Bigg]>M-R\Bigg\rparen=o(1)\kern 5.0pt,

where the last line follows from the strong Law of Large Numbers
(
18
)
.
∎

Next, we show that if Assumption
6
holds, then the estimator is not superefficient with high probability.
We emphasize that there are two levels of randomness. The first level comes from the randomness of the potential outcomes
y_{i}(e_{k})
.
Conditioned on the values of the potential outcomes, the second level of randomness comes from the design that assigns the randomized treatment vector. To distinguish between the two sources, we will use the notation
\Varsym\lparen\hat{\tau}|y\rparen
to denote the variance of
\hat{\tau}
, after conditioning on the potential outcomes
y_{i}(e_{k})
. Thus, this quantity is only a function of the potential outcomes, so the probability in the statement is with respect to the randomness of the potential outcomes. We use a subscript of
\mathcal{D}
to denote that.

Proposition E.3
.

Suppose Assumption
6
holds.
Then, there exists a constant
M>0
, such that

\lim_{n\to\infty}\Pr_{#1}\Bigg\lparen\Varsym\lparen\hat{\tau}|y\rparen<M\frac{\lambda(\mathcal{H})}{n}\Bigg\rparen=0

Proof.

In the below calculations, the expectation is taken with respect to the randomness of the potential outcomes, as explained above.
Using (
24
), we get that

\displaystyle\Esym_{#1}[\Varsym\lparen\hat{\tau}|y\rparen]

\displaystyle=\Esym_{#1}\Bigg[\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\{0,1\}\\
\ell\in\{0,1\}\end{subarray}}(-1)^{k-\ell}y_{i}(e_{k})y_{j}(e_{\ell})\Covsym\Bigg\lparen\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(i,k)}]}{\Pr\lparen E_{(i,k)}\rparen},\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(j,\ell)}]}{\Pr\lparen E_{(j,\ell)}\rparen}\Bigg\rparen\Bigg]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{k=0}^{1}\Esym[y_{i}(e_{k})^{2}]\Varsym\Bigg\lparen\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(i,k)}]}{\Pr\lparen E_{(i,k)}\rparen}\Bigg\rparen

\displaystyle\quad\quad+\frac{2}{n^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Esym[y_{i}(e_{1})y_{i}(e_{0})]\cdot\Covsym\Bigg\lparen\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(i,0)}]}{\Pr\lparen E_{(i,0)}\rparen},\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(i,1)}]}{\Pr\lparen E_{(i,1)}\rparen}\Bigg\rparen

where we used the fact that
y_{i}(e_{k})
and
y_{j}(e_{\ell})
are independent zero mean if
i\neq j
.
We will first handle the variance terms.
Using Lemma
D.1
together with
r\geq 1
, we obtain that

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{k\in\{0,1\}}\Esym[y_{i}(e_{k})^{2}](2\lambda(\mathcal{H})-1)

\displaystyle quad\quad+\frac{2}{n^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Esym[y_{i}(e_{1})y_{i}(e_{0})]\cdot\Covsym\Bigg\lparen\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(i,0)}]}{\Pr\lparen E_{(i,0)}\rparen},\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(i,1)}]}{\Pr\lparen E_{(i,1)}\rparen}\Bigg\rparen

Next, we analyze the covariance terms.
Recall that these events
E_{i,1}
and
E_{i,0}
are mutually exclusive.
Thus, the covariance terms are
-1
.
Using this, we obtain

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Big\lparen\Esym[y_{i}(e_{0})^{2}](2\lambda(\mathcal{H})-1)+\Esym[y_{i}(e_{1})^{2}](2\lambda(\mathcal{H})-1)-2\Esym[y_{i}(e_{1})y_{i}(e_{0})]\Big\rparen

Using the definition of the correlation
\rho
, we have that

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Big\lparen\Esym[y_{i}(e_{0})^{2}](2\lambda(\mathcal{H})-1)+\Esym[y_{i}(e_{1})^{2}](2\lambda(\mathcal{H})-1)-2\rho\sqrt{\Esym[y_{i}(e_{1})^{2}]\Esym[y_{i}(e_{0})^{2}]}\Big\rparen

Using Cauchy-Schwartz to lower bound this third term and rearranging, we obtain that

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Big\lparen\Esym[y_{i}(e_{0})^{2}](2\lambda(\mathcal{H})-1)+\Esym[y_{i}(e_{1})^{2}](2\lambda(\mathcal{H})-1)-2\rho\lparen\Esym[y_{i}(e_{1})^{2}]+\Esym[y_{i}(e_{0})^{2}]\rparen\Big\rparen

\displaystyle=\frac{\lambda(\mathcal{H})}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Bigg\lparen\Esym[y_{i}(e_{0})^{2}]\Big\lparen 2-\frac{1+\rho}{\lambda(\mathcal{H})}\Big\rparen+\Esym[y_{i}(e_{1})^{2}]\Big\lparen 2-\frac{1+\rho}{\lambda(\mathcal{H})}\Big\rparen\Bigg\rparen

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\lambda(\mathcal{H})}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Bigg\lparen\Esym[y_{i}(e_{0})^{2}](1-\rho)+\Esym[y_{i}(e_{1})^{2}](1-\rho)\Bigg\rparen\kern 5.0pt,

where the last line using the fact that
\lambda(\mathcal{H})\geq 1
.
Overall, this yields the lower bound

\Esym_{#1}[\Varsym\lparen\hat{\tau}|y\rparen]\geq\frac{\lambda(\mathcal{H})}{n}\Bigg\lparen\Esym[y_{i}(e_{0})^{2}](1-\rho)+\Esym[y_{i}(e_{1})^{2}](1-\rho)\Bigg\rparen

(31)

Now, to apply the second moment method, let’s calculate the variance of
\Varsym\lparen\hat{\tau}|y\rparen
with respect to the randomness of the outcomes.

\displaystyle\Varsym_{#1}\lparen\Varsym\lparen\hat{\tau}\rparen\rparen

\displaystyle=\Varsym_{#1}\Bigg\lparen\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\{0,1\}\\
\ell\in\{0,1\}\end{subarray}}(-1)^{k-\ell}y_{i}(e_{k})y_{j}(e_{\ell})\Covsym\Bigg\lparen\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(i,k)}]}{\Pr\lparen E_{(i,k)}\rparen},\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(j,\ell)}]}{\Pr\lparen E_{(j,\ell)}\rparen}\Bigg\rparen\Bigg\rparen

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{k\in\{0,1\}}(y_{i}(e_{k})^{2}-\Esym[y_{i}(e_{k})^{2}])\Varsym\Big\lparen\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(i,k)}]}{\Pr\lparen E_{(i,k)}\rparen}\Big\rparen\right.\right.

\displaystyle\left.\left.+\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i\neq j}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\{0,1\}\\
\ell\in\{0,1\}\end{subarray}}(-1)^{k-\ell}y_{i}(e_{k})y_{j}(e_{\ell})\Covsym\Bigg\lparen\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(i,k)}]}{\Pr\lparen E_{(i,k)}\rparen},\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(j,\ell)}]}{\Pr\lparen E_{(j,\ell)}\rparen}\Bigg\rparen\right.\right.

\displaystyle\left.\left.+\frac{2}{n^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\lparen y_{i}(e_{0})y_{i}(e_{1})-\Esym[y_{i}(e_{0})y_{i}(e_{1})]\rparen\Covsym\Bigg\lparen\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(i,0)}]}{\Pr\lparen E_{(i,0)}\rparen},\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(i,1)}]}{\Pr\lparen E_{(i,1)}\rparen}\Bigg\rparen\right)^{2}\right]

Now, we use the fact that for any random variables
X,Y,Z
, using Cauchy-Schwartz

\Esym[(X+Y+Z)^{2}]\leq 3\Esym[X^{2}]+3\Esym[Y^{2}]+3\Esym[Z^{2}]\kern 5.0pt.

Using this, the variance can be bounded as

\displaystyle\Varsym_{#1}\lparen\Varsym\lparen\hat{\tau}\rparen\rparen

\displaystyle\leq 3\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{k\in\{0,1\}}(y_{i}(e_{k})^{2}-\Esym[y_{i}(e_{k})^{2}])\Varsym\Big\lparen\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(i,k)}]}{\Pr\lparen E_{(i,k)}\rparen}\Big\rparen\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle+3\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{n^{2}}\left(\sum_{i\neq j}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\{0,1\}\\
\ell\in\{0,1\}\end{subarray}}(-1)^{k-\ell}\lparen y_{i}(e_{k})y_{j}(e_{\ell})\right.\right.

\displaystyle\quad\quad\left.\left.-\Esym[y_{i}(e_{k})y_{j}(e_{\ell})]\rparen\Covsym\Bigg\lparen\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(i,k)}]}{\Pr\lparen E_{(i,k)}\rparen},\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(j,\ell)}]}{\Pr\lparen E_{(j,\ell)}\rparen}\Bigg\rparen\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle+3\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{n^{2}}\left(2\sum_{i=1}^{n}\lparen y_{i}(e_{0})y_{i}(e_{1})-\Esym[y_{i}(e_{0})y_{i}(e_{1})]\rparen\Covsym\Bigg\lparen\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(i,0)}]}{\Pr\lparen E_{(i,0)}\rparen},\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(i,1)}]}{\Pr\lparen E_{(i,1)}\rparen}\Bigg\rparen\right)^{2}\right]

Let’s focus on the first term. As before, let
R=\max(\Esym[X^{4}],\Esym[Y^{4}])
, where
(X,Y)\sim\mathcal{D}
.
For any random variables
X,Y
, we have
\Esym[(X+Y)^{2}]\leq 2\Esym[X^{2}]+2\Esym[Y^{2}]
. Applying this for the first term gives

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{k\in\{0,1\}}(y_{i}(e_{k})^{2}-\Esym[y_{i}(e_{k})^{2}])\Varsym\Big\lparen\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(i,k)}]}{\Pr\lparen E_{(i,k)}\rparen}\Big\rparen\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{n^{4}}\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Esym\big[(y_{i}(e_{0})^{2}-\Esym[y_{i}(e_{0})^{2}])^{2}]\Varsym\Big\lparen\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(i,0)}]}{\Pr\lparen E_{(i,0)}\rparen}\Big\rparen^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{2}{n^{4}}\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Esym\big[(y_{i}(e_{1})^{2}-\Esym[y_{i}(e_{1})^{2}])^{2}]\Varsym\Big\lparen\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(i,1)}]}{\Pr\lparen E_{(i,1)}\rparen}\Big\rparen^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n^{4}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{k\in\{0,1\}}\Esym\big[(y_{i}(e_{k})^{2}-\Esym[y_{i}(e_{k})^{2}])^{2}]\Varsym\Big\lparen\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(i,k)}]}{\Pr\lparen E_{(i,k)}\rparen}\Big\rparen^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2R}{n^{4}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{k\in\{0,1\}}\Bigg\lparen\frac{2r\lambda(\mathcal{H})}{(1-1/(2r))^{2}}\Bigg\rparen^{2}

where we have used Lemma
4.2
to bound the variance of the indicators and Lemma
E.1
to bound the moments

\displaystyle=\frac{4R}{n^{3}}\Bigg\lparen\frac{2r}{(1-1/(2r))^{2}}\Bigg\rparen^{2}\lambda(\mathcal{H})^{2}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}\lparen\lambda(\mathcal{H})^{2}/n^{3}\rparen

For the second term, again by independence we get

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n^{4}}\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\sum_{i\neq j}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\{0,1\}\\
\ell\in\{0,1\}\end{subarray}}(-1)^{k-\ell}\lparen y_{i}(e_{k})y_{j}(e_{\ell})\right.\right.

\displaystyle\left.\left.\quad\quad-\Esym[y_{i}(e_{k})y_{j}(e_{\ell})]\rparen\Covsym\Bigg\lparen\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(i,k)}]}{\Pr\lparen E_{(i,k)}\rparen},\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(j,\ell)}]}{\Pr\lparen E_{(j,\ell)}\rparen}\Bigg\rparen\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n^{4}}\sum_{i\neq j}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\{0,1\}\\
\ell\in\{0,1\}\end{subarray}}\Esym[y_{i}(e_{k})^{2}]\Esym[y_{j}(e_{\ell})^{2}]\Covsym\Bigg\lparen\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(i,k)}]}{\Pr\lparen E_{(i,k)}\rparen},\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(j,\ell)}]}{\Pr\lparen E_{(j,\ell)}\rparen}\Bigg\rparen^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n^{4}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\neq j\\
d_{\mathcal{H}}(i,j)\geq 2\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\{0,1\}\\
\ell\in\{0,1\}\end{subarray}}\Esym[y_{i}(e_{k})^{2}]\Esym[y_{j}(e_{\ell})^{2}]\Covsym\Bigg\lparen\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(i,k)}]}{\Pr\lparen E_{(i,k)}\rparen},\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(j,\ell)}]}{\Pr\lparen E_{(j,\ell)}\rparen}\Bigg\rparen^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{n^{4}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\neq j\\
d_{\mathcal{H}}(i,j)\leq 1\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\{0,1\}\\
\ell\in\{0,1\}\end{subarray}}\Esym[y_{i}(e_{k})^{2}]\Esym[y_{j}(e_{\ell})^{2}]\Covsym\Bigg\lparen\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(i,k)}]}{\Pr\lparen E_{(i,k)}\rparen},\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(j,\ell)}]}{\Pr\lparen E_{(j,\ell)}\rparen}\Bigg\rparen^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C\frac{R}{n^{4}}\sum_{i,j:d_{\mathcal{H}}(i,j)\geq 2}\Bigg\lparen\frac{|\mathcal{N}_{b}(i)\cap\mathcal{N}_{b}(j)|}{\lambda(\mathcal{H})}\Bigg\rparen^{2}+C\frac{R\cdot|E_{\mathcal{H}}|}{n^{4}}

for some absolute constant
C
.
To see this, observe that if
i,j
are neighbors in
\mathcal{H}
, their covariance is
-1
by Lemma
4.2
, which gives a total contribution of
|E_{\mathcal{H}}|
for these terms.
If
i,j
are at distance
2
, their covariance can be bounded by the number of common parents of
i,j
as per Lemma
4.2
.
We have also used Lemma
E.1
to bound the moments by
R
.
Moreover, we can further upper bound the above by

\displaystyle\leq C\frac{R}{n^{4}}\sum_{i\neq j}\frac{|\mathcal{N}_{b}(i)\cap\mathcal{N}_{b}(j)|}{\lambda(\mathcal{H})}+C\frac{R\cdot\lambda(\mathcal{H})n}{n^{4}}

where for the first term we have used using the importance ordering property
|\mathcal{N}_{b}(i)\cap\mathcal{N}_{b}(j)|\leq\lambda(\mathcal{H})
so that
|\mathcal{N}_{b}(i)\cap\mathcal{N}_{b}(j)|/\lambda(\mathcal{H})\leq 1
.
For the second term, we use that that the average degree is always bounded by the largest eigenvalue, i.e.
n^{-1}|E_{\mathcal{H}}|=d_{avg}(\mathcal{H})\leq\lambda(\mathcal{H})
.
To bound the first term further, we use the same observation as in the proof of Theorem
4.1
about paths of length
2
and the squared adjacency matrix (see Section
D.4
for more details). Overall, this yields that

\displaystyle\leq 2C\frac{R\cdot\lambda(\mathcal{H})}{n^{3}}

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{O}\lparen\lambda(\mathcal{H})/n^{3}\rparen\kern 5.0pt,

For the third term, we have by independence that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n^{4}}\mathbb{E}\left[\left(2\sum_{i=1}^{n}\lparen y_{i}(e_{0})y_{i}(e_{1})-\Esym[y_{i}(e_{0})y_{i}(e_{1})]\rparen\Covsym\Bigg\lparen\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(i,0)}]}{\Pr\lparen E_{(i,0)}\rparen},\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(i,1)}]}{\Pr\lparen E_{(i,1)}\rparen}\Bigg\rparen\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\qquad=4\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Esym[\lparen y_{i}(e_{0})y_{i}(e_{1})-\Esym[y_{i}(e_{0})y_{i}(e_{1})]\rparen^{2}]\Covsym\Bigg\lparen\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(i,0)}]}{\Pr\lparen E_{(i,0)}\rparen},\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(i,1)}]}{\Pr\lparen E_{(i,1)}\rparen}\Bigg\rparen^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\frac{4R}{n^{4}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Covsym\Bigg\lparen\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(i,0)}]}{\Pr\lparen E_{(i,0)}\rparen},\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(i,1)}]}{\Pr\lparen E_{(i,1)}\rparen}\Bigg\rparen^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad=\frac{4R}{n^{3}}

by Lemma
E.1

\displaystyle\qquad=\mathcal{O}\Bigg\lparen\frac{1}{n^{3}}\Bigg\rparen

by Lemma
D.1
.

Overall, the above analysis gives us that
\Varsym_{#1}\lparen\Varsym\lparen\hat{\tau}|y\rparen\rparen=\mathcal{O}\lparen\lambda(\mathcal{H})^{2}/n^{3}\rparen
.
To complete the proof, we apply Chebyshev’s inequality.
Define
Q=\Esym[y_{i}(e_{0})^{2}](1-\rho)+\Esym[y_{i}(e_{1})^{2}](1-\rho)
.
An earlier calculation showed that
\Esym_{#1}[\Varsym\lparen\hat{\tau}|y\rparen]\geq\frac{\lambda(\mathcal{H})}{n}\cdot Q
.
By Assumption
6
, we have that the correlations are not perfect so that
Q>0
.
Set
M=2Q
and observe that

\displaystyle\Pr_{#1}\Bigg\lparen\Varsym\lparen\hat{\tau}|y\rparen<\frac{M}{n}\lambda(\mathcal{H})\Bigg\rparen

\displaystyle=\Pr_{#1}\Bigg\lparen\Varsym\lparen\hat{\tau}|y\rparen-\Esym_{#1}[\Varsym\lparen\hat{\tau}|y\rparen]<\frac{M}{n}\lambda(\mathcal{H})-\Esym_{#1}[\Varsym\lparen\hat{\tau}|y\rparen]\Bigg\rparen

\displaystyle\leq\Pr_{#1}\Bigg\lparen\Varsym\lparen\hat{\tau}|y\rparen-\Esym_{#1}[\Varsym\lparen\hat{\tau}|y\rparen]<\frac{\lambda(\mathcal{H})}{n}\cdot\lparen M-Q\rparen\Bigg\rparen

where the last line follows from the lower bound on
\Esym_{#1}[\Varsym\lparen\hat{\tau}|y\rparen]
.
Now applying the choice of
M
and Chebyshev’s inequality, we have that

\displaystyle=\Pr_{#1}\Bigg\lparen\Varsym\lparen\hat{\tau}|y\rparen-\Esym_{#1}[\Varsym\lparen\hat{\tau}\mid y\rparen]<\frac{\lambda(\mathcal{H})}{n}\cdot Q\Bigg\rparen

\displaystyle\leq\Pr\Bigg\lparen\Big\lvert\Varsym\lparen\hat{\tau}\rparen-\Esym_{#1}[\Varsym\lparen\hat{\tau}\mid y\rparen]\Big\rvert>\frac{\lambda(\mathcal{H})}{n}\cdot Q\Bigg\rparen

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\Varsym_{#1}\lparen\Varsym\lparen\hat{\tau}\rparen\rparen}{\Big\lparen\frac{\lambda(\mathcal{H})}{n}Q\Big\rparen^{2}}

Finally, using that
Q>0
together with the upper bound on
\Varsym_{#1}\lparen\Varsym\lparen\hat{\tau}\rparen\rparen
, we have that

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}\Bigg\lparen\frac{\lambda(\mathcal{H})^{2}/n^{3}}{\lambda(\mathcal{H})^{2}/n^{2}}\Bigg\rparen

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}\lparen 1/n\rparen\kern 5.0pt,

which concludes the proof.
∎

E.2
Variance Bound is Conservative (Proposition
5.1
)

Proposition
5.1
.

The variance bound is conservative:
\Varsym\lparen\hat{\tau}\rparen\leq\mathrm{VB}
.
Moreover, equality holds for potential outcomes which align with the leading eigenvector of
\boldsymbol{V}
.

Proof.

The fact that
\Varsym\lparen\hat{\tau}\rparen\leq\mathrm{VB}
follows from the proof of Theorem
4.1
.
In particular, from the discussion after equation(
28
) it follows that

\sup_{\boldsymbol{y}(e_{0}),\boldsymbol{y}(e_{1})}\frac{n\Varsym\lparen\hat{\tau}\rparen}{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}y_{i}(e_{1})^{2}+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}y_{i}(e_{1})^{2}}=\lambda(\boldsymbol{V})

Thus, when the potential outcomes are aligned with the leading eigenvector of
\boldsymbol{V}
, we get equality in the above expression.
∎

E.3
Estimator
\widehat{\mathrm{VB}}
is Unbiased (Proposition
5.2
)

We start with the proof that the variance estimator
\widehat{\mathrm{VB}}
is unbiased for estimating the bound
\mathrm{VB}
on the variance.

Proposition
5.2
.

Under Assumption
1
, the variance estimator is unbiased for the variance bound:
\Esym[\widehat{\mathrm{VB}}]=\mathrm{VB}
.
Under Assumptions
1
-
2
, the expected variance bound has the rate:
\Esym[\widehat{\mathrm{VB}}]=\mathcal{O}\lparen\lambda(\mathcal{H})/n\rparen
.

Proof.

For the first part, we use the observation that
Y_{i}^{2}\mathbf{1}[E_{(i,k)}]=y_{i}(e_{k})^{2}\mathbf{1}[E_{(i,k)}]
, which was justified in the proof of Proposition
3.5
and requires Assumption
1
.

\displaystyle\Esym[\widehat{\mathrm{VB}}]

\displaystyle=\Esym\Bigg[\frac{\lambda(\boldsymbol{V})}{n}\Big\lparen\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}Y_{i}^{2}\Big\lparen\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(i,1)}]}{\Pr\lparen E_{(i,1)}\rparen}+\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(i,1)}]}{\Pr\lparen E_{(i,1)}\rparen}\Big\rparen\Big\rparen\Bigg]

\displaystyle=\frac{\lambda(\boldsymbol{V})}{n}\Big\lparen\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Big\lparen y_{i}^{2}(e_{1})+y_{i}^{2}(e_{0})\Big\rparen\Big\rparen

\displaystyle=\mathrm{VB}

For the second part, it follows from the proof of Theorem
4.1
that
\lambda(\boldsymbol{V})\leq 12.5\cdot\lambda(\mathcal{H})
.
Thus, using Assumption
2
we get
VB=\mathcal{O}\lparen\lambda(\mathcal{H})/n\rparen
, which concludes the proof.
∎

E.4
Consistency of
\widehat{\mathrm{VB}}
(Proposition
5.3
)

We next prove that
\widehat{\mathrm{VB}}
converges to
\mathrm{VB}
at a sufficiently fast rate that will allow us to construct confidence intervals later.

Proposition
5.3
.

Under Assumptions
1
and
4
,

the additive error between the normalized variance estimator and the variance bound scales as

\Big\lvert\frac{n}{\lambda(\mathcal{H})}\cdot\widehat{\mathrm{VB}}-\frac{n}{\lambda(\mathcal{H})}\cdot\mathrm{VB}\Big\rvert=\mathcal{O}_{p}\Bigg\lparen\sqrt{\frac{\lambda(\mathcal{H})}{n}\ }\Bigg\rparen\kern 5.0pt.

Proof.

Let’s calculate the variance of
\widehat{\mathrm{VB}}
.
Using the exact same calculations as in calculating the variance of the modified Horvitz-Thompson estimator, we get

\displaystyle\Varsym\Bigg\lparen\frac{n}{\lambda(\mathcal{H})}\cdot\widehat{\mathrm{VB}}\Bigg\rparen

\displaystyle=\Varsym\Bigg\lparen\frac{n}{\lambda(\mathcal{H})}\cdot\frac{\lambda(\boldsymbol{V})}{n}\Bigg[\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}Y_{i}^{2}\Big\{\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(i,1)}]}{\Pr\lparen E_{(i,1)}\rparen}+\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(i,0)}]}{\Pr\lparen E_{(i,0)}\rparen}\Big\}\Bigg]\Bigg\rparen

\displaystyle=\Big\lparen\frac{\lambda(\boldsymbol{V})}{\lambda(\mathcal{H})}\Big\rparen^{2}\cdot\Varsym\Big\lparen\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}Y_{i}^{2}\Big\{\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(i,1)}]}{\Pr\lparen E_{(i,1)}\rparen}+\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(i,0)}]}{\Pr\lparen E_{(i,0)}\rparen}\Big\}\Big\rparen

\displaystyle=\Big\lparen\frac{\lambda(\boldsymbol{V})}{\lambda(\mathcal{H})}\Big\rparen^{2}\cdot\Varsym\Big\lparen\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}y_{i}(e_{1})^{2}\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(i,1)}]}{\Pr\lparen E_{(i,1)}\rparen}+y_{i}(e_{0})^{2}\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(i,0)}]}{\Pr\lparen E_{(i,0)}\rparen}\Big\rparen

\displaystyle=\Big\lparen\frac{\lambda(\boldsymbol{V})}{\lambda(\mathcal{H})}\Big\rparen^{2}\cdot\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\{0,1\}\\
\ell\in\{0,1\}\end{subarray}}C_{(i,k),(j,l)}\cdot y_{i}^{2}(e_{k})y_{j}^{2}(e_{l})\kern 5.0pt.

By the proof of Theorem
4.1
it follows that
\lambda(\boldsymbol{V})\leq 12.5\lambda(\mathcal{H})
so that the first term in the expression is at most
12.5^{2}
.
Next, notice the similarity of the sum in the above expression and the expression for the variance of the effect estimator in (
24
).
In fact, they differ only in the extra
(-1)^{k-l}
factor that is appearing in (
24
) and the extra squares on the outcomes that are appearing in the expression above.
To make these two expressions appear exactly the same, imagine we define the following potential outcomes
y^{\prime}
.

y_{i}^{\prime}(e_{0}):=y_{i}^{2}(e_{0})\quad\quad y_{i}^{\prime}(e_{1}):=-y_{i}^{2}(e_{1})\kern 5.0pt.

Then, notice that the variance of
\hat{\tau}
for these particular outcomes
y^{\prime}
is exactly the same as the expression above (if we disregard the constant factor that multiplies it).
Moreover, for the
y^{\prime}
outcomes we have

\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(y_{i}^{\prime}(e_{0}))^{2}+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(y_{i}^{\prime}(e_{1}))^{2}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}y_{i}^{4}(e_{0})+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}y_{i}^{4}(e_{1})=\mathcal{O}\lparen 1\rparen\,,

where the last step follows from Assumption
4
. Thus, we can apply Theorem
4.1
for the
y^{\prime}
outcomes to get

\displaystyle\Varsym\Bigg\lparen\frac{n}{\lambda(\mathcal{H})}\widehat{\mathrm{VB}}\Bigg\rparen\leq 12.5^{2}\cdot\frac{12.5\cdot\lambda(\mathcal{H})}{n}\Big\lparen\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(y_{i}^{\prime}(e_{0}))^{2}+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(y_{i}^{\prime}(e_{1}))^{2}\Big\rparen=\mathcal{O}\Big\lparen\frac{\lambda(\mathcal{H})}{n}\Big\rparen\kern 5.0pt.

By Proposition
5.2
, the variance estimator is unbiased for the conservative upper bound.
Thus, an application of Chebyshev’s inequality now implies the desired result.
∎

Next, we prove that the above approximation is strong enough to imply convergence of the ratio
\mathrm{VB}/\widehat{\mathrm{VB}}
to 1.

Proposition E.4
.

Under Assumptions
1
-
5
,
the ratio of the variance estimator and the variance bound converges to 1 in probability:
\mathrm{VB}/\widehat{\mathrm{VB}}\xrightarrow{p}1
.

Proof.

It will be more convenient to prove the equivalent claim
\widehat{\mathrm{VB}}/\mathrm{VB}\xrightarrow{p}1
.
By Proposition
5.3
we get

\Big\lvert\frac{\widehat{\mathrm{VB}}}{\mathrm{VB}}-1\Big\rvert=\Big\lvert\frac{\widehat{\mathrm{VB}}-\mathrm{VB}}{\mathrm{VB}}\Big\rvert\leq\frac{\mathcal{O}_{p}\Big\lparen(\lambda(\mathcal{H})/n)^{3/2}\Big\rparen}{\Omega\left(\lambda(\mathcal{H})/n\right)}\leq\mathcal{O}_{p}\lparen\sqrt{\lambda(\mathcal{H})/n}\rparen=o_{p}\lparen 1\rparen\kern 5.0pt,

where the inequality uses Assumption
5
to lower bound the denominator and Proposition
5.3
to upper bound the denominator.
Finally, the second inequality uses Assumption
3
which states that
\lambda(\mathcal{H})=o\lparen n\rparen
.
∎

E.5
Chebyshev-Style Confidence Intervals (Theorem
5.4
)

We restate the Theorem for convenience.

Theorem
5.4
.

Under Assumptions
1
-
5
, the Chebyshev intervals asymptotically cover at the nominal rates: for each
\alpha\in(0,1]
,
\liminf_{n\to\infty}\Pr\lparen\tau\in C_{\textrm{Cheb}}(\alpha)\rparen\geq 1-\alpha
.

Proof.

We will use Chebyshev’s inequality. Indeed, let’s fix some
\epsilon\in(0,1)
. By Chebyshev’s inequality, together with the Proposition
5.1
, we have that

\displaystyle\Pr\lparen\tau\notin C_{\textrm{Cheb}}(\alpha)\rparen

\displaystyle=\Pr\Bigg\lparen\lvert\hat{\tau}-\tau\rvert>\frac{\sqrt{\widehat{\mathrm{VB}}}}{\sqrt{\alpha}},\Big\lvert\frac{\widehat{\mathrm{VB}}}{\mathrm{VB}}-1\Big\rvert\leq\epsilon\Bigg\rparen

(32)

\displaystyle\quad\quad+\Pr\Bigg\lparen\lvert\hat{\tau}-\tau\rvert>\frac{\sqrt{\widehat{\mathrm{VB}}}}{\sqrt{\alpha}},\Big\lvert\frac{\widehat{\mathrm{VB}}}{\mathrm{VB}}-1\Big\rvert>\epsilon\Bigg\rparen

\displaystyle\leq\Pr\Bigg\lparen\lvert\hat{\tau}-\tau\rvert>\frac{\sqrt{\mathrm{VB}}\sqrt{1-\epsilon}}{\sqrt{\alpha}}\Bigg\rparen+\Pr\Bigg\lparen\lvert\hat{\tau}-\tau\rvert>\frac{\sqrt{\widehat{\mathrm{VB}}}}{\sqrt{\alpha}},\Big\lvert\frac{\widehat{\mathrm{VB}}}{\mathrm{VB}}-1\Big\rvert>\epsilon\Bigg\rparen

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\alpha\Varsym\lparen\hat{\tau}\rparen}{(1-\epsilon)\widehat{\mathrm{VB}}}+\Pr\Bigg\lparen\lvert\hat{\tau}-\tau\rvert>\frac{\sqrt{\widehat{\mathrm{VB}}}}{\sqrt{\alpha}},\Big\lvert\frac{\widehat{\mathrm{VB}}}{\mathrm{VB}}-1\Big\rvert>\epsilon\Bigg\rparen

(33)

In the last step, we used Chebyshev’s inequality. Now, using Proposition
5.1
we have that

\frac{\alpha\Varsym\lparen\hat{\tau}\rparen}{(1-\epsilon)\widehat{\mathrm{VB}}}\leq\frac{\alpha}{1-\epsilon}

Also, by Proposition
E.4
(which we can apply since Assumptions
1
-
5
are satisfied) we obtain

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\Pr\Bigg\lparen\lvert\hat{\tau}-\tau\rvert>\frac{\sqrt{\widehat{\mathrm{VB}}}}{\sqrt{\alpha}},\Big\lvert\frac{\widehat{\mathrm{VB}}}{\mathrm{VB}}-1\Big\rvert>\epsilon\Bigg\rparen\leq\limsup_{n\to\infty}\Pr\Bigg\lparen\Big\lvert\frac{\widehat{\mathrm{VB}}}{\mathrm{VB}}-1\Big\rvert>\epsilon\Bigg\rparen=0

Overall, (
32
) implies that

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\Pr\lparen\tau\notin C_{\textrm{Cheb}}(\alpha)\rparen\leq\frac{\alpha}{1-\epsilon}

This holds for any
\epsilon\in(0,1)
. Taking
\epsilon\to 0
finishes the claim.
∎

E.6
Central Limit Theorem (Theorem
5.5
)

To prove a Central Limit Theorem, we use Stein’s method with dependency graphs, which is a common technique in the literature on causal inference under interference.
We will use the following result found in the survey of
57
.

Definition 5

(Definition 3.1 of
57
)
.

A collection of random variables
X_{1}\dots X_{n}
has
dependency neighborhoods

N(i)\subseteq\{1\dots n\}
if
X_{i}
is independent of
\{X_{j}\}_{j\notin N(i)}

Proposition E.5

(Theorem of 3.5
57
)
.

Let
X_{1}\dots X_{n}
be random variables with
\Esym[X_{i}^{4}]<\infty
,
\Esym[X_{i}]=0
, and
\sigma^{2}=\Varsym\lparen\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}\rparen
, and define
W=\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}/\sigma
.
Suppose that
X_{1}\dots X_{n}
has dependency neighborhoods with
D=\max_{i\in[n]}\lvert N(i)\rvert
.
Then for a standard normal random variable
Z
,

d_{W}(W,Z)\leq\frac{D^{2}}{\sigma^{3}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Esym[\lvert X_{i}\rvert^{3}]+\sqrt{\frac{26}{\pi}}\frac{D^{3/2}}{\sigma^{2}}\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Esym[X_{i}^{4}]}\kern 5.0pt,

where
d_{W}
is the Wasserstein distance.

The proof of the following theorem relies on using these results applied to the modified Horvitz–Thompson estimator.

Theorem
5.5
.

Suppose that for the maximum degree
d_{\textrm{max}}
we have
d_{\textup{max}}(\mathcal{H})=o(n^{1/9})
.
Then, under Assumptions
1
-
5
, the standardized estimator is asymptotically normal under the Conflict Graph Design:
(\hat{\tau}-\tau)/\sqrt{\Varsym\lparen\hat{\tau}\rparen}\xrightarrow{d}\mathcal{N}(0,1)
.

Proof.

We consider the case where the graph has at least one edge so that
\lambda(\mathcal{H})\geq 2
, otherwise there is no interference and standard results may be applied.
Recall that the Horvitz–Thompson estimator may be written as the average of individual effect estimators,

\hat{\tau}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\hat{\tau}_{i}\quad\text{where}\quad\hat{\tau}_{i}=Y_{i}\Big\lparen\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(i,1)}]}{\Pr\lparen E_{(i,1)}\rparen}-\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(i,0)}]}{\Pr\lparen E_{(i,0)}\rparen}\Big\rparen\kern 5.0pt.

Under the arbitrary neighborhood interference model (Assumption
1
), we have that the individual effect estimators take the form

\hat{\tau}_{i}=y_{i}(e_{1})\cdot\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(i,1)}]}{\Pr\lparen E_{(i,1)}\rparen}-y_{i}(e_{0})\cdot\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(i,0)}]}{\Pr\lparen E_{(i,0)}\rparen}\kern 5.0pt.

For each subject
i\in[n]
, define the random variable
X_{i}
to be the error of the individual effect estimator, i.e.

X_{i}=\hat{\tau}_{i}-\tau_{i}=y_{i}(e_{1})\cdot\Bigg\lparen\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(i,1)}]}{\Pr\lparen E_{(i,1)}\rparen}-1\Bigg\rparen-y_{i}(e_{0})\cdot\Bigg\lparen\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(i,0)}]}{\Pr\lparen E_{(i,0)}\rparen}-1\Bigg\rparen\kern 5.0pt.

Define
\sigma^{2}=\Varsym\lparen\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}\rparen
and
W=\frac{1}{\sigma}\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}
.
By construction, we have that the standardized estimator is equal to
W
, i.e.

\frac{\hat{\tau}-\tau}{\sqrt{\Varsym\lparen\hat{\tau}\rparen}}=W\kern 5.0pt.

We now seek to show that the conditions of Proposition
E.5
hold.
There will be four main conditions that we need to check.

First, we need to check that the variables
X_{i}
have mean zero.
To this end, observe that by Lemma
3.3
, the probabilities
\Pr\lparen E_{(i,k)}\rparen
are positive so that the individual effect estimators are unbiased for the individual effects, i.e.
\Esym[\hat{\tau}_{i}]=\tau_{i}
.
This means that
\Esym[X_{i}]=0
.

Second, we need to bound the third and fourth moments of the individual variables
X_{i}
.
To this end, consider a positive integer
m
and observe that

\displaystyle\Esym\big[\lvert X_{i}\rvert^{m}\big]

\displaystyle=\Esym\Bigg[\Bigg\lvert y_{i}(e_{1})\cdot\Bigg\lparen\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(i,1)}]}{\Pr\lparen E_{(i,1)}\rparen}-1\Bigg\rparen-y_{i}(e_{0})\cdot\Bigg\lparen\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(i,0)}]}{\Pr\lparen E_{(i,0)}\rparen}-1\Bigg\rparen\Bigg\rvert^{m}\Bigg]

\displaystyle\leq 2^{m-1}\Esym\Bigg[\Bigg\lvert y_{i}(e_{1})\cdot\Bigg\lparen\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(i,1)}]}{\Pr\lparen E_{(i,1)}\rparen}-1\Bigg\rparen\Bigg\rvert^{m}\Bigg]

\displaystyle\quad\quad+2^{m-1}\Esym\Bigg[\Bigg\lvert y_{i}(e_{0})\cdot\Bigg\lparen\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(i,0)}]}{\Pr\lparen E_{(i,0)}\rparen}-1\Bigg\rparen\Bigg\rvert^{m}\Bigg]

\displaystyle\leq 2^{m-1}\Big\lparen\lvert y_{i}(e_{1})\rvert^{m}+\lvert y_{i}(e_{0})\rvert^{m}\Big\rparen\cdot\max_{k\in\{0,1\}}\Esym\Bigg[\Bigg\lvert\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(i,k)}]}{\Pr\lparen E_{(i,k)}\rparen}-1\Bigg\rvert^{m}\Bigg]

We can calculate this last term directly as

\displaystyle\Esym\Bigg[\Bigg\lvert\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(i,k)}]}{\Pr\lparen E_{(i,k)}\rparen}-1\Bigg\rvert^{m}\Bigg]

\displaystyle=(1-\Pr\lparen E_{(i,k)}\rparen)+\Pr\lparen E_{(i,k)}\rparen\cdot\Big\lparen\frac{1}{\Pr\lparen E_{(i,k)}\rparen}-1\Big\rparen^{m}

\displaystyle\leq 1+\frac{1}{\Pr\lparen E_{(i,k)}\rparen^{m-1}}

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{O}\lparen\lambda(\mathcal{H})^{m-1}\rparen\kern 5.0pt,

where the last inequality follows from the analysis that
1/\Pr\lparen E_{(i,k)}\rparen\leq\mathcal{O}\lparen\lambda(\mathcal{H})\rparen
.
Thus, we have that

\Esym\big[\lvert X_{i}\rvert^{m}\big]\leq\Big\lparen\lvert y_{i}(e_{1})\rvert^{m}+\lvert y_{i}(e_{0})\rvert^{m}\Big\rparen\cdot\mathcal{O}\lparen\lambda(\mathcal{H})^{m-1}\rparen\kern 5.0pt.

Next, we derive a lower bound on
\sigma
using Assumption
5
, which states that the variance of the modified Horvitz–Thompson estimator is bounded below as
\Varsym\lparen\hat{\tau}\rparen\geq\Omega\lparen\lambda(\mathcal{H})/n\rparen
.
To this end, observe that

\sigma^{2}=\Varsym\Big\lparen\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}\Big\rparen=\Varsym\Big\lparen\sum_{i=1}^{n}\hat{\tau}_{i}-\tau_{i}\Big\rparen=n^{2}\Varsym\Big\lparen\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\hat{\tau}_{i}\Big\rparen=n^{2}\Varsym\lparen\hat{\tau}\rparen\geq\Omega\lparen n\cdot\lambda(\mathcal{H})\rparen\kern 5.0pt.

Finally, we discuss the dependency neighborhoods associated to these random variables.
For a variable
i\in[n]
, the neighborhood
N(i)
may be taken to be the 2-hop neighborhood in the conflict graph
\mathcal{H}
, i.e.

N(i)=\big\{j\in[n]:\mathcal{N}(i)\cap\mathcal{N}(j)\neq\emptyset\big\}\kern 5.0pt.

Let us verify that this indeed forms a dependency neighborhood.
First, observe that each
X_{i}
is a function of the independent random variables
U_{\ell}:\ell\in\mathcal{N}(i)
.
Fix
i\in[n]
and consider the collection of random variables
\{X_{j}\}_{j\notin N(i)}
.
Let us use the shorthand
R_{i}=[n]\setminus N(i)
.
The random variables in this collection are functions of the independent random variables
U_{k}:k\in\mathcal{N}(R_{i})
.
Note that
\{U_{\ell}:\ell\in\mathcal{N}(i)\}
and
\{U_{k}:k\in\mathcal{N}(R_{i})\}
are, by definition, disjoint sets of independent random variables.
Thus,
X_{i}
is independent from the collection of random variables
\{X_{j}\}_{j\notin N(i)}
so that
N(i)
forms a valid dependency graph.
Thus, the maximum degree of the dependency graph may be bounded as
D\leq d_{\max}(\mathcal{H})^{2}
.

We are now ready to plug these results into Stein’s method via dependency graphs.
By directly applying Proposition
E.5
, we obtain the bound on the Wasserstein distance:

\displaystyle d_{W}(W,Z)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{D^{2}}{\sigma^{3}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Esym[\lvert X_{i}\rvert^{3}]+\sqrt{\frac{26}{\pi}}\frac{D^{3/2}}{\sigma^{2}}\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Esym[X_{i}^{4}]}

by Proposition
E.5

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{O}\Bigg\lparen\frac{d_{\max}(\mathcal{H})^{4}\lambda(\mathcal{H})^{2}}{\Big\lparen n\cdot\lambda(\mathcal{H})\Big\rparen^{3/2}}\Bigg\rparen\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{n}\lvert y_{i}(e_{1})\rvert^{3}+\lvert y_{i}(e_{0})\rvert^{3}

\displaystyle\qquad+\mathcal{O}\Bigg\lparen\frac{d_{\max}(\mathcal{H})^{3}\lambda(\mathcal{H})^{3/2}}{n\cdot\lambda(\mathcal{H})}\Bigg\rparen\cdot\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\lvert y_{i}(e_{1})\rvert^{4}+\lvert y_{i}(e_{0})\rvert^{4}}

by calculations above

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}\Bigg\lparen\frac{d_{\max}(\mathcal{H})^{4}\lambda(\mathcal{H})^{1/2}}{n^{1/2}}\Bigg\rparen\cdot\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\lvert y_{i}(e_{1})\rvert^{3}+\lvert y_{i}(e_{0})\rvert^{3}

\displaystyle\qquad+\mathcal{O}\Bigg\lparen\frac{d_{\max}(\mathcal{H})^{3}\lambda(\mathcal{H})^{1/2}}{n^{1/2}}\Bigg\rparen\cdot\sqrt{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\lvert y_{i}(e_{1})\rvert^{4}+\lvert y_{i}(e_{0})\rvert^{4}}

by rearranging

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}\Bigg\lparen\frac{d_{\max}(\mathcal{H})^{4}\lambda(\mathcal{H})^{1/2}}{n^{1/2}}\Bigg\rparen

by Assumption
4

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}\Bigg\lparen\Big\lparen\frac{d_{\max}(\mathcal{H})^{9}}{n}\Big\rparen^{1/2}\Bigg\rparen

by using
\lambda(\mathcal{H})\leq d_{\max}(\mathcal{H})
.

Thus, the standardized estimator converges to a normal distribution in Wasserstein metric if
d_{\max}(\mathcal{H})^{9}\lambda(\mathcal{H})=o\lparen n\rparen
.
This implies convergence in distribution, which completes the proof.
∎

E.7
Wald-Type Confidence Intervals (Corollary
5.6
)

Corollary
5.6
.

Under Assumptions
1
-
5
and
d_{\textup{max}}(\mathcal{H})=o(n^{1/9})
, the Wald intervals asymptotically cover at the nominal rates: for each
\alpha\in(0,1]
,
\liminf_{n\to\infty}\Pr\lparen\tau\in C_{\textrm{Wald}}(\alpha)\rparen\geq 1-\alpha
.

Proof.

We start again by fixing some
\epsilon\in(0,1)
. We can write

\displaystyle\Pr\lparen\tau\notin C_{\textrm{Wald}}(\alpha)\rparen

(34)

\displaystyle=\Pr\Bigg\lparen\lvert\hat{\tau}-\tau\rvert>\Phi^{-1}(1-\alpha/2)\cdot\sqrt{\widehat{\mathrm{VB}}},\Big\lvert\frac{\widehat{\mathrm{VB}}}{\mathrm{VB}}-1\Big\rvert\leq\epsilon\Bigg\rparen

(35)

\displaystyle\qquad+\Pr\Bigg\lparen\lvert\hat{\tau}-\tau\rvert>\Phi^{-1}(1-\alpha/2)\cdot\sqrt{\widehat{\mathrm{VB}}},\Big\lvert\frac{\widehat{\mathrm{VB}}}{\mathrm{VB}}-1\Big\rvert>\epsilon\Bigg\rparen

\displaystyle\leq\Pr\Bigg\lparen\lvert\hat{\tau}-\tau\rvert>\Phi^{-1}(1-\alpha/2)\cdot\sqrt{\mathrm{VB}}\sqrt{1-\epsilon}\Bigg\rparen

(36)

\displaystyle\qquad+\Pr\Bigg\lparen\lvert\hat{\tau}-\tau\rvert>\Phi^{-1}(1-\alpha/2)\cdot\sqrt{\widehat{\mathrm{VB}}},\Big\lvert\frac{\widehat{\mathrm{VB}}}{\mathrm{VB}}-1\Big\rvert>\epsilon\Bigg\rparen

\displaystyle\leq\Pr\Bigg\lparen\lvert\hat{\tau}-\tau\rvert>\Phi^{-1}(1-\alpha/2)\cdot\sqrt{\Varsym\lparen\hat{\tau}\rparen}\sqrt{1-\epsilon}\Bigg\rparen

(37)

\displaystyle\qquad+\Pr\Bigg\lparen\lvert\hat{\tau}-\tau\rvert>\Phi^{-1}(1-\alpha/2)\cdot\sqrt{\mathrm{VB}},\Big\lvert\frac{\widehat{\mathrm{VB}}}{\mathrm{VB}}-1\Big\rvert>\epsilon\Bigg\rparen\kern 5.0pt.

(38)

The last step follows since by Proposition
5.1
we have
\Varsym\lparen\hat{\tau}\rparen\leq\widehat{\mathrm{VB}}
.
By Proposition
E.4
(which we can apply since Assumptions
1
-
5
are satisfied) we obtain

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\Pr\Bigg\lparen\lvert\hat{\tau}-\tau\rvert>\Phi^{-1}(1-\alpha/2)\cdot\sqrt{\mathrm{VB}},\Big\lvert\frac{\widehat{\mathrm{VB}}}{\mathrm{VB}}-1\Big\rvert>\epsilon\Bigg\rparen\leq\limsup_{n\to\infty}\Pr\Bigg\lparen\Big\lvert\frac{\widehat{\mathrm{VB}}}{\mathrm{VB}}-1\Big\rvert>\epsilon\Bigg\rparen=0\kern 5.0pt.

Also, since Assumptions
1
-
5
are satisfied and
d_{\textup{max}}(\mathcal{H})=o(n^{1/9})
, we can apply the result of Theorem
5.5
, which yields

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\Pr\Bigg\lparen\lvert\hat{\tau}-\tau\rvert>\Phi^{-1}(1-\alpha/2)\cdot\sqrt{\Varsym\lparen\hat{\tau}\rparen}\sqrt{1-\epsilon}\Bigg\rparen

\displaystyle\qquad=\lim_{n\to\infty}\Pr\Bigg\lparen\frac{\hat{\tau}-\tau}{\sqrt{\Varsym\lparen\hat{\tau}\rparen}}\notin\left[-\Phi^{-1}(1-\alpha/2)\cdot\sqrt{1-\epsilon},\Phi^{-1}(1-\alpha/2)\cdot\sqrt{1-\epsilon}\right]\Bigg\rparen

\displaystyle\qquad=2\cdot\Bigg\lparen 1-\Phi\Big\lparen\Phi^{-1}(1-\alpha/2)\cdot\sqrt{1-\epsilon}\Big\rparen\Bigg\rparen\kern 5.0pt.

Thus, overall using (
38
) we get

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\Pr\lparen\tau\notin C_{\textrm{Wald}}(\alpha)\rparen\leq 2\cdot\Bigg\lparen 1-\Phi\Big\lparen\Phi^{-1}(1-\alpha/2)\cdot\sqrt{1-\epsilon}\Big\rparen\Bigg\rparen\kern 5.0pt.

Since this holds for every
\epsilon\in(0,1)
, the above bound also holds when taking
\epsilon\to 0
, which yields by continuity of
\Phi

2\cdot\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\Bigg\lparen 1-\Phi\Big\lparen\Phi^{-1}(1-\alpha/2)\cdot\sqrt{1-\epsilon}\Big\rparen\Bigg\rparen=2\cdot\Big\lparen 1-\Phi\Big\lparen\Phi^{-1}(1-\alpha/2)\Big\rparen\Big\rparen=\alpha\kern 5.0pt.\qed

E.8
Counterexample for Central Limit Theorem (Proposition
5.7
)

In this section, we provide the explicit construction of the sequence of graphs where the standardized estimator fails to converge in distribution.

Proposition
5.7
.

Suppose the causal estimand is the direct effect.
There exists a sequence of graphs with
\lambda(\mathcal{H})=\Theta\left(\sqrt{n}\right)
and a sequence of outcome functions
y^{(n)}
satisfying Assumptions
1
-
5
, such that the standardized estimator
(\hat{\tau}-\tau)/\sqrt{\Varsym\lparen\hat{\tau}\rparen}
does not converge in distribution under the Conflict Graph Design.

Proof.

Our strategy will be to define two sequences of graphs
G_{1}^{(n)},G_{2}^{(n)}
and corresponding sequences of potential outcomes, so that
\hat{\tau}
has different limiting distributions across the two sequences. Then, we can construct a third sequence by alternating between these two, which will clearly not have a limiting distribution. Throughout this section, we will use the notation
X=o_{P}(1)
to denote that
X
converges to
0
in probability.

We start by describing the first sequence
G_{1}^{(n)}=([n],E_{n})
. This is a star graph on
n
vertices, meaning that node
1
is the center and there is an edge
(1,i)
for all
i\in\{2,\ldots,n\}
. Since we focus on the Average Direct Treatment Effect, the conflict graph
\mathcal{H}
is
G_{1}^{(n)}
itself. Standard calculations show that the maximum eigenvalue
\lambda(\mathcal{H})=\sqrt{n-1}
and the eigenvalue ordering
\pi_{\textup{eig}}
has
1
as the most important node, followed by all the rest. Thus, all nodes
\geq 2
have
1
as a parent, and this is the only parent they have.

We consider the following sequence of outcomes

y_{i}(e_{0})=0,\forall i\in[n]\quad\quad y_{i}(e_{1})=1\forall i\in[n]\kern 5.0pt.

Clearly, the outcomes satisfy Assumption
4
.
Also,
\lambda(\mathcal{H})=\Theta(\sqrt{n})
, as the premise of the proposition suggests.
We will calculate the limiting distribution of the standardized estimator
(\hat{\tau}-\tau)/\sqrt{\Varsym\lparen\hat{\tau}\rparen}
.
We start by calculating the variance, using the expressions for the covariances established in Lemma
D.1
.
For simplicity, we consider the Conflict Graph Design with
r=1
.

\displaystyle n^{2}\Varsym\lparen\hat{\tau}\rparen

\displaystyle=(2\lambda(\mathcal{H})-1)+\sum_{i=2}^{n}\Bigg\lparen\frac{2\lambda(\mathcal{H})}{1-\frac{1}{\lambda(\mathcal{H})}}-1\Bigg\rparen

\displaystyle\quad\quad+\sum_{i=2}^{n}\lparen-1\rparen+\sum_{j,k\geq 2,j\neq k}\Big\lparen(1-1/\lambda(\mathcal{H}))^{-1}-1\Big\rparen

\displaystyle=2\sqrt{n-1}-1+2n\sqrt{n-1}-2n+\frac{(n-1)(n-2)}{\sqrt{n-1}}+o(n^{3/2})

\displaystyle\asymp 3n^{3/2}\kern 5.0pt.

In the above, the notation
a_{n}\asymp b_{n}
means that
a_{n}/b_{n}\to 1
as
n\to\infty
.
Next, observe that the error of the estimator can be written as

\displaystyle n(\hat{\tau}-\tau)

\displaystyle=\Big\lparen\frac{\mathbf{1}[U_{1}=e_{1}]}{\Pr\lparen U_{1}=e_{1}\rparen}-1\Big\rparen+\sum_{i\geq 2}\Big\lparen\frac{\mathbf{1}[U_{1}=*,U_{i}=e_{1}]}{\Pr\lparen U_{1}=*,U_{i}=e_{1}\rparen}-1\Big\rparen

\displaystyle=\Big\lparen\frac{\mathbf{1}[U_{1}=e_{1}]}{\Pr\lparen U_{1}=e_{1}\rparen}-1\Big\rparen+\mathbf{1}[U_{1}=*]\sum_{i\geq 2}\Big\lparen\frac{\mathbf{1}[U_{i}=e_{1}]}{\Pr\lparen U_{1}=*,U_{i}=e_{1}\rparen}-1\Big\rparen

\displaystyle\quad\quad+\mathbf{1}[U_{1}\neq*](-(n-1))

\displaystyle=\Big\lparen\frac{\mathbf{1}[U_{1}=e_{1}]}{\Pr\lparen U_{1}=e_{1}\rparen}-1\Big\rparen+\mathbf{1}[U_{1}=*]\underbrace{\sum_{i\geq 2}\Big\lparen\frac{\mathbf{1}[U_{i}=e_{1}]}{\Pr\lparen U_{1}=*,U_{i}=e_{1}\rparen}-(1-1/\lambda(\mathcal{H}))^{-1}\Big\rparen}_{S_{n}}

\displaystyle\quad+\mathbf{1}[U_{1}=*]\underbrace{(n-1)\Big\lparen(1-1/\lambda(\mathcal{H}))^{-1}-1\Big\rparen}_{\asymp\sqrt{n}}+\underbrace{\mathbf{1}[U_{1}\neq*](-(n-1))}_{o_{P}(1)}\kern 5.0pt.

In the above, the second term, which we have denoted by
S_{n}
, is a sum of
n-1
independent zero mean random variables. The last term is
o_{P}(1)
since
\Pr\lparen U_{1}\neq*\rparen=1/\lambda(\mathcal{H})=1/\sqrt{n-1}\to 0
as
n\to\infty
.
For
S_{n}
, we can use the standard Central Limit Theorem for independent variables, which yields

\frac{S_{n}}{\sqrt{\Varsym\lparen S_{n}\rparen}}\xrightarrow{d}\mathcal{N}(0,1)\kern 5.0pt.

Next, we calculate
\Varsym\lparen S_{n}\rparen
.
Because it is a sum of independent random variables, we easily get

\Varsym\lparen S_{n}\rparen=(n-1)(1-1/\lambda(\mathcal{H}))^{-2}(2\lambda(\mathcal{H})-1)\asymp 2n^{3/2}\kern 5.0pt.

Thus, we have that

\frac{n(\hat{\tau}-\tau)}{\sqrt{\Varsym\lparen S_{n}\rparen}}=\frac{S_{n}}{\sqrt{\Varsym\lparen S_{n}\rparen}}+O(n^{-1/4})+o_{P}(1)\xrightarrow{d}\mathcal{N}(0,1)\kern 5.0pt.

In the final step, we used Slutsky’s Theorem.
However, notice that
\Varsym\lparen S_{n}\rparen
and
\Varsym\lparen\hat{\tau}\rparen
are not asymptotically the same.
Indeed, we have that

\frac{\hat{\tau}-\tau}{\sqrt{\Varsym\lparen\hat{\tau}\rparen}}=\frac{n(\hat{\tau}-\tau)}{\sqrt{n^{2}\Varsym\lparen\hat{\tau}\rparen}}=\frac{n(\hat{\tau}-\tau)}{\sqrt{\Varsym\lparen S_{n}\rparen}}\frac{\sqrt{\Varsym\lparen S_{n}\rparen}}{\sqrt{n^{2}\Varsym\lparen\hat{\tau}\rparen}}\kern 5.0pt.

By the preceding calculations,

\frac{\sqrt{\Varsym\lparen S_{n}\rparen}}{\sqrt{n^{2}\Varsym\lparen\hat{\tau}\rparen}}\to\sqrt{\frac{2}{3}}\kern 5.0pt.

Thus, again by using Slutsky’s Theorem, we conclude that

\frac{\hat{\tau}-\tau}{\sqrt{\Varsym\lparen\hat{\tau}\rparen}}\xrightarrow{d}\mathcal{N}(0,2/3)\kern 5.0pt.

Thus, the limiting distribution has variance strictly smaller than
1
. The reason for this “loss of variance” becomes clear once we examine the role of node
1
. It is influencing all nodes in the graph, meaning that it’s fluctuations account for a constant fraction of the overall variance of the estimator. On the other hand, the terms that are affected by the fluctuations of
1
converge to
0
in probability and hence do not influence the limiting distribution. This is the part of the variance that was “lost” in the limit.
Such phenomena seem to arise whenever there is a large discrepancy between the maximum eigenvalue and the largest degree in the graph. This is because nodes with large degree can affect the overall variance by a constant factor, but this effect is lost in the limiting distribution.
Thus, it is clear that determining the limiting distribution is a significantly more subtle problem than point estimation of
\tau
.

\displaystyle C_{1}
\displaystyle C_{2}
\displaystyle C_{4}
\displaystyle C_{3}
\displaystyle r_{1}
\displaystyle r_{2}
\displaystyle r_{3}
\displaystyle r_{4}

Figure 19
:
Graph
G_{16}^{(2)}
used in the counterexample for the Central Limit Theorem. This graph has
4
cliques of size
4
.

Going back to the proof, we construct the second sequence of graphs
G_{n}^{(2)}
.
For simplicity, we will focus on the case where
\sqrt{n}
is an integer. Otherwise, we replace
\sqrt{n}
with
\lceil\sqrt{n}\rceil
. That way, we might end up with
n-\sqrt{n}
vertices in the end, so we can add
O(\sqrt{n})
independent vertices, which do not affect the limiting distribution, in order to have a total of
n
vertices.
Since nothing changes asymptotically, to avoid those complications we assume
\sqrt{n}
is an integer from now on.

To construct
G_{2}^{(n)}
, consider
\sqrt{n}
copies of the complete graph on
\sqrt{n}
vertices
K_{\sqrt{n}}
and let
C_{i}
be the
i
-th copy of
K_{\sqrt{n}}
.
Let
r_{i}
be a distinguished node in
C_{i}
. For all
i\neq j
, connect
r_{i}
and
r_{j}
by an edge. This is the topology of
G_{n}^{(2)}
and Figure
19
contains an example for
n=16
.

First of all, notice that the maximum degree in this graph is
2\sqrt{n}
and the minimum degree is
\sqrt{n}
. Thus, for the maximum eigenvalue
\lambda(\mathcal{H})
of
G_{2}^{(n)}
, we have that
\lambda(\mathcal{H})=\Theta(\sqrt{n})
, which again satisfies the premise of our Proposition.
It’s also easy to see that in the eigenvalue ordering
\pi_{\textup{eig}}
, the nodes
r_{1},\ldots,r_{\sqrt{n}}
come first in arbitrary order and then the rest of the variables follow. Thus, every node
r_{i}
has the rest of the nodes in
C_{i}
as children.
We will calculate the limiting distribution of
(\hat{\tau}-\tau)/\sqrt{\Varsym\lparen\hat{\tau}\rparen}
for this graph sequence under the Conflict Graph Design.

Next, let us define the potential outcomes. We set

y_{i}(e_{0})=0,\forall i\in[n]\quad\quad y_{i}(e_{1})=1,\forall i\in[n]\kern 5.0pt.

It is clear that these outcomes satisfy Assumption
4
.

We continue with some calculations involving the variance of
\hat{\tau}
.
In particular, denoting
p_{i}=\Pr\lparen E_{(i,1)}\rparen
for every unit
i
, we have

\displaystyle n^{2}\Varsym\lparen\hat{\tau}\rparen

\displaystyle=\Varsym\Bigg\lparen\sum_{i=1}^{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{j\in C_{i}}\left(\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(j,1)}]}{p_{j}}-1\right)\Bigg\rparen

\displaystyle=\Varsym\Bigg\lparen\sum_{i=1}^{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{j\in C_{i},j\neq r_{i}}\left(\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(j,1)}]}{p_{j}}-1\right)+\sum_{i=1}^{\sqrt{n}}\left(\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(r_{i},1)}]}{p_{r_{i}}}-1\right)\Bigg\rparen

\displaystyle=\Varsym\Bigg\lparen\sum_{i=1}^{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{j\in C_{i},j\neq r_{i}}\left(\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(j,1)}]}{p_{j}}-1\right)\Bigg\rparen+\Varsym\Bigg\lparen\sum_{i=1}^{\sqrt{n}}\left(\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(r_{i},1)}]}{p_{r_{i}}}-1\right)\Bigg\rparen

\displaystyle+\Covsym\Bigg\lparen\sum_{i=1}^{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{j\in C_{i},j\neq r_{i}}\left(\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(j,1)}]}{p_{j}}-1\right),\sum_{i=1}^{\sqrt{n}}\left(\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(r_{i},1)}]}{p_{r_{i}}}-1\right)\Bigg\rparen\kern 5.0pt.

Let us focus on the first term, as the remaining ones will be negligible as we will see in the sequel.
Notice that since we have excluded
r_{i}
from each sum, the sums corresponding to different clusters
C_{i}
are independent. This is because for
j\in C_{i}
, with
j\neq r_{i}
, the random variable
\mathbf{1}[E_{(j,1)}]
only depends on
U
variables of
C_{i}
. Thus, we get

\displaystyle\Varsym\Bigg\lparen\sum_{i=1}^{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{j\in C_{i},j\neq r_{i}}\left(\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(j,1)}]}{p_{j}}-1\right)\Bigg\rparen

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{\sqrt{n}}\Varsym\Bigg\lparen\sum_{j\in C_{i},j\neq r_{i}}\left(\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(j,1)}]}{p_{j}}-1\right)\Bigg\rparen=\sqrt{n}v\kern 5.0pt,

where

v=v_{i}:=\Varsym\lparen\sum_{j\in C_{i},j\neq r_{i}}\left(\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(j,1)}]}{p_{j}}-1\right)\rparen\kern 5.0pt.

The preceding observation follows
by symmetry, since all
C_{i}
blocks will have the same variance
v
.
Note also that an easy calculation shows that
v=\Theta(n)
, since it is essentially a clique on
\sqrt{n}
vertices. Thus, the first term is
\Omega(n^{3/2})
.
Now, let’s show that the remaining terms are negligible. The second term is essentially the variance of a clique of
\sqrt{n}
vertices,
where the vertices are the
r_{i}
nodes. Thus, it can be upper bounded by
O(n)
.
For the third term, notice that each
r_{i}
is independent of all nodes in
C_{j}
except when
j=i
. Thus, we can write

\displaystyle\Covsym\left(\sum_{i=1}^{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{j\in C_{i},j\neq r_{i}}\left(\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(j,1)}]}{p_{j}}-1\right),\sum_{i=1}^{\sqrt{n}}\left(\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(r_{i},1)}]}{p_{r_{i}}}-1\right)\right)

\displaystyle\qquad=\sum_{i=1}^{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{j\in C_{i},j\neq r_{i}}\Covsym\left(\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(r_{i},1)}]}{p_{r_{i}}}-1,\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(j,1)}]}{p_{j}}-1\right)

\displaystyle\qquad=\sum_{i=1}^{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{j\in C_{i},j\neq r_{i}}(-1)

\displaystyle\qquad=-\sqrt{n}(\sqrt{n}-1)\kern 5.0pt.

Overall, this implies that
n^{2}\Varsym\lparen\hat{\tau}\rparen=\Theta(n^{3/2})
and in particular

n^{2}\Varsym\lparen\hat{\tau}\rparen=\sqrt{n}v\big\lparen 1+o\lparen 1\rparen\big\rparen\kern 5.0pt.

Thus, we can substitute in the normalization of the estimator
\sqrt{n}v
instead of
\Varsym\lparen\hat{\tau}\rparen
without changing the limiting distribution, by Slutsky’s Theorem.
The reason we do this will become apparent when we analyze the estimator.
Indeed, we can write

\frac{\hat{\tau}-\tau}{\sqrt{\Varsym\lparen\hat{\tau}\rparen}}=\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{\sqrt{n}}\overbrace{\sum_{j\in C_{i},j\neq r_{i}}\left(\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(j,1)}]}{p_{j}}-1\right)}^{L_{i}}}{\sqrt{\sqrt{n}v}}\big\lparen 1+o\lparen 1\rparen\big\rparen+\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{\sqrt{n}}\left(\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(r_{i},1)}]}{p_{r_{i}}}-1\right)}{\sqrt{\sqrt{n}v}}\big\lparen 1+o\lparen 1\rparen\big\rparen\kern 5.0pt.

(39)

For the first term, as we explained earlier, variables
L_{i}
are completely independent from each other, since we have excluded the
r_{i}
nodes. Each one of them have variance
v
, so we have the correct normalization in the denominator. Thus, we can use the standard Central Limit Theorem for independent random variables. This guarantees that

\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{\sqrt{n}}L_{i}}{\sqrt{\sqrt{n}v}}\xrightarrow{d}\mathcal{N}(0,1)\kern 5.0pt.

For the second term, we now show it converges to
0
in probability.
Indeed, we can easily bound

-\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\sqrt{\sqrt{n}v}}\leq\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{\sqrt{n}}\left(\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(r_{i},1)}]}{p_{r_{i}}}-1\right)}{\sqrt{\sqrt{n}v}}\leq\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{\sqrt{n}}\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(r_{i},1)}]}{p_{r_{i}}}}{\sqrt{\sqrt{n}v}}\kern 5.0pt.

For the left hand side, by the preceding observations it decays as
O(n^{-1/4})
. Thus, it suffices to show that the right hand sice converges to
0
in probability.
For the right hand side, we can write

\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{\sqrt{n}}\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{(r_{i},1)}]}{p_{r_{i}}}}{\sqrt{\sqrt{n}v}}\leq\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{\sqrt{n}}\frac{\mathbf{1}[u_{r_{i}}=1]}{p_{r_{i}}}}{\sqrt{\sqrt{n}v}}\kern 5.0pt.

The right hand side is a sum of
\sqrt{n}
independent scaled Bernoulli random variables with probability
1/\lambda(\mathcal{H})=\Theta(1/\sqrt{n})
. Since
p_{r_{i}}=\Omega(1/\sqrt{n})
, it suffices to show convergence to
0
in probability of the following quantity:

\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{\sqrt{n}}\mathbf{1}[u_{r_{i}}=1]}{n^{1/4}}\kern 5.0pt.

The numerator has mean and variance
\Theta(1)
, so by standard second-moment arguments, the fraction is
o_{P}(1)
. Thus, using (
39
) and applying Slutksy’s Theorem once more, we have shown that for the sequence
G_{2}^{(n)}

\frac{\hat{\tau}-\tau}{\sqrt{\Varsym\lparen\hat{\tau}\rparen}}\xrightarrow{d}\mathcal{N}(0,1)\kern 5.0pt.

To conclude the proof of the proposition, we can now construct a third sequence of graphs

G_{3}^{(n)}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}G_{1}^{(n)}&\text{ if $n$ is even}\\
G_{2}^{(n)}&\text{ if $n$ is odd}\end{array}\right.

The outcome functions are chosen similarly corresponding to the sequence for even and odd
n
. We now notice that the sequence of random variables
(\hat{\tau}-\tau)/\sqrt{\Varsym\lparen\hat{\tau}\rparen}
does not have a limit in distribution. That is because the subsequence corresponding to even
n
converges in distribution to
\mathcal{N}(0,2/3)
and the subsequence corresponding to odd
n
converges to
\mathcal{N}(0,1)
.
This concludes the proof.
∎

Appendix F
Analysis of Standard Horvitz-Thompson estimator (Theorem
4.6
)

In this Section, we present an analysis of the standard Horvitz–Thompson estimator for the direct effect under the Conflict Graph Design under the stronger condition that
d_{\max}(G)=\Theta\lparen\lambda(G)\rparen
.
The proof technique used here is considerably more involved than for analyzing the modified Horvitz–Thompson estimator.

Because the theorem focuses on the direct treatment effect, the conflict graph
\mathcal{H}
is simply to
G
with added self-cycles, as discussed in Section
3.1
.
Thus,
\lambda(\mathcal{H})=\lambda(G)=\lVert\boldsymbol{A}_{G}\rVert+1
, where as usual
\boldsymbol{A}_{G}
is the adjacency matrix of
G
, so we will simply use
\lambda(G)
for the remainder of this discussion.
Given the graph
G
, we refer to its maximum degree as
d_{\textrm{max}}
.
The assumption in Theorem
4.6
then becomes
d_{\textrm{max}}\leq C\cdot\lambda(G)
.
Throughout this section,
C
will be this specific constant. We will use notation such as
C^{\prime},C^{\prime\prime}
to denote constants that depend on
C
but are not computed explicitly for convenience. Note that
C^{\prime},C^{\prime\prime}
might refer to different constants in different places in the proof.
We will denote by
N^{(G)}(i),N_{b}^{(G)}(i),N_{a}^{(G)}(i)
the set of parents and children of
i
in
G
, respectively. As usual, we use the notation
\widetilde{N}^{(G)}(i)=N^{(G)}(i)\cup\{i\}
for the extended neighborhood of a node
i
.
We also abuse notation and denote for any subset
S\subseteq[n]
the set of parents of nodes in
S
to be
N_{b}^{(G)}(S)=\cup_{i\in S}N^{(G)}_{b}(i)
.
We will sometimes suppress the dependence of the neighborhood on the graph to avoid overloading notation, when it is clear from the context.

F.1
Proof of Theorem
4.6

Theorem
4.6
.

Consider the direct treatment effect
\tau
, as defined in Section
2.3
.
Suppose Assumptions
1
-
3
hold and, in addition,
d_{\textrm{max}}(\mathcal{H})=\Theta\lparen\lambda(\mathcal{H})\rparen
.
Then, under the Conflict Graph Design the standard Horvitz–Thompson estimator converges in mean square at
\sqrt{\lambda(\mathcal{H})/n}
rate:

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\sqrt{\frac{n}{\lambda(\mathcal{H})}}\cdot\Esym\Big[\big\lparen\tau-\hat{\tau}_{\textrm{standard}}\big\rparen^{2}\Big]^{1/2}<\infty\kern 5.0pt.

We make a few straightforward remarks about the Conflict Graph Design specialized to the case of the Direct Effect.
First, recall that the direct effect has contrastive interventions
\boldsymbol{z}_{i,(1)}=\boldsymbol{a}_{i}
and
\boldsymbol{z}_{i,(0)}=\boldsymbol{0}
, where
\boldsymbol{a}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
is the standard basis vector with
i
-th entry being
1
and the rest
0
and
\boldsymbol{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
is the all zeroes vector.
For convenience, we restate the Conflict Graph Design below, where we make a few cosmetic simplifications due to the fact that we are focusing specifically on the direct effect.
Indeed, we replaced Line
2
in the general description of Conflict Graph Design (Algorithm
1
) with a more explicit description: if
U_{i}=e_{0}
, then
i
and all of its neighbors in
G
receive control. If
U_{i}=e_{1}
, then
i
receives treatment and all of its neighbors in
G
receive control.
We emphasize again that this is a difference only in presentation and the design remains unchanged.

Input:

Importance ordering
\pi
, maximum eigenvalue
\lambda(G)
, and graph
G
.

Output:

Random intervention
Z\in\mathcal{Z}=\{0,1\}^{n}

Sample desired exposure variables
U_{1},\dots,U_{n}
independently and identically as

U_{i}\leftarrow\left\{\begin{array}[]{lr}e_{1}&\text{with probability }\frac{1}{2\lambda(G)}\\
e_{0}&\text{with probability }\frac{1}{2\lambda(G)}\\
&\text{with probability }1-\frac{1}{\lambda(G)}\end{array}\right.

Initialize the intervention vector
Z\leftarrow\boldsymbol{0}
.

1

for

i=1\dots n

do

2

if

U_{i}\in\{e_{1},e_{0}\}
and
U_{j}=*
for all
j\in N_{b}(i)

then

3

if

U_{i}=e_{1}

then

4

Z_{i}\leftarrow 1
and
Z_{j}\leftarrow 0
for all
j\in N(i)

else

5

Z_{i}\leftarrow 0
and
Z_{j}\leftarrow 0
for all
j\in N(i)

Algorithm 5

Conflict-Graph-Design-Direct-Effect

We next state some useful results that will be helpful for the proof of Theorem
4.6
.
At the end of the Section we give the proof of Theorem
4.6
, based on these results.

We start by showing that for the direct effect, the desired exposure event
E_{(i,1)}
is precisely the event of true exposure.
Define the events
F_{(i,0)}=\{d_{i}(Z)=d_{i}(\boldsymbol{0})\}
and
F_{(i,1)}=\{d_{i}(Z)=d_{i}(\boldsymbol{a}_{i})\}
.
These are exactly the events appearing in the standard Horvitz–Thompson estimator, which we seek to analyze.

Lemma F.1
.

Let
Z
be the output of the Conflict Graph Design. Then
F_{(i,1)}
holds if and only if
E_{(i,1)}
holds.

Proof.

Clearly, if
E_{(i,1)}
holds, then by Lemma
3.2

d_{i}(Z)=d_{i}(\boldsymbol{a}_{i})
, so
F_{(i,1)}
holds, so it suffices to prove the converse. Suppose
F_{(i,1)}
holds. This means in particular that
Z_{i}=1
. In the execution of Algorithm
1
, the vector
Z
is initialized to
\boldsymbol{0}
. The only time when
Z_{i}
can obtain the value
1
is when it is
i
’s turn in the For loop and Line 7 is executed. The fact that Line
7
is executed means that
U_{i}=e_{i}
and
U_{j}=*
for all
j\in N_{b}(i)
. Thus,
E_{(i,1)}
holds and the proof is complete.
∎

In order to bound the variance of
\hat{\tau}_{\textrm{standard}}
, we will need to upper bound the covariance terms between all pairs of units
i
and
j
.
Lemma
F.1
shows that the correlation analysis in Theorem
4.1
will be appropriate for analyzing the direct exposure
e_{1}
.
The correlation of the control exposure
e_{0}
terms will be more involved they will depend in an intricate way on the relative topologies of their neighborhoods.

It will prove easier to abstract away these details and provide a general way of bounding correlations between subsets of nodes.
We now give some definitions of useful quantities for arbitrary subsets.
For a subset of nodes
A\subseteq[n]
, we define the following event

E_{A}:=\{Z_{k}=0,\forall k\in A\}\kern 5.0pt.

For any two subsets of nodes
A,B\subseteq[n]
, define their
correlation

C_{A,B}:=\frac{\Pr_{#1}\big\lparen Z_{k}=0,\forall k\in A\cup B\big\rparen}{\Pr_{#1}\big\lparen Z_{k}=0,\forall k\in A\big\rparen\Pr_{#1}\big\lparen Z_{k}=0,\forall k\in B\big\rparen}-1=\Covsym_{#1}\Bigg\lparen\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{A}]}{\Pr_{#1}\lparen E_{A}\rparen},\frac{\mathbf{1}[E_{B}]}{\Pr_{#1}\lparen E_{B}\rparen}\Bigg\rparen

(40)

Note that
A
and
B
could potentially be overlapping.
For any two subsets
A,B
, we define the set of edges
F_{A,B}
to be

\displaystyle F_{A,B}

\displaystyle:=\Bigg\lvert\Big\{(i,j)\in E_{G}:i,j\in A\cup B\Big\}\setminus

\displaystyle\Bigg\lparen\Big\{(i,j)\in E_{G}:i,j\in A\cap B\Big\}\cup\Big\{(i,j)\in E_{G}:i,j\in A\setminus B\Big\}\cup\Big\{(i,j)\in E_{G}:i,j\in B\setminus A\Big\}\Bigg\rparen\Bigg\rvert\kern 5.0pt.

In words, if we think of
A\setminus B,B\setminus A,A\cap B
as a partition of
A\cup B
, then
F_{A,B}
counts the number of edges where the endpoints lie in different subsets of the partition.
Moreover, for any two subsets
A,B\subseteq[n]
, define

R_{A,B}:=\big\{k\in V_{G}\setminus(A\cup B):N_{a}^{(G)}(k)\cap A\neq\emptyset\text{ and }N_{a}^{(G)}(k)\cap B\neq\emptyset\big\}

In other words,
R_{A,B}
contains the nodes outside of
A\cup B
that have children in both
A
and
B
.
Intuitively, edges in
F_{A,B}
and nodes in
R_{A,B}
induce correlations between subsets
A,B
when we run the Conflict Graph Design.

We now present a general theorem that bounds
C_{A,B}
for arbitrary sets
A,B
, which we refer to as the
correlation lemma
.
This result will be instantiated in the proof of Theorem
4.6
with
A
and
B
being the extended neighborhoods of two nodes in the graph.

Theorem F.2

(Correlation Lemma)
.

Let
G
be a graph with maximum eigenvalue
\lambda(G)
satisfying
d_{\textrm{max}}\leq C\cdot\lambda(G)
for some constant
C>0
.
Let
A,B\subseteq[n]
be two arbitrary subsets with
|A|,|B|\leq d_{\textrm{max}}+1
and let
I=A\cap B
. Then, there exists an absolute constant
C^{\prime}
that depends only on
C
, such that

|C_{A,B}|\leq C^{\prime}\cdot\Bigg\lparen\frac{|I|}{\lambda(G)}+\frac{|F_{A,B}|}{\lambda(G)^{2}}+\frac{\sum_{k\in R_{A,B}}|N_{a}(k)\cap A|\cdot|N_{a}(k)\cap B|}{\lambda(G)^{3}}\Bigg\rparen\kern 5.0pt.

(41)

Intuitively, the events
E_{A},E_{B}
depend on the assignments of the
U
variables in a neighborhood of
A,B
.
These neighborhoods could be overlapping, which introduces a lot of dependence between the two events for the two subsets.
We will disentangle these two events by slowly transforming the local topology around
A
and
B
, with the final goal of making
E_{A}
and
E_{B}
independent in the end.
The key technical challenge will be to monitor how
C_{A,B}
changes along this transformation.
This requires a detailed analysis of the probability distribution induced by our design.
The proof of Theorem
F.2
is presented in Section
F.7
, while the transformation process is described in Sections
F.4
,
F.5
and
F.6
.

Theorem
F.2
will be instantiated in the proof of Theorem
4.6
with
A
and
B
being the extended neighborhoods of two nodes in the graph. It will thus be useful to provide an interpretation of the quantities
A\cap B,F_{A,B},R_{A,B}
in terms of more familiar combinatorial quantities in the graph
G
.
In particular, let
P^{(s)}_{i,j}
denote the number of undirected walks of length
s
that start in
i
and end in
j
(repetitions of edges are allowed).
The following
counting lemma
makes the interpretation quantitative.

Lemma F.3

(Counting Lemma)
.

Let
G=(V,E)
be a simple graph and
i,j\in V
be two distinct vertices. Then

\displaystyle|\widetilde{N}(i)\cap\widetilde{N}(j)|

\displaystyle=2P^{(1)}_{i,j}+P^{(2)}_{i,j}

(42)

\displaystyle\big\lvert F_{\widetilde{N}(i),\widetilde{N}(j)}\big\rvert

\displaystyle\leq 2P^{(2)}_{i,j}+P^{(3)}_{i,j}

(43)

\displaystyle\sum_{k\in R_{\widetilde{N}(i),\widetilde{N}(j)}}\big\lvert N_{a}(k)\widetilde{N}(i)\big\rvert\big\lvert N_{a}(k)\cap\widetilde{N}(j)\big\rvert

\displaystyle\leq P^{(4)}_{i,j}

(44)

The counting lemma (Lemma
F.3
above) will enable us to interpret the correlation lemma (Theorem
F.2
) in terms of walks in the graph
G
.
This will be a crucial ingredient in analyzing the correlation terms appearing in the standard Horvitz–Thompson estimator.
The proof of Lemma
F.3
is deferred to Section
F.2
.

With these lemmas in hand, we are now ready to prove Theorem
4.6
.

Proof of Theorem
4.6
.

As discussed at the beginning of this section,
d_{\max}(\mathcal{H})=\Theta\lparen\lambda(\mathcal{H})\rparen
is equivalent to
d_{\max}(G)=\Theta\lparen\lambda(G)\rparen
because we are considering the direct effect.
This means that there exists a constant
C>0
such that
d_{\max}(G)\leq C\lambda(G)
.

Our goal will be to establish a finite sample result similar to the one in Theorem
4.1
.
In particular, we will prove that there exists an absolute constant
K(C)>0
(which depends only on
C
) such that for arbitrary potential outcomes
y_{i}(e_{0}),y_{i}(e_{1})

\Varsym\lparen\hat{\tau}_{\textrm{standard}}\rparen\leq\frac{K(C)\lambda(\mathcal{H})}{n}\cdot\Big\lparen\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}y_{i}(e_{1})^{2}+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}y_{i}(e_{0})^{2}\Big\rparen\kern 5.0pt.

(45)

Since
\hat{\tau}_{\textrm{standard}}
is unbiased, the result of Theorem
4.6
then immediately follows using Assumption
2
, similarly to the proof of Theorem
4.3
.
We will analyze the Conflict Graph Design with sampling parameter
r=1
for simplicity, since our focus is not on the optimal constant
K(C)
that we can obtain.

Let’s remember the definition of the standard Horvitz-Thompson estimator
\hat{\tau}_{\textrm{standard}}
.

\hat{\tau}_{\textrm{standard}}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}Y_{i}\cdot\Bigg\lparen\frac{\mathbf{1}\big[d_{i}(Z)=d_{i}(\boldsymbol{z}_{i,(1)})\big]}{\Pr\big\lparen d_{i}(Z)=d_{i}(\boldsymbol{z}_{i,(1)})\big\rparen}-\frac{\mathbf{1}\big[d_{i}(Z)=d_{i}(\boldsymbol{z}_{i,(0)})\big]}{\Pr\big\lparen d_{i}(Z)=d_{i}(\boldsymbol{z}_{i,(0)})\big\rparen}\Bigg\rparen\kern 5.0pt.

We proceed by splitting
\hat{\tau}_{\textrm{standard}}
into the following two parts, one for estimating average potential outcome under the
e_{1}
exposure and the other for estimating the average potential outcome under the
e_{0}
exposure.
Lemma
F.1
implies that the exposure event is precisely the desired exposure event
E_{(i,1)}
, so that we can write this decomposition as

\hat{\tau}_{\textrm{standard}}=\underbrace{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}Y_{i}\cdot\frac{\mathbf{1}\big[E_{(i,1)}\big]}{\Pr\big\lparen E_{(i,1)}\big\rparen}}_{\hat{\tau}_{\textrm{standard}}^{(1)}}-\underbrace{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}Y_{i}\cdot\frac{\mathbf{1}\big[F_{(i,0)}\big]}{\Pr\big\lparen F_{(i,0)}\big\rparen}}_{\hat{\tau}_{\textrm{standard}}^{(0)}}\kern 5.0pt.

We can bound the variance of
\hat{\tau}_{\textrm{standard}}
by the variances of the individual components as follows

\displaystyle\Varsym\lparen\hat{\tau}_{\textrm{standard}}\rparen

\displaystyle=\Varsym\lparen\hat{\tau}_{\textrm{standard}}^{(1)}\rparen+\Varsym\lparen\hat{\tau}_{\textrm{standard}}^{(0)}\rparen-2\Covsym\lparen\hat{\tau}_{\textrm{standard}}^{(1)},\hat{\tau}_{\textrm{standard}}^{(0)}\rparen

\displaystyle\leq\Varsym\lparen\hat{\tau}_{\textrm{standard}}^{(1)}\rparen+\Varsym\lparen\hat{\tau}_{\textrm{standard}}^{(0)}\rparen+2\sqrt{\Varsym\lparen\hat{\tau}_{\textrm{standard}}^{(1)}\rparen\Varsym\lparen\hat{\tau}_{\textrm{standard}}^{(0)}\rparen}

\displaystyle\leq 2\Varsym\lparen\hat{\tau}_{\textrm{standard}}^{(1)}\rparen+2\Varsym\lparen\hat{\tau}_{\textrm{standard}}^{(0)}\rparen\,,

where in the first inequality we used Cauchy-Schwartz and in the second the AM-GM inequality.
It thus suffices to bound the variance of each of the two components of
\hat{\tau}_{\textrm{standard}}
. For
\hat{\tau}_{\textrm{standard}}^{(1)}
, we observed that it is exactly the same as in the modified estimator
\hat{\tau}
that we analyzed in Theorem
4.1
. In fact
\hat{\tau}_{\textrm{standard}}^{(1)}
is exactly the same as
\hat{\tau}
in the setting where the outcomes
y_{i}(e_{0})=0
. Thus, we can immediately apply Theorem
4.1
to obtain

\Varsym\big\lparen\hat{\tau}_{\textrm{standard}}^{(1)}\big\rparen\leq\frac{12.5\lambda(\mathcal{H})}{n}\cdot\Big\lparen\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}y_{i}(e_{1})^{2}\Big\rparen\kern 5.0pt.

Thus, we are left with upper bounding
\Varsym\lparen\hat{\tau}_{\textrm{standard}}^{(0)}\rparen
.

Lemma
3.2
implies that
E_{(i,0)}\subseteq F_{(i,0)}
. Unfortunately, equality does not hold in general.
This is because there are many different paths that would lead to
F_{(i,0)}
occurring at the end of execution.
For example, suppose a unit
i
has
U_{i}=*
and all of its neighbors
j\in N(i)
also satisfy
U_{j}=*
.
Then, in the final treatment assignment
d_{i}(Z)=d_{i}(\boldsymbol{0})
, but clearly
E_{(i,0)}
doesn’t hold.
The fact that the event
F_{(i,0)}
may be significantly larger than
E_{(i,0)}
is the main source of difficulty in analyzing
\hat{\tau}_{\textrm{standard}}
.
This is because these larger events introduce further correlations that lead to a significantly more complicated analysis.
We will see that now units at distance
3
or
4
away could also be correlated, and we will bound their correlation in terms of the number of paths of a given length between them in the graph
G
.

To start the analysis, let’s write the variance in expanded form

n\cdot\Varsym\lparen\hat{\tau}_{\textrm{standard}}^{(0)}\rparen=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}y_{i}(e_{0})y_{j}(e_{0})\Covsym\Bigg\lparen\frac{\mathbf{1}[F_{(i,0)}]}{\Pr\lparen F_{(i,0)}\rparen},\frac{\mathbf{1}[F_{(j,0)}]}{\Pr\lparen F_{(j,0)}\rparen}\Bigg\rparen\kern 5.0pt.

We will use the notation

C_{ij}:=\Covsym\Bigg\lparen\frac{\mathbf{1}[F_{(i,0)}]}{\Pr\lparen F_{(i,0)}\rparen},\frac{\mathbf{1}[F_{(j,0)}]}{\Pr\lparen F_{(j,0)}\rparen}\Bigg\rparen\kern 5.0pt.

If
\boldsymbol{C}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
is the matrix with entries
C_{ij}
, then we can write the variance as a quadratic form

n\cdot\Varsym\lparen\hat{\tau}_{\textrm{standard}}^{(0)}\rparen=\frac{1}{n}\boldsymbol{y}(e_{0})^{\top}\boldsymbol{C}\boldsymbol{y}(0)\kern 5.0pt.

As discussed in the proof of Theorem
4.1
, proving (
45
) is thus equivalent to showing that for some absolute constant
K(C)>0
(which depends only on
C
) we have

\lVert\boldsymbol{C}\rVert\leq K(C)\cdot\lambda(\mathcal{H})\kern 5.0pt.

(46)

Thus, in the remainder of the proof, our goal will be to establish (
46
).

First, notice that if there are no edges in the graph, then the conflict graph only has self edges, which means that
\lambda(\mathcal{H})=1
.
In that case, as we also remarked in the proof of Theorem
4.1
, all nodes are independent and
\lVert\boldsymbol{C}\rVert=1
, meaning that (
46
) follows trivially.

In the remaining, let’s assume that there is at least one edge in
G
, meaning
\lambda(\mathcal{H})\geq 2
so that
\lambda(G)\geq 1
.
As in the proof of Theorem
4.1
, our strategy will be to upper bound
|C_{ij}|
for all pairs of units
i,j
, based on their distance in the graph. However, the situation in Theorem
4.1
was simpler, since we only had correlations up to nodes of distance
2
from each other. We will see that now we have non-zero correlations up to distance
4
. In addition, even nodes that are at distance less than
4
could exhibit correlations based on paths of length
3
or
4
between them.
Thus, we need a more general way of bounding
C_{ij}
than simply splitting
\boldsymbol{C}
into parts according to the distance. This is provided by Theorem
F.2
. Indeed, let’s fix two nodes
i,j\in V
. Consider the set
A=\widetilde{N}(i),B=\widetilde{N}(j)
, where we recall that
\widetilde{N}(i)=N(i)\cup\{i\}
.
Then, by Definition (
40
) we have that

C_{ij}=C_{A,B}\kern 5.0pt.

It is clear that
|\widetilde{N}(i)|,|\widetilde{N}(j)|\leq d_{\textrm{max}}+1\leq C\lambda(G)
by assumption. Thus, we can apply Theorem
F.2
, which says that there exists a constant
C^{\prime}
depending on
C
, such that

|C_{ij}|\leq C^{\prime}\cdot\left(\frac{|\widetilde{N}(i)\cap\widetilde{N}(j)|}{\lambda(G)}\right.\\
\left.+\frac{|F_{\widetilde{N}(i),\widetilde{N}(j)}|}{\lambda(G)^{2}}+\frac{\sum_{k\in R_{\widetilde{N}(i),\widetilde{N}(j)}}|N_{a}^{(G)}(k)\cap\widetilde{N}(i)|\cdot|N_{a}^{(G)}(k)\cap\widetilde{N}(j)|}{\lambda(G)^{3}}\right)

(47)

Let
P_{ij}^{(s)}
be the number of undirected paths of length
s
from
i
to
j
in
G
(possibly with repeated vertices or edges). Using Lemma
F.3
along with (
47
), we conclude that

\displaystyle|C_{ij}|
