\displaystyle\leq C^{\prime}\cdot\Bigg\lparen\frac{2P^{(1)}_{i,j}+P^{(2)}_{i,j}}{\lambda(G)}+\frac{2P^{(2)}_{i,j}+P^{(3)}_{i,j}}{\lambda(G)^{2}}+\frac{P^{(4)}_{i,j}}{\lambda(G)^{3}}\Bigg\rparen

\displaystyle\leq C^{\prime}\cdot\Bigg\lparen 2\cdot\frac{P^{(1)}_{i,j}}{\lambda(G)}+3\cdot\frac{P^{(2)}_{i,j}}{\lambda(G)}+\frac{P^{(3)}_{i,j}}{\lambda(G)^{2}}+\frac{P^{(4)}_{i,j}}{\lambda(G)^{3}}\Bigg\rparen

\displaystyle\leq 3C^{\prime}\cdot\Bigg\lparen\frac{P^{(1)}_{ij}}{\lambda(G)}+\frac{P^{(2)}_{i,j}}{\lambda(G)}+\frac{P^{(3)}_{i,j}}{\lambda(G)^{2}}+\frac{P^{(4)}_{i,j}}{\lambda(G)^{3}}\Bigg\rparen

(48)

In the above, we used the fact that
\lambda(G)\geq 2
.

Now, note that by simple linear algebra,
P^{(s)}_{i,j}
is exactly the
(i,j)
-th entry in the matrix
\boldsymbol{A}_{G}^{s}
, where
\boldsymbol{A}_{G}
is the adjacency matrix of
G
. Thus, (
48
) implies that

|C_{ij}|\leq 3C^{\prime}\cdot\Bigg\lparen\frac{(\boldsymbol{A}_{G})_{ij}}{\lambda(G)}+\frac{(\boldsymbol{A}_{G}^{2})_{ij}}{\lambda(G)}+\frac{(\boldsymbol{A}_{G}^{3})_{ij}}{\lambda(G)^{2}}+\frac{(\boldsymbol{A}_{G}^{4})_{ij}}{\lambda(G)^{3}}\Bigg\rparen\kern 5.0pt.

It is well known that the operator norm
\lVert\boldsymbol{C}\rVert
could only increase if we take absolute values on all entries of
\boldsymbol{C}
. It also follows from Peron-Frobenius theorem for matrices with non-negative entries that increasing the entries can only increase the operator norm
(
62
)
. These observations imply

\lVert\boldsymbol{C}\rVert\leq 3C^{\prime}\cdot\Bigg\lparen\frac{\lVert\boldsymbol{A}_{G}\rVert}{\lambda(G)}+\frac{\lVert\boldsymbol{A}_{G}^{2}\rVert}{\lambda(G)}+\frac{\lVert\boldsymbol{A}_{G}^{3}\rVert}{\lambda(G)^{2}}+\frac{\lVert\boldsymbol{A}_{G}^{4}\rVert}{\lambda(G)^{3}}\Bigg\rparen\kern 5.0pt.

Moreover, it holds that
\lVert\boldsymbol{A}_{G}^{s}\rVert=\lambda(G)^{s}
.
Thus, setting
K(C)=12C^{\prime}
, the above reduces to

\lVert\boldsymbol{C}\rVert\leq K(C)\cdot\lambda(G)\leq K(C)\cdot\lambda(\mathcal{H})\kern 5.0pt,

which is exactly what we wanted to prove in (
46
). This concludes the proof.
∎

F.2
Proof of Counting Lemma (Lemma
F.3
)

See
F.3

Proof.

In the following, if we want to denote a path with nodes
u_{1},\ldotp,u_{s}
, we will write
u_{1}\to\ldotp\to u_{s}
. We emphasize that the path is still undirected, so this notation is not referring to the direction of edges.

We first prove (
42
). We distinguish between two cases.

•

(i,j)\in E
.
In that case,
\widetilde{N}(i)\cap\widetilde{N}(j)=\{i,j\}\cup(N(i)\cap N(j))
. Notice that the set
N(i)\cap N(j)
is in one to one correspondence with paths of length
2
between
i
and
j
in
G
(each path of length
2
has the form
i\to k\to j
with
k\in N(i)\cap N(j)
). Thus, in that case

\big\lvert\widetilde{N}(i)\cap\widetilde{N}(j)\big\rvert=2+P^{(2)}_{i,j}\kern 5.0pt.

•

(i,j)\notin E
.
In that case,
\widetilde{N}(i)\cap\widetilde{N}(j)=N(i)\cap N(j)
. As we explained in the previous case, the set
N(i)\cap N(j)
is in one to one correspondence with paths of length
2
between
i
and
j
in
G
. Thus, in that case

\big\lvert\widetilde{N}(i)\cap\widetilde{N}(j)\big\rvert=P^{(2)}_{i,j}\kern 5.0pt.

Combining these two cases proves (
42
).

Moving on to (
43
), let
\mathcal{P}_{ij}^{(s)}
be the set of walks from
i
to
j
of length
s
.
By definition
F_{\widetilde{N}(i),\widetilde{N}(j)}
contains the edges with endpoints belonging to different sets in the partition with 3 parts: the intersection of extended neighborhoods
\widetilde{N}(i)\cap\widetilde{N}(j)
, extended neighbors of
i
who are not extended neighbors of
j

\widetilde{N}(i)\setminus\widetilde{N}(j)
, and extended neighbors of
j
which are not extended neighbors of
i

\widetilde{N}(j)\setminus\widetilde{N}(i)
.
We will map each of these edges injectively to

\mathcal{P}_{ij}^{(2)}\cup(\mathcal{P}_{ij}^{(2)})^{\prime}\cup\mathcal{P}_{ij}^{(3)}
, where
(\mathcal{P}_{ij}^{(2)})^{\prime}
is a copy of
\mathcal{P}_{ij}^{(2)}
.
We again distinguish between two cases depending on whether
(i,j)\in E
and also examine edges
(u,v)\in F_{\widetilde{N}(i),\widetilde{N}(j)}
, depending on which two of the three subsets
u
and
v
belong to.

•

(i,j)\in E
.
In this case, observe that the intersection of extended neighborhoods is
\widetilde{N}(i)\cap\widetilde{N}(j)=\{i,j\}\cup(N(i)\cap N(j))
, the extended neighbors of
i
not
j
are
\widetilde{N}(i)\setminus\widetilde{N}(j)=N(i)\setminus N(j)
, and the extended neighbors of
j
not
i
are
\widetilde{N}(j)\setminus\widetilde{N}(i)=N(j)\setminus N(i)
.

–

u\in\widetilde{N}(i)\cap\widetilde{N}(j)
and
v\in\widetilde{N}(i)\setminus\widetilde{N}(j)
.
In that case,
u\in\{i,j\}\cup(N(i)\cap N(j)),v\in N(i)\setminus N(j)
. If
u\in N(i)\cap N(j)
and
v\in N(i)\setminus N(j)
, then
i\to u\to v\to j
is a valid path of length
3
, so we map
(u,v)
to that path. If
u=i
, then
i\to u\to i\to j
is a valid path of length
3
, which has not been accounted in the previous cases, so we map
(u,v)
to it. If
u=j
, then there are not edges between
u
and
N(i)\setminus N(j)
by definition.

–

u\in\widetilde{N}(i)\cap\widetilde{N}(j)
and
v\in\widetilde{N}(j)\setminus\widetilde{N}(i)
.
This case is symmetric to the previous one, and
(u,v)
will again be mapped to paths of length
3
that have not appeared before. In particular,
u\in\{i,j\}\cup(N(i)\cap N(j)),v\in N(j)\setminus N(i)
. If
u\in N(i)\cap N(j)
and
v\in N(j)\setminus N(i)
, then
i\to u\to v\to j
is a valid path of length
3
that has not appeared before, so we map
(u,v)
to that path. If
u=j
, then
i\to j\to v\to j
is a valid path of length
3
, which has not been accounted in the previous cases, so we map
(u,v)
to it. If
u=i
, then there are not edges between
u
and
N(j)\setminus N(i)
by definition

–

u\in\widetilde{N}(i)\setminus\widetilde{N}(j)
and
v\in\widetilde{N}(j)\setminus\widetilde{N}(i)
.

In that case, we have
u\in N(i)\setminus N(j)
and
v\in N(j)\setminus N(i)
. Thus,
(u,v)
can be mapped to the path
i\to u\to v\to j
, which has not been accounted before.

It is clear that the above mapping is injective to
\mathcal{P}_{ij}^{(3)}
, which implies in that case

\big\lvert F_{\widetilde{N}(i),\widetilde{N}(j)}\big\rvert\leq P^{(3)}_{i,j}\kern 5.0pt.

•

(i,j)\notin E
.
In that case, the intersection of extended neighborhood is
\widetilde{N}(i)\cap\widetilde{N}(j)=N(i)\cap N(j)
, the extended neighbors of
i
not
j
are
\widetilde{N}(i)\setminus\widetilde{N}(j)=\{i\}\cup(N(i)\setminus N(j))
, and the extended neighbors of
j
not
i
are
\widetilde{N}(j)\setminus\widetilde{N}(i)=\{j\}\cup(N(j)\setminus N(i))
.

–

u\in\widetilde{N}(i)\cap\widetilde{N}(j)
and
v\in\widetilde{N}(i)\setminus\widetilde{N}(j)
.

In that case,
u\in N(i)\cap N(j),v\in\{i\}\cup(N(i)\setminus N(j))
. If
v\in N(i)\setminus N(j)
, then we map
(u,v)
to the path
i\to u\to v\to j
. If
v=i
, then
(u,v)
is mapped to the path
i\to u\to j
, which belongs to
\mathcal{P}_{ij}^{(2)}
.

–

u\in\widetilde{N}(i)\cap\widetilde{N}(j)
and
v\in\widetilde{N}(j)\setminus\widetilde{N}(i)
.

In that case,
u\in N(i)\cap N(j),v\in\{j\}\cup(N(j)\setminus N(i))
. If
v\in N(j)\setminus N(i)
, then we map
(u,v)
to the path
i\to u\to v\to j
. If
v=j
, then
(u,v)
is mapped to the path
i\to u\to j
, which belongs to the copy
(\mathcal{P}_{ij}^{(2)})^{\prime}
.

–

u\in\widetilde{N}(i)\setminus\widetilde{N}(j)
and
v\in\widetilde{N}(j)\setminus\widetilde{N}(i)
.

In that case,
u\in N(i)\setminus N(j),v\in\{j\}\cup(N(j)\setminus N(i))
. Then, we map
(u,v)
to the path
i\to u\to v\to j
.

(
43
) now follows because the mapping was injective:

\big\lvert F_{\widetilde{N}(i),\widetilde{N}(j)}\big\rvert\leq 2\cdot P^{(3)}_{i,j}+P^{(3)}_{i,j}\kern 5.0pt.

Moving to (
44
), we will map each
k\in R_{\widetilde{N}(i),\widetilde{N}(j)}
to a subset of paths
\phi(k)\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{ij}^{(4)}
such that

|\phi(k)|=|N_{a}(k)\cap\widetilde{N}(i)|\cdot|N_{a}(k)\cap\widetilde{N}(j)|\kern 5.0pt.

(49)

We will also see that if
k\neq k^{\prime}
we have
\phi(k)\cap\phi(k^{\prime})=\emptyset
. (
44
) would then follow trivially.
Indeed, let
k\in R_{\widetilde{N}(i),\widetilde{N}(j)}
. Let us consider all ordered pairs
(u,v)
with
u\in N_{a}(k)\cap\widetilde{N}(i)
and
v\in N_{a}(k)\cap\widetilde{N}(j)
. Notice that each pair defines a path of lenght
4
as
i\to u\to k\to v\to j
. Moreover, since all pairs
(u,v)
are distinct, all of these paths will be distinct. Thus, if we define
\phi(k)
to be the set of all those paths defined for all such ordered pairs
(u,v)
, then clearly (
49
) holds.
Moreover, notice that the third node in each path of
\phi(k)
is
k
, hence
\phi(k)\cap\phi(k^{\prime})=\emptyset
whenever
k\neq k^{\prime}
. This concludes the proof.
∎

F.3
Correlation Lemma: Preliminary Results for Interpolation

In this Section we provide statements that will be useful for the proof of Theorem
41
, which is the only remaining claim to establish Theorem
4.6
.
As discussed earlier in this section, the correlation lemma is proved using a novel coupling argument where the original graph
G
is transformed into a graph with simpler correlations.
A key aspect in the proof will be the
interpolation between different graph topologies
.

It will be more convenient to view
G
as a directed graph, where the direction of an edge is determined by the parent-child relationship between the two endpoints. In particular, if
(i,j)
is an undirected edge, then the directed edge is
i\to j
if and only if
i\in N_{b}^{(G)}(j)
.
We will also view the interpolated graphs as being directed.
Therefore, we now provide a definition of what it means to run the Conflict Graph Design on an arbitrary Directed Acyclic Graph (DAG).
Naturally, it corresponds to running Algorithm
5
using a topological ordering induced by the DAG.

Definition 6
.

Let
H=(V,E)
be a DAG. Then, for each node
i\in V
we define the set of
parents

N_{b}(i)
to be

N_{b}^{(H)}(i):=\{j\in V:j\to i\in E\}

Similarly, we define the set of
children
of
i
to be

N_{a}^{(H)}(i):=\{j\in V:i\to j\in E\}

Then, we say that
Z\in\{0,1\}^{n}
is the
output of the Conflict Graph Design run on
H
, if
Z
is the output of Algorithm
5
, run using the definition of
N_{b}^{(H)}(i)
above.

We can view running the Conflict Graph Design on
H
as running Algorithm
1
using any topological ordering
\pi
of the nodes in
H
.
Notice that the probabilities of the
U_{i}
variables still depend on
\lambda(G)
, which is our original graph, and not on the eigenvalues of the directed graph
H
.

Suppose we run the Conflict Graph Design on a DAG
H
. Whenever we refer to the probability of an event
E
under this design, we will use
\Pr_{#1}\lparen E\rparen
to indicate the fact that Algorithm
5
is run on
H
. Likewise, for the expectation of a function
f
, we will use
\Esym_{#1}[f]
. For every node
i
, we introduce the notations

\alpha_{k}^{(H)}:=\mathbf{1}[U_{k}=e_{1}]\qquad\beta_{k}^{(H)}:=\mathbf{1}[U_{k}=*]

to denote the random variables used in the execution of Algorithm
5
for graph
H
.

We begin our analysis by providing a simple expression for the event that a node receives treatment in the Conflict Graph Design. Specifically, we show that the only possibility for a node to receive treatment is if it has desired exposure
e_{1}
and all of its parents have desired exposures
*
. The arguments are similar to the ones used for Lemma
3.2
, where the event under which a unit receives the desired exposure is analyzed.

Lemma F.4
.

Suppose we run the Conflict Graph Design on a directed graph
H
. Then, for any node
k
we have that

\{Z_{k}=1\}=\{U_{k}=e_{1},U_{k^{\prime}}=*,\forall k^{\prime}\in N_{b}^{(H)}(k)\}\kern 5.0pt.

Proof.

First, observe that for a node
k
, the only way the assignment
Z_{k}=1
happens during execution is if Line
5
is executed for the iteration corresponding to
k
, which means that the condition
U_{k}=e_{1},U_{k^{\prime}}=*,\forall k^{\prime}\in N_{b}^{(H)}(k)
is satisfied. Thus,

\{Z_{k}=1\}\subseteq\{U_{k}=e_{1},U_{k^{\prime}}=*,\forall k^{\prime}\in N_{b}^{(H)}(k)\}\kern 5.0pt.

To prove the reverse inclusion, suppose
U_{k}=e_{1},U_{k^{\prime}}=*,\forall k^{\prime}\in N_{b}^{(H)}(k)
holds. This is equivalent to saying that
E_{(i,k)}
holds, thus by Lemma
3.2
, we know that the assignment of
Z_{k}
will not be altered in the subsequent iterations. This completes the proof.
∎

The standard Horvitz-Thompson depends on the probability of receiving exposure, i.e.
\Pr\lparen d_{i}(Z)=e_{k}\rparen
.
This exposure event
\{d_{i}(Z)=e_{k}\}
is more complicated than the desired exposure event
E_{(i,k)}
used in the construction of the modified Horvitz–Thompson estimator.
As such, it will be useful to obtain formulas for calculating these events.
One very useful such formula is given in the lemma below, and is based on rewriting the probability in terms of indicator random variables.
We remind the reader of the definitions

\alpha_{k}^{(H)}:=\mathbf{1}[U_{k}=e_{1}]\qquad\beta_{k}^{(H)}:=\mathbf{1}[U_{k}=*]

Lemma F.5
.

Suppose we run the Algorithm
5
for a DAG
H
. Let
S
be an arbitrary subset of the nodes.
Then

\Pr_{#1}\big\lparen Z_{k}=0,\forall k\in S\big\rparen=\Esym_{#1}\Bigg[\prod_{k\in S}\Big\lparen 1-\alpha_{k}^{(H)}\prod_{k^{\prime}\in N_{b}^{(H)}}\beta_{k^{\prime}}^{(H)}\Big\rparen\Bigg]

Proof.

By Lemma
F.4
, we can write

\mathbf{1}[Z_{k}=1]=\mathbf{1}[U_{k}=e_{1},U_{k^{\prime}}=*,\forall k^{\prime}\in N_{b}^{(H)}(i)]=\alpha_{k}^{(H)}\prod_{k^{\prime}\in N_{b}^{(H)}(i)}\beta_{k^{\prime}}^{(H)}\kern 5.0pt.

This allows us to write

\displaystyle\Pr_{#1}\big\lparen Z_{k}=0,\forall k\in S\big\rparen

\displaystyle=\Esym_{#1}\Bigg[\prod_{k\in S}\Big\lparen 1-\mathbf{1}[Z_{k}=1]\Big\rparen\Bigg]

\displaystyle=\Esym_{#1}\Bigg[\prod_{k\in S}\Big\lparen 1-\alpha_{k}^{(H)}\prod_{k^{\prime}\in N_{b}^{(H)}}\beta_{k}^{(H)}\Big\rparen\Bigg]\kern 5.0pt.\qed

The next Lemma allows us to linearize product expressions, which we will very often encounter in the proof.
Its proof is a standard application of Taylor’s theorem.

Lemma F.6
.

Let
a_{1},\ldotp,a_{p}\in Reals
be real numbers such that

\sum_{k=1}^{p}|a_{k}|\leq C\kern 5.0pt,

where
C>0
. Then,

\Big\lvert\prod_{k=1}^{p}(1+a_{k})-1\Big\rvert\leq e^{C}\sum_{k=1}^{p}|a_{k}|

Proof.

Let’s start by observing that for every
l\leq p
, using the inequality
1+x\leq e^{x}
, we have that

\Bigg\lvert\prod_{k=1}^{l}(1+a_{k})\Bigg\rvert=\prod_{k=1}^{l}(1+|a_{k}|)\leq e^{\sum_{k=1}^{l}|a_{k}|}\leq e^{C}

(50)

Now, we can write inductively

\displaystyle\Bigg\lvert\prod_{k=1}^{p}(1+a_{k})-1\Bigg\rvert

\displaystyle=\Big\lvert\prod_{k=1}^{p}(1+a_{k})-\prod_{k=1}^{p-1}(1+a_{k})+\prod_{k=1}^{p-1}(1+a_{k})-1\Big\rvert

\displaystyle\leq|a_{p}|\Bigg\lvert\prod_{k=1}^{p-1}(1+a_{k})\Bigg\rvert+\Bigg\lvert\prod_{k=1}^{p-1}(1+a_{k})+\prod_{k=1}^{p-1}(1+a_{k})-1\Bigg\rvert

by triangle inequality

\displaystyle\leq e^{C}|a_{p}|+\Bigg\lvert\prod_{k=1}^{p-1}(1+a_{k})+\prod_{k=1}^{p-1}(1+a_{k})-1\Bigg\rvert

by (
50
)

\displaystyle\leq\ldots\leq e^{C}\sum_{k=1}^{p}|a_{k}|\kern 5.0pt.\qed

We now prove that the probability that a subset of nodes are untreated is lower bounded by a constant, as long as the size of this subset is
\mathcal{O}\lparen\lambda(G)\rparen
.

Lemma F.7
.

Suppose we run the Conflict Graph Design on a DAG
H
and let
S
be a subset of nodes.
Furthermore, assume
\lambda(G)>1
.
Then, if
|S|\leq C\cdot\lambda(G)
, then

\Pr_{#1}\lparen Z_{k}=0,\forall k\in S\rparen\geq e^{-C}\kern 5.0pt.

Proof.

We observe that if all units in
S
receive
*
exposure, then they will all be untreated. Thus

\displaystyle\Pr_{#1}\lparen Z_{k}=0,\forall k\in S\rparen\geq\Pr_{#1}\lparen U_{k}=*,\forall k\in S\rparen=\Big\lparen 1-\frac{1}{\lambda(G)}\Big\rparen^{|S|}\geq\Big\lparen 1-\frac{1}{\lambda(G)}\Big\rparen^{C\cdot\lambda(G)}\kern 5.0pt.

In the last step, we used the assumption on the size of
S
.
Consider the function:

f(\lambda):=\Big\lparen 1-\frac{1}{\lambda}\Big\rparen^{C\lambda}\kern 5.0pt.

We can show that
f
is decreasing as long as
\lambda(G)>1
. Indeed,

f^{\prime}(\lambda)=C\Big\lparen\ln\Big\lparen 1-\frac{1}{\lambda}\Big\rparen+\frac{1}{\lambda-1}\Big\rparen f(\lambda)<0\kern 5.0pt,

where we use the inequality
\ln(1-1/\lambda)+1/(\lambda-1)<0
, for all
\lambda>1
.
Thus,

\Big\lparen 1-\frac{1}{\lambda(G)}\Big\rparen^{C\lambda(G)}\geq\lim_{\lambda\to\infty}f(\lambda)=e^{-C}\kern 5.0pt.\qed

We next present an important lemma that bounds the multiplicative difference between the probability of the same event, evaluated at two different directed graphs
H_{1}
and
H_{2}
that only differ locally. This situation will appear often once we start interpolating between different graphs that only differ locally in the proof of Theorem
4.6
.

Proposition F.8
.

Let
H_{1}=(V,E_{1}),H_{2}=(V,E_{2})
be two directed graphs defined on the same set of vertices. Let
v\in V
be a fixed node and
S\subseteq R\subseteq V
be two arbitrary subsets, such that
v\notin S
and
|R|\leq C\cdot\lambda(G)
.
Suppose further
H_{1}
and
H_{2}
differ only in edges that are incident to
v
and in particular that
v
has no edges with nodes in
S
in
H_{1}
but has directed edges to every node in
S
in
H_{2}
, i.e.
E_{2}=E_{1}\cup\{k\to v:k\in S\}
.
Then,

\Bigg\lvert\frac{\Pr_{#1}\lparen Z_{k}=0,\forall k\in R\rparen}{\Pr_{#1}\lparen Z_{k}=0,\forall k\in R\rparen}-1\Bigg\rvert\leq\frac{e^{C}}{2}\cdot\frac{|S|}{\lambda(G)^{2}}\kern 5.0pt.

Proof.

We begin by writing the difference in the two probabilities according to Lemma
F.5

\displaystyle\Pr_{#1}\lparen Z_{k}=0,\forall k\in R\rparen-\Pr_{#1}\lparen Z_{k}=0,\forall k\in R\rparen

\displaystyle\qquad=\Esym_{#1}\Bigg[\prod_{k\in R}\Big\lparen 1-\alpha_{k}^{(H_{2})}\prod_{k^{\prime}\in N_{b}^{(H_{2})}}\beta_{k}^{(H_{2})}\Big\rparen\Bigg]-\Esym_{#1}\Bigg[\prod_{k\in R}\Big\lparen 1-\alpha_{k}^{(H_{1})}\prod_{k^{\prime}\in N_{b}^{(H_{1})}}\beta_{k}^{(H_{1})}\Big\rparen\Bigg]\kern 5.0pt.

The crucial observation here is that we can
couple
the two distributions of the treatment assignment vector for
H_{1}
and
H_{2}
.
We can do this simply by coupling the two executions of the Conflict Graph Design for
H_{1}
and
H_{2}
as follows: for all
k\in V
, we
sample independently a common variable
U_{k}
for both graphs and set

\displaystyle\alpha_{k}^{(H_{1})}

\displaystyle=\alpha_{k}^{(H_{2})}:=\alpha_{k}=\mathbf{1}[U_{k}=e_{1}]

\displaystyle\beta_{k}^{(H_{1})}

\displaystyle=\beta_{k}^{(H_{2})}:=\beta_{k}=\mathbf{1}[U_{k}=*]\kern 5.0pt.

We remark that while the random
U_{1}\dots U_{n}
are shared between
H_{1}
and
H_{2}
, the resulting distribution of
Z
will be different under
H_{1}
and
H_{2}
because the graphs, and thus topological orderings, are different.
This is a valid coupling as it preserves the marginal distribution of
Z
under
H_{1}
and
H_{2}
.
We will denote expectation with respect to this coupling simply as
\mathbb{E}
, since now everything lies in the same probability space.
The only difference in sampling for
H_{1}
and
H_{2}
now is that in
H_{2}
the nodes in
S
have one extra parent, which is
v
.
Armed with this observation, we can write

\displaystyle\Pr_{#1}\lparen Z_{k}=0,\forall k\in R\rparen-\Pr_{#1}\lparen Z_{k}=0,\forall k\in R\rparen

\displaystyle\qquad=\operatorname{E}\left[\Bigg\lparen\prod_{k\in S}\Big\lparen 1-\alpha_{k}\beta_{v}\prod_{k^{\prime}\in N_{b}^{(H_{1})}(k^{\prime})}\beta_{k^{\prime}}\Big\rparen-\prod_{k\in S}\Big\lparen 1-\alpha_{k}\prod_{k^{\prime}\in N_{b}^{(H_{1})}(k^{\prime})}\beta_{k^{\prime}}\Big\rparen\Bigg\rparen\right.

\displaystyle\left.\qquad\times\prod_{k\in R\setminus S}\Big\lparen 1-\alpha_{k}\prod_{k^{\prime}\in N_{b}^{(H_{1})}(k)}\beta_{k^{\prime}}\Big\rparen\right]\kern 5.0pt.

Now, some simple algebra, along with the fact that
\alpha_{v}\beta_{v}=0
always yields

\displaystyle\prod_{k\in S}\Big\lparen 1-\alpha_{k}\beta_{v}\prod_{k^{\prime}\in N_{b}^{(H_{1})}(k)}\beta_{k^{\prime}}\Big\rparen-\prod_{k\in S}\Big\lparen 1-\alpha_{k}\prod_{k^{\prime}\in N_{b}^{(H_{1})}(k)}\beta_{k^{\prime}}\Big\rparen

\displaystyle=\Bigg\lparen\sum_{T\subseteq S}(-1)^{|T|}\Big\lparen\beta_{v}^{\mathbf{1}[T\neq\emptyset]}-1\Big\rparen\prod_{k\in T}\Big\lparen\alpha_{k}\prod_{k^{\prime}\in N_{b}^{(H_{1})}(k)}\beta_{k^{\prime}}\Big\rparen\Bigg\rparen

\displaystyle=\Bigg\lparen\sum_{T\subseteq S,T\neq\emptyset}(-1)^{|T|}\Big\lparen\beta_{v}-1\Big\rparen\prod_{k\in T}\Big\lparen\alpha_{k}\prod_{k^{\prime}\in N_{b}^{(H_{1})}(k)}\beta_{k^{\prime}}\Big\rparen\Bigg\rparen

\displaystyle=\Big\lparen\beta_{v}-1\Big\rparen\sum_{T\subseteq S,T\neq\emptyset}(-1)^{|T|}\prod_{k\in T}\Big\lparen\alpha_{k}\prod_{k^{\prime}\in N_{b}^{(H_{1})}(k)}\beta_{k^{\prime}}\Big\rparen

\displaystyle=\Big\lparen 1-\beta_{v}\Big\rparen\Bigg\lparen 1-\prod_{k\in S}\Big\lparen 1-\alpha_{k}\prod_{k^{\prime}\in N_{b}^{(H_{1})}(k)}\beta_{k^{\prime}}\Big\rparen\Bigg\rparen

\displaystyle=\mathbf{1}[U_{v}\neq*]\mathbf{1}[\cup_{k\in S}\{Z_{k}=1\}]\kern 5.0pt.

The last step follows from the definition of the indicators
\alpha_{k},\beta_{k}
. Substituting back to the expectation, we get

\displaystyle\Pr_{#1}\lparen Z_{k}=0,\forall k\in R\rparen-\Pr_{#1}\lparen Z_{k}=0,\forall k\in R\rparen

\displaystyle\qquad=\Esym\Bigg[\Big\lparen 1-\beta_{v}\Big\rparen\Bigg\lparen 1-\prod_{k\in S}\Big\lparen 1-\alpha_{k}\prod_{k^{\prime}\in N_{b}^{(H_{1})}(k)}\beta_{k^{\prime}}\Big\rparen\Bigg\rparen\prod_{k\in R\setminus S}\Big\lparen 1-\alpha_{k}\prod_{k^{\prime}\in N_{b}^{(H_{1})}(k)}\beta_{k^{\prime}}\Big\rparen\Bigg]

\displaystyle\qquad=\Pr_{#1}\Big\lparen\{U_{v}\neq*\}\cap(\cup_{k\in S}\{Z_{k}=1\})\cap(\cap_{k\in R\setminus S}\{Z_{k}=0\})\Big\rparen\kern 5.0pt.

The last step follows since in the expression of the probability we have excluded
v
as a parent of the nodes in
S
, hence we follow the topology of
H_{1}
.
At this point, we can draw some conclusions. The first is that clearly
\Pr_{#1}\lparen Z_{k}=0,\forall k\in R\rparen\geq\Pr_{#1}\lparen Z_{k}=0,\forall k\in R\rparen
.
This is to be expected because
H_{2}
contains more edges so that it is more likely that nodes in
S
will “yield” to the desires of their parent
v
and thus receive control.
We also get a nice expression for the difference in these probabilities.
By Lemma
F.4
, we conclude that
U_{v}=*
affects the treatment assignment of itself and its children.
Thus, we can write

\displaystyle\Pr_{#1}\Bigg\lparen\big\{U_{v}\neq*\big\}\cap\Big\{\cup_{k\in S}\{Z_{k}=1\}\Big\}\cap\Big\{\cap_{k\in R\setminus S}\{Z_{k}=0\}\Big\}\Bigg\rparen

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\Pr_{#1}\Bigg\lparen\big\{U_{v}\neq*\big\}\cap\Big\{\cup_{k\in S}\{Z_{k}=1\}\Big\}\cap\Big\{\cap_{k\in R\setminus(S\cup\{v\})}\{Z_{k}=0\}\Big\}\Bigg\rparen

The above follows by monotonicity since
R\setminus S\subseteq R\setminus(S\cup\{v\})
. Notice that it might be the case that
v
does not belong to
R\setminus S
, but the conclusion still holds.
The only treatment assignments that variable
U_{v}
affects other than
Z_{v}
are
Z_{k}
for
k\in N_{a}^{(H_{1})}(v)
. We can thus continue as

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\Pr_{#1}\Bigg\lparen\big\{U_{v}\neq*\big\}\cap\Big\{\cup_{k\in S}\{Z_{k}=1\}\Big\}\cap\Big\{\cap_{k\in R\setminus(S\cup\{v\}\cup N_{a}^{(H_{1})}(v))}\{Z_{k}=0\}\Big\}\Bigg\rparen

which follows since, by Lemma
F.4
,
U_{v}\neq*
implies that
Z_{k}=0
for all
k\in N_{a}^{(H_{1})}(v)
. Now notice that the treatment of units in
R\setminus\{\{v\}\cup N_{a}^{(H_{1})}(v)\}
is independent of
U_{v}
. Hence we can continue as

\displaystyle\qquad=\Pr\lparen U_{k}\neq*\rparen\cdot\Pr_{#1}\Bigg\lparen\Big\{\cup_{k\in S}\{Z_{k}=1\}\Big\}\cap\Big\{\cap_{k\in R\setminus(S\cup\{v\}\cup N_{a}^{(H_{1})}(v))}\{Z_{k}=0\}\Big\}\Bigg\rparen

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\frac{1}{\lambda(G)}\sum_{k\in S}\operatorname{Pr}_{H_{1}}\left(\{U_{k}=e_{1}\}\cap\Big\{\cap_{k^{\prime}\in N_{b}^{(H_{1})}(k)}\{U_{k^{\prime}}=*\}\Big\}\cap\right.

\displaystyle\qquad\left.\Big\{\cap_{k\in R\setminus(S\cup N_{a}^{(H_{1})}(v)\cup\{v\})}\{Z_{k}=0\}\Big\}\right)

where the above follows from a union bound. We get a further upper bound by evaluating the probability of the larger event,

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\frac{1}{\lambda(G)}\sum_{k\in S}\Pr\lparen U_{k}=e_{1}\rparen

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\frac{|S|}{2\lambda(G)^{2}}\kern 5.0pt.

We now use Lemma
F.7
for graph
H_{1}
and set
S
, which has size at most
C\lambda(G)
units by assumption. Thus, we have

\displaystyle\Pr_{#1}\lparen Z_{k}=0,\forall k\in R\rparen

\displaystyle-\Pr_{#1}\lparen Z_{k}=0,\forall k\in R\rparen

\displaystyle\leq\frac{|S|}{2\lambda(G)^{2}}

(above)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{|S|}{2\lambda(G)^{2}}\cdot\Big\{e^{C}\Pr_{#1}\lparen Z_{k}=0,\forall k\in R\rparen\Big\}

(Lemma
F.7
)

\displaystyle=\frac{e^{C}}{2}\cdot\frac{|S|}{\lambda(G)^{2}}\cdot\Pr_{#1}\lparen Z_{k}=0,\forall k\in R\rparen\kern 5.0pt.

Moreover, we have that
\Pr_{#1}\lparen Z_{k}=0,\forall k\in R\rparen\geq\Pr_{#1}\lparen Z_{k}=0,\forall k\in R\rparen
.
By rearranging terms and using this non-negativity, we attain the desired result:

\Bigg\lvert\frac{\Pr_{#1}\lparen Z_{k}=0,\forall k\in R\rparen}{\Pr_{#1}\lparen Z_{k}=0,\forall k\in R\rparen}-1\Bigg\rvert\leq e^{C}\cdot\frac{|S|}{2\lambda(G)^{2}}\kern 5.0pt.\qed

F.4
Correlation Lemma: Interpolation Phase I

The next Sections will be devoted to establishing results that will aid with the proof of Theorem
F.2
.
Intuitively, the events
E_{A},E_{B}
depend on the assignments of the
U
variables in a neighborhood of
A,B
. These neighborhoods could be overlapping, which introduces a lot of dependence between the two events for the two subsets. We will disentangle these two events by slowly transforming the local topology around
A
and
B
, with the final goal of making
E_{A}
and
E_{B}
independent in the end.
The key technical challenge will be to monitor how
C_{A,B}
changes along this transformation.

Our goal is to bound

\big\lvert C_{A,B}\big\rvert=\Bigg\lvert\frac{\Pr_{#1}\lparen E_{A}\cap E_{B}\rparen}{\Pr_{#1}\lparen E_{A}\rparen\Pr_{#1}\lparen E_{B}\rparen}-1\Bigg\rvert\kern 5.0pt.

We would essentially like to know how close events
E_{A},E_{B}
are to being independent. To do that, we will slowly transform
G
into different topologies, with the goal of keeping the marginal probabilities
\Pr\lparen E_{A}\rparen,\Pr\lparen E_{B}\rparen
fixed, but slowly changing the probability of the intersection, so that in the final topology
\Pr\lparen E_{A}\cap E_{B}\rparen=\Pr\lparen E_{A}\rparen\cdot\Pr\lparen E_{B}\rparen
holds.

\displaystyle 1
\displaystyle 2
\displaystyle 3
\displaystyle 4
\displaystyle 5
\displaystyle 6_{A}
\displaystyle 7_{A}
\displaystyle 8
\displaystyle A^{(1)}
\displaystyle B^{(1)}
\displaystyle 6_{B}
\displaystyle 7_{B}
\displaystyle 1
\displaystyle 2
\displaystyle 3
\displaystyle 4
\displaystyle 5
\displaystyle 6
\displaystyle 7
\displaystyle 8
\displaystyle A
\displaystyle B
Phase I
Phase II
\displaystyle 1
\displaystyle 2
\displaystyle 3
\displaystyle 4
\displaystyle 5
\displaystyle 6_{A}
\displaystyle 7_{A}
\displaystyle 8
\displaystyle A^{(1)}
\displaystyle B^{(1)}
\displaystyle 6_{B}
\displaystyle 7_{B}
\displaystyle 3^{c}
\displaystyle 4^{c}
\displaystyle 5^{c}
\displaystyle 1^{c}
Phase III
\displaystyle 1
\displaystyle 2_{A}
\displaystyle 3
\displaystyle 4
\displaystyle 5
\displaystyle 6_{A}
\displaystyle 7_{A}
\displaystyle 8
\displaystyle A^{(1)}
\displaystyle B^{(1)}
\displaystyle 6_{B}
\displaystyle 7_{B}
\displaystyle 3^{c}
\displaystyle 4^{c}
\displaystyle 5^{c}
\displaystyle 1^{c}
\displaystyle 2_{B}

Figure 20
:
Example of Full Interpolation
:
The three phases of the interpolation process for two selected sets
A,B
of an example graph. Note that the only phase that extends the sets
A,B
is Phase I, when we get the sets
A^{(1)},B^{(1)}
that are used for the rest of the interpolation. We remark that we never need to explicitly construct these graphs; they are only used in the variance analysis for the standard Horvitz-Thompson estimator.

The interpolation is carried out in three phases.
In this section, we describe Phase I of this interpolation process and focus on bounding the changes in the probability of
E_{A\cup B}
.
Phase II and Phase III are described in Sections
F.5
and
F.6
, respectively.
In Figure
20
, we present an illustration of a particular graph
G
going through the three phases.

The first source of dependence between events
E_{A}
and
E_{B}
comes from the fact that their intersection of
A
and
B
might be nonempty.
The idea will be to compare the distribution of the Conflict Graph Design when run on the original graph
G
versus a modification of
G
where
A
and
B
no longer intersect each other.
We call this modified graph
G^{(1)}
and its construction is described in pseudocode in Algorithm
6
. We note that we do not actually need to run this Algorithm and it is only useful to consider it for the sake of analysis.

Input:

Graph
G=(V,E)
, ordering
\pi
, subsets
A,B\subseteq V
.

Output:

Graph
G^{(1)}=(V_{1},E_{1})
and sets
A^{(1)}

B^{(1)}

Initialize vertices
V^{(1)}\leftarrow V
and edges
E^{(1)}\leftarrow E

Initialize sets
A_{0}\leftarrow A,B_{0}\leftarrow B
and graph
G_{0}=G
.

Construct the intersection
I\leftarrow A\cap B

Let
\{k_{1},\ldotp,k_{|I|}\}
be the nodes of
I
in decreasing order of
\pi
.

for

i=1
to |I|

do

1

/*
Step 1: Make two copies
k_{i,A}
and
k_{i,B}
of
k_{i}
. */

V^{(1)}\leftarrow(V^{(1)}\cup\{k_{i,A},k_{i,B}\})\setminus\{k_{i}\}

E^{(1)}\leftarrow(E^{(1)}\cup\{k^{\prime}\to k_{i,A}:k^{\prime}\in N_{b}^{(G)}(k_{i})\}\cup\{k^{\prime}\to k_{i,B}:k^{\prime}\in N_{b}^{(G)}(k_{i})\})\setminus\{k^{\prime}\to k_{i}:k^{\prime}\to k_{i}\in E\}

E^{(1)}\leftarrow E^{(1)}\cup\{k_{i,B}\to k^{\prime}:k^{\prime}\in N_{a}^{(G_{i-1})}(k_{i})\}

A_{i}\leftarrow\big\lparen A_{i-1}\setminus\{k_{i}\}\big\rparen\cup\{k_{i,A}\}

B_{i}\leftarrow\big\lparen B_{i-1}\setminus\{k_{i}\}\big\rparen\cup\{k_{i,B}\}

G_{i}\leftarrow(V^{(1)},E^{(1)})

/*
Step 2: Connect
k_{i,B}
to children of
k_{i}
in
B_{i}
*/

E^{(1)}\leftarrow E^{(1)}\cup\{k_{i,A}\to k^{\prime}:k^{\prime}\in N_{a}^{(G_{i})}(k_{i})\cap A_{i}\}

G_{i}^{\prime}\leftarrow(V^{(1)},E^{(1)})

/*
Step 3: Disconnect
k_{i,A}
from children of
k_{i}
in
B_{i}
*/

E^{(1)}\leftarrow E^{(1)}\setminus\{k_{i,B}\to k^{\prime}:k^{\prime}\in N_{a}^{(G_{i}^{\prime})}(k_{i})\cap A_{i}\}

G_{i}^{\prime\prime}\leftarrow(V^{(1)},E^{(1)})

A^{(1)}\leftarrow A_{|I|}

B^{(1)}\leftarrow B_{|I|}

Algorithm 6

Phase I Interpolation: from
G
to
G^{(1)}

See Figure
21
for an illustration of this first phase.
The algorithm begins by constructing the intersection
A\cap B=I
.
The procedure then iterates over nodes
k_{i}\in I
in the intersection, starting from the least important node, and creates two copies
k_{i,A}
and
k_{i,B}
. The iteration for
k_{i}
can be broken down in three smaller steps. In the first step, copy
k_{i,B}
is identified with
k_{i}
, while
k_{i,A}
has the same parent set as
k
. We call this topology
G_{i}=(V_{k},E_{k})
.
In the second step, we add edges from
k_{A}
to all units in
N_{a}^{(G)}(k)\cap A
. We call this topology
G_{i}^{\prime}=(V_{k}^{\prime},E_{k}^{\prime})
. Finally, in the third step, we remove all edges from
k_{B}
to
N_{a}^{(G)}(k)\cap A
. We call this topology
G_{i}^{\prime\prime}=(V_{k}^{\prime\prime},E_{k}^{\prime\prime})
.
This process continues until
I
becomes empty.
If we denote the copies
I_{A},I_{B}
of
I
, then
notice that at the end of Algorithm
6

A^{(1)}=\lparen A\setminus I\rparen\cup I_{A}
and
B^{(1)}=\lparen B\setminus I\rparen\cup I_{B}
.
If the intersection is empty in the original graph (i.e.
A\cap B=I=\emptyset
) then the graph
G^{(1)}
returned by Phase I is identical to the original input graph
G
.

An example of the first phase transformation is given in Figure
20
.

\displaystyle G_{i-1}
\displaystyle G_{i}
\displaystyle G_{i}^{\prime}
\displaystyle G_{i}^{\prime\prime}
\displaystyle k_{i}
\displaystyle N_{a}^{G_{i-1}}(k_{i})\cap A_{i}
\displaystyle N_{a}^{G_{i-1}}(k_{i})\cap B_{i}
\displaystyle N_{b}(k_{i})
\displaystyle k_{i,B}
\displaystyle N_{b}(k_{i})
\displaystyle k_{i,A}
\displaystyle N_{a}^{G_{i-1}}(k_{i})\cap A_{i}
\displaystyle N_{a}^{G_{i-1}}(k_{i})\cap B_{i}
\displaystyle k_{i,B}
\displaystyle N_{b}(k_{i})
\displaystyle k_{i,A}
\displaystyle N_{a}^{G_{i-1}}(k_{i})\cap A_{i}
\displaystyle N_{a}^{G_{i-1}}(k_{i})\cap B_{i}
\displaystyle k_{i,B}
\displaystyle N_{b}(k_{i})
\displaystyle k_{i,A}
\displaystyle N_{a}^{G_{i-1}}(k_{i})\cap A_{i}
\displaystyle N_{a}^{G_{i-1}}(k_{i})\cap B_{i}

Figure 21
:
Interpolation Phase I.

The purpose of this transformation is to make the sets
A^{(1)}
and
B^{(1)}
disjoint in
G^{(1)}
, while ensuring that the local topology of
A^{(1)}
and
B^{(1)}
in
G^{(1)}
matches that of
A
and
B
from
G
.
In fact, the following lemma shows that the marginal distributions of
A^{(1)}
and
B^{(1)}
in
G^{(1)}
match those of
A
and
B
in
G
.

Lemma F.9
.

Given the construction of the Phase I graph
G^{(1)}
in Algorithm
6
and the sets
A^{(1)}
and
B^{(1)}
above, the marginals are preserved:

\Pr_{#1}\lparen E_{A}\rparen=\Pr_{#1}\lparen E_{A^{(1)}}\rparen\quad\text{and}\quad\Pr_{#1}\lparen E_{B}\rparen=\Pr_{#1}\lparen E_{B^{(1)}}\rparen\kern 5.0pt.

Proof.

We prove the claim for
A
and
A^{(1)}
, as the situation for
B
and
B^{(1)}
is identical.
The proof essentially follows from observing that we can couple the execution of Algorithm
5
for
G
and
G^{(1)}
so that the outputs are the same for all variables in
A
and
A^{(1)}
, respectively.
More specifically, we can find a one-to-one and onto mapping
\phi:V\to V_{1}
by simply mapping every
k\in I
to its corresponding copy
k_{A}\in I_{A}
, while the remaining nodes are mapped to themselves.
We can check that by the construction of
G^{(1)}
(Algorithm
6
), that for any
k\in A
, the sets
N_{b}^{(G)}(k)
and
N_{b}^{(G^{(1)})}(\phi(k))
are in one-to-one correspondence using
\phi
.

To define the coupling, for each
k\in A
and corresponding
\phi(k)\in A^{(1)}
, we use the same variable
U_{k}
to run Algorithm
5
. In other words

\alpha_{k}^{(G)}=\alpha_{\phi(k)}^{(G^{(1)})}=\mathbf{1}[U_{k}=e_{1}]\qquad\beta_{k}^{(G)}=\beta_{\phi(k)}^{(G^{(1)})}=\mathbf{1}[U_{k}=*]\kern 5.0pt.

It is then clear that the output of Algorithm
5
when run on
G
and
G^{(1)}
will be exactly the same, restricted to the sets
A
and
A^{(1)}
respectively. This concludes the argument.
∎

Our goal will be to study how the probability
\Pr\lparen E_{A}\cup E_{B}\rparen
changes when we interpolate from
G
to
G^{(1)}
. Since the “analog” of
A,B
in
G^{(1)}
is
A^{(1)},B^{(1)}
, we seek to control the quantity:

\Bigg\lvert\frac{\Pr_{#1}\lparen E_{A\cup B}\rparen}{\Pr_{#1}\lparen E_{{A^{(1)}}\cup B^{(1)}}\rparen}-1\Bigg\rvert=\Bigg\lvert\frac{\Pr_{G}\left(Z_{k}=0,\forall k\in A\cup B\right)}{\Pr_{G^{(1)}}\left(Z_{k}=0,\forall k\in A^{(1)}\cup B^{(1)}\right)}-1\Bigg\rvert\kern 5.0pt.

To analyze the above quantity, it will be useful to consider the smaller steps of the transformation
that are described in Algorithm
6
.

In particular, let
k_{1},\ldotp k_{|I|}
be the decreasing ordering of the nodes in
I
. For a node
k_{i}\in I
, we wish to analyze how the probability
\Pr_{#1}\lparen E_{A\cup B}\rparen
changes along the transition
G_{{i-1}}^{\prime\prime}\to G_{i}\to G_{i}^{\prime}\to G_{i}^{\prime\prime}
.
In order to do this, we must define the “analog” of
A\cup B
in
G_{i},G_{i}^{\prime},G_{i}^{\prime\prime}
, since
A,B
do not exist anymore in these graphs.
To this end, we need natural “analogues” of
A
and
B
in these graphs. These are given by the intermediate sets
A_{i},B_{i}
. In particular,
A_{i}
contains all copies of nodes
k_{1,A},\ldotp,k_{i,A}
and the rest of the original nodes on
A
, similarly for
B_{i}
.
Our goal will be to study the following three quantities

\mathcal{P}_{i}:=\Pr_{#1}\lparen E_{A_{i}\cup B_{i}}\rparen\quad\mathcal{P}_{i}^{\prime}:=\Pr_{#1}\lparen E_{A_{i}\cup B_{i}}\rparen\quad\mathcal{P}_{i}^{\prime\prime}:=\Pr_{#1}\lparen E_{A_{i}\cup B_{i}}\rparen\kern 5.0pt.

Our first Lemma establishes that
\mathcal{P}_{i-1}^{\prime\prime},\mathcal{P}_{i}
are multiplicatively close.

Lemma F.10
.

For some constant
C^{\prime}
we have that for every
i

\Bigg\lvert\frac{\mathcal{P}_{i-1}^{\prime\prime}}{\mathcal{P}_{i}}-1\Bigg\rvert\leq\frac{C^{\prime}}{\lambda(G)}

Proof.

We will couple the execution of Algorithm
5
for
G
and
G_{i}
. We can identify the
U_{k}
variables used in Algorithm
5
for all nodes in
G
and
G_{i}
using a one-to-one correspondence, except for node
k_{i,A}
, which only exists in
G_{i}
. Thus, in the coupling
k_{i,A}
will have an independent variable
U_{k_{i,A}}
assigned to it. Unit
k_{i}
in
G
corresponds to unit
k_{i,B}
in
G_{i}
and we use the variables
\alpha_{k_{i}},\beta_{k_{i}}
for both.
Thus, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{i-1}^{\prime\prime}-\mathcal{P}_{i}

\displaystyle=\operatorname{E}\left[\prod_{k\in A\cup B}\Big\lparen 1-\alpha_{k}\prod_{k^{\prime}\in N_{b}(k_{i})}\beta_{k^{\prime}}\Big\rparen-\right.

\displaystyle\qquad\left.\Big\lparen 1-\alpha_{k_{i,A}}\prod_{k^{\prime}\in N_{b}(k_{i,A})}\beta_{k^{\prime}}\Big\rparen\prod_{k\in A\cup B}\Big\lparen 1-\alpha_{k}\prod_{k^{\prime}\in N_{b}(k_{i})}\beta_{k^{\prime}}\Big\rparen\right]

\displaystyle=\Esym\Bigg[\alpha_{k_{i,A}}\prod_{k^{\prime}\in N_{b}(k_{i,A})}\beta_{k^{\prime}}\prod_{k\in A\cup B}\Big\lparen 1-\alpha_{k}\prod_{k^{\prime}\in N_{b}(k_{i})}\beta_{k^{\prime}}\Big\rparen\Bigg]

\displaystyle\leq\Esym\Bigg[\alpha_{k_{i,A}}\prod_{k\in A\cup B}\Big\lparen 1-\alpha_{k}\prod_{k^{\prime}\in N_{b}(k_{i})}\beta_{k^{\prime}}\Big\rparen\Bigg]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\lambda(G)}\Esym\Bigg[\prod_{k\in A\cup B}\Big\lparen 1-\alpha_{k}\prod_{k^{\prime}\in N_{b}(k_{i})}\beta_{k^{\prime}}\Big\rparen\Bigg]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\lambda(G)}\mathcal{P}_{i}\kern 5.0pt.

In the second to last step, we used the fact that variable
\alpha_{k_{i,A}}
does not affect any other nodes in the graph
G_{i}
, since
k_{i,A}
has no children in
G_{i}
.
It is also clear from the above expression that
\mathcal{P}_{i-1}^{\prime\prime}\geq\mathcal{P}_{i}
.
Rearranging the above gives

\Bigg\lvert\frac{\Pr_{G}\left(E_{A\cup B}\right)}{\mathcal{P}_{i}}-1\Bigg\rvert\leq\Bigg\lvert\frac{1}{1-1/(2\lambda(G))}-1\Bigg\rvert\leq\frac{C^{\prime}}{2\lambda(G)}\,,

(51)

where
C^{\prime}
is an absolute constant and we have used that
\lambda(G)\geq 2
.
∎

Our second Lemma establishes that
\mathcal{P}_{i},\mathcal{P}_{i}^{\prime}
are multiplicatively close.

Lemma F.11
.

For some constant
C^{\prime}
we have that for every
i

\Bigg\lvert\frac{\mathcal{P}_{i}}{\mathcal{P}_{i}^{\prime}}-1\Bigg\rvert\leq\frac{C^{\prime}}{\lambda(G)}

Proof.

In this step, we add edges from
k_{i,A}
to all nodes in
N_{a}(k_{i})\cap A_{i}
.
We can view this as an instance of Proposition
F.8
where
H_{1}=G_{i},H_{2}=G_{i}^{\prime},R=A_{i}\cup B_{i},S=N_{a}(k_{i})\cap A,v=k_{i,A}
.
Observe that
S\subseteq R
and that
|R|\leq 2C\lambda(G)+1
.
Hence, there exists constants
C^{\prime}
and
C^{\prime\prime}
that only depend on
C
, such that

\Bigg\lvert\frac{\mathcal{P}_{i}}{\mathcal{P}_{i}^{\prime}}-1\Bigg\rvert\leq C^{\prime}\frac{|S|}{\lambda(G)^{2}}\leq C^{\prime}\cdot\frac{2C\lambda(G)+1}{\lambda(G)^{2}}\leq C^{\prime\prime}\frac{1}{\lambda(G)}\kern 5.0pt.\qed

Our third Lemma establishes that
\mathcal{P}_{i}^{\prime},\mathcal{P}_{i}^{\prime\prime}
are multiplicatively close.

Lemma F.12
.

For some constant
C^{\prime}
we have that for every
i

\Bigg\lvert\frac{\mathcal{P}_{i}^{\prime}}{\mathcal{P}_{i}^{\prime\prime}}-1\Bigg\rvert\leq\frac{C^{\prime}}{\lambda(G)}\kern 5.0pt.

Proof.

At this step, we remove the edges from
k_{i,B}
to the set
N_{a}(k_{i})\cap A
.
Again, we can view this as an instance of Proposition
F.8
where
H_{1}=G_{i}^{\prime\prime},H_{2}=G_{i}^{\prime},R=A_{i}\cup B_{i},S=N_{a}(k_{i})\cap A,v=k_{i,B}
.
Observe that
S\subseteq R
and that
|R|\leq 2C\lambda(G)+1
.
Hence, by Proposition
F.8
, there exists constants
C^{\prime}
and
C^{\prime\prime}
that only depends on
C
, such that

\Bigg\lvert\frac{\mathcal{P}_{i}^{\prime}}{\mathcal{P}_{i}^{\prime\prime}}-1\Bigg\rvert\leq C^{\prime}\frac{|S|}{\lambda(G)^{2}}\leq C^{\prime}\cdot\frac{2C\lambda(G)+1}{\lambda(G)^{2}}\leq C^{\prime\prime}\frac{1}{\lambda(G)}\kern 5.0pt.\qed

Finally, we provide a Lemma that quantifies the change in probability between graphs
G
and
G^{(1)}
, using the previous three Lemmas as building blocks. The proof proceeds by essentially adding up all the errors accumulated over the individual steps in Algorithm
6
.

Lemma F.13
.

There exists a constant
C^{\prime}
only depending on
C
, such that

\Bigg\lvert\frac{\Pr_{#1}\lparen E_{A\cup B}\rparen}{\Pr_{#1}\lparen E_{A^{(1)}\cup B^{(1)}}\rparen}-1\Bigg\rvert\leq C^{\prime}\frac{|I|}{\lambda(G)}\kern 5.0pt.

(52)

Proof.

By definition, we have
G_{|I|}^{\prime\prime}=G^{(1)}
and thus
\mathcal{P}_{|I|}^{\prime\prime}=\Pr_{#1}\lparen E_{A^{(1)}\cup B^{(1)}}\rparen
.
Using the convention
\Pr_{#1}\lparen E_{A\cup B}\rparen=G_{0}^{\prime\prime}
, we can write the telescopic product

\displaystyle\Bigg\lvert\frac{\Pr_{#1}\lparen E_{A\cup B}\rparen}{\Pr_{#1}\lparen E_{A^{(1)}\cup B^{(1)}}\rparen}-1\Bigg\rvert

\displaystyle=\Bigg\lvert\prod_{i=1}^{|I|}\Big\lparen\frac{\mathcal{P}_{i-1}^{\prime\prime}}{\mathcal{P}_{i}}\cdot\frac{\mathcal{P}_{i}}{\mathcal{P}_{i}^{\prime}}\cdot\frac{\mathcal{P}_{i}^{\prime}}{\mathcal{P}_{i}^{\prime\prime}}\Big\rparen-1\Bigg\rvert\kern 5.0pt.

We can now use Lemma
F.6
with

a_{1,i}=\frac{\mathcal{P}_{i-1}^{\prime\prime}}{\mathcal{P}_{i}}-1\qquad a_{2,i}=\frac{\mathcal{P}_{i}}{\mathcal{P}_{i}^{\prime}}-1\qquad a_{3,i}=\frac{\mathcal{P}_{i}^{\prime}}{\mathcal{P}_{i}^{\prime\prime}}-1\kern 5.0pt.

Indeed, by Lemmas
F.10
,
F.11
and
F.12
we get that, for a constant
C^{\prime}

\sum_{i=1}^{|I|}\lparen|a_{1,i}|+|a_{2,i}|+|a_{3,i}|\rparen\leq C^{\prime}\frac{|I|}{\lambda(G)}\leq C^{\prime}\frac{d_{\textrm{max}}}{\lambda(G)}\leq C^{\prime}\cdot C\kern 5.0pt.

Thus, by Lemma
F.6
we get that for some constant
C^{\prime}

\Bigg\lvert\frac{\Pr_{#1}\lparen E_{A\cup B}\rparen}{\Pr_{#1}\lparen E_{A^{(1)}\cup B^{(1)}}\rparen}-1\Bigg\rvert\leq C^{\prime}\sum_{i=1}^{|I|}\lparen|a_{1,i}|+|a_{2,i}|+|a_{3,i}|\rparen\leq C^{\prime\prime}\frac{|I|}{\lambda(G)}\kern 5.0pt.\qed

F.5
Correlation Lemma: Interpolation Phase II

Phase I ensures that
A^{(1)}
and
B^{(1)}
are disjoint sets, i.e.
A^{(1)}\cap B^{(1)}=\emptyset
. The goal of Phase II will be to eliminate edges from
A^{(1)}
to
B^{(1)}
. Intuitively, this will reduce correlations even further between the two sets. As always, our goal will be to preserve the marginal probabilities for
A^{(1)}
and
B^{(1)}
.
We now define the graph
G^{(2)}=(V_{2},E_{2})
through an iterative procedure described in Algorithm
7
. Again, we emphasize that this is a procedure that we study for the sake of analysis, we don’t need to actually run it.

Input:

Graph
G^{(1)}=(V^{(1)},E^{(1)})
, subsets
A^{(1)},B^{(1)}\subseteq V_{1}
.

Output:

Graph
G^{(2)}=(V^{(2)},E^{(2)})

V^{(2)}\leftarrow V^{(1)},E^{(2)}\leftarrow E^{(1)}

Let
A^{(1)}=\{k_{1},\ldotp,k_{|A^{(1)}|}\},B^{(1)}=\{l_{1},\ldotp,l_{|B^{(1)}|}\}
be arbitrary ordering.

for

i=1
to
|A^{(1)}|

do

1

/*
Step 1: Create a copy
k_{i}^{(c)}
of
k_{i}
and connect it to children of
k_{i}
in
B^{(1)}
*/

V^{(2)}\leftarrow V^{(2)}\cup\{k_{i}^{(c)}\}

E^{(2)}\leftarrow E^{(2)}\cup\{k_{i}^{(c)}\to k^{\prime}:k^{\prime}\in N_{a}^{(G^{(1)})}(k_{i})\cap B^{(1)}\}

G_{i}^{(1)}\leftarrow(V^{(2)},E^{(2)})

/*
Step 2: Remove edges from
k_{i}
to its children in
B^{(1)}
*/

E^{(2)}\leftarrow E^{(2)}\setminus\{k_{i}\to k^{\prime}:k^{\prime}\in N_{a}^{(G^{(1)})}(k_{i})\cap B^{(1)}\}

\big\lparen G_{i}^{(1)}\big\rparen^{\prime}\leftarrow(V^{(2)},E^{(2)})

for

i=1
to
|B^{(1)}|

do

2

/*
Step 1: Create a copy of
l_{i}^{(c)}
of
l_{i}
and connect it to children of
l_{i}
in
A^{(1)}
*/

V^{(2)}\leftarrow V^{(2)}\cup\{l_{i}^{(c)}\}

E^{(2)}\leftarrow E^{(2)}\cup\{l_{i}^{(c)}\to l^{\prime}:l^{\prime}\in N_{a}^{(G^{(1)})}(l_{i})\cap A^{(1)}\}

G_{|A|+i}^{(1)}\leftarrow(V^{(2)},E^{(2)})

/*
Step 2: Remove edges from
l_{i}
to its children in
A^{(1)}
*/

E^{(2)}\leftarrow E^{(2)}\setminus\{k_{i}\to l^{\prime}:l^{\prime}\in N_{a}^{(G^{(1)})}(l_{i})\cap A^{(1)}\}

\big\lparen G_{|A|+i}^{(1)}\big\rparen^{\prime}\leftarrow(V^{(2)},E^{(2)})

Algorithm 7

Phase II Interpolation: from
G^{(1)}
to
G^{(2)}

In every iteration of Phase II, we select a node
k_{i}
that has children on the other subset. Without loss of generality, suppose we choose
k\in A^{(1)}
with
N_{a}(k)\cap B^{(1)}\neq\emptyset
.
We then create a copy
k^{(c)}
and connect
k_{i}^{(c)}
as a parent to
N_{a}^{(G^{(1)})}(k_{i})\cap B^{(1)}
.
We call this topology
G^{(1)}_{i}
.
Then, we delete all edges from
k_{i}
to
N_{a}^{(G^{(1)})}(k_{i})\cap B^{(1)}
.
We call the resulting topology
(G^{(1)}_{i})^{\prime}
.
Note that the sets
A^{(1)},B^{(1)}
remain the same, i.e.
k_{i}^{(c)}
is not added to them.
Pictorially,the steps of a single iteration are shown in Figure
22
.
An example of the phase II transformation is given in Figure
20
.

\displaystyle k_{i}^{(c)}
\displaystyle k_{i}
\displaystyle N_{a}(k_{i})\cap A^{(1)}
\displaystyle N_{a}(k_{i})\cap B^{(1)}
\displaystyle A^{(1)}
\displaystyle B^{(1)}
\displaystyle k_{i}
\displaystyle N_{a}(k_{i})\cap A^{(1)}
\displaystyle N_{a}(k_{i})\cap B^{(1)}
\displaystyle A^{(1)}
\displaystyle B^{(1)}
\displaystyle k_{i}^{(c)}
\displaystyle k_{i}
\displaystyle N_{a}(k_{i})\cap A^{(1)}
\displaystyle N_{a}(k_{i})\cap B^{(1)}
\displaystyle A^{(1)}
\displaystyle B^{(1)}
\displaystyle G_{i}^{(1)}
\displaystyle G_{i-1}^{(1)}
\displaystyle(G_{i}^{(1)})^{\prime}

Figure 22
:
Interpolation Phase II.

As in the previous phase, we argue that the marginals of the distribution on
A^{(1)},B^{(1)}
don’t change from
G^{(1)}
to
G^{(2)}
.

Lemma F.14
.

Given the construction of the Phase II graph
G^{(2)}
in Algorithm
7
and the sets
A^{(1)}
and
B^{(1)}
above, the marginals are preserved:

\Pr_{#1}\lparen E_{A^{(1)}}\rparen=\Pr_{#1}\lparen E_{A^{(1)}}\rparen\quad\text{and}\quad\Pr_{#1}\lparen E_{B^{(1)}}\rparen=\Pr_{#1}\lparen E_{B^{(1)}}\rparen\kern 5.0pt.

Proof.

We prove the claim for
A^{(1)}
, as the proof for
B^{(1)}
is similar.
Similarly to the proof of Lemma
F.9
, we couple the distributions of
A^{(1)}
when Algorithm
5
is run for
G^{(1)}
and
G^{(2)}
, respectively. In particular, we can define a mapping
\phi:V_{1}\mapsto V_{2}
, where every node in
A^{(1)}
is mapped to itself and every node
k\in B^{(1)}
with
N_{a}^{(G^{(1)})}(k)\cap A^{(1)}\neq\emptyset
is mapped to it’s copy
k_{0}^{(c)}
.
Then, we identify
k\in V_{1}
and
\phi(k)\in V_{2}
with the same random variable
U_{k}
in the execution.
It is straightforward to check using Algorithm
7
that for every
k\in A^{(1)}
, the sets
N_{b}^{(G^{(1)})}(k)
and
N_{b}^{(G^{(2)})}(k)
are in one-to-one correspondence using
\phi
. Thus, the output of the coupled executions will be the same for
G^{(1)}
and
G^{(2)}
and the proof is complete.
∎

Our goal will be to study how
\Pr\lparen E_{A^{(1)}\cup B^{(1)}}\rparen
changes from
(G_{i-1}^{(1)})^{\prime}
to
G^{(1)}_{i}
and then to
(G^{(1)}_{i})^{\prime}
.
Thus, let us define the quantities

\mathcal{P}_{i}=\Pr_{#1}\lparen E_{A^{(1)}\cup B^{(1)}}\rparen\quad\text{and}\quad\mathcal{P}_{i}^{\prime}=\Pr_{#1}\lparen E_{A^{(1)}\cup B^{(1)}}\rparen\kern 5.0pt.

The first lemma establishes that
\mathcal{P}_{i-1}^{\prime},\mathcal{P}_{i}
are multiplicatively close to each other.

Lemma F.15
.

For every
0\leq i\leq|A|+|B|
, the following holds

•

If
i\leq|A|
, then

\Bigg\lvert\frac{\mathcal{P}_{i-1}^{\prime}}{\mathcal{P}_{i}}-1\Bigg\rvert\leq C^{\prime}\frac{|N_{a}^{(G^{(1)})}(k_{i})\cap B^{(1)}|}{\lambda(G)^{2}}

•

If
i>|A|
, then

\Bigg\lvert\frac{\mathcal{P}_{i-1}^{\prime}}{\mathcal{P}_{i}}-1\Bigg\rvert\leq C^{\prime}\frac{|N_{a}^{(G^{(1)})}(l_{i-|A|})\cap A^{(1)}|}{\lambda(G)^{2}}

Proof.

The case analysis on the indices simply corresponds to analysing the copies in
A^{(1)}
and
B^{(1)}
, respectively. We focus on the case
i\leq|A|
for simplicity, the proof is identical for the other case.
Fix
k_{i}\in A^{(1)}
. The difference from
\mathcal{P}_{i-1}^{\prime}
to
\mathcal{P}_{i}
is that we are adding an extra parent
k_{i}^{(c)}
for all nodes in
N_{a}^{(G^{(1)})}(k_{i})\cap B^{(1)}
.
Thus, we can apply Proposition
F.8
with
H_{1}=(G_{i-1}^{(1)})^{\prime},H_{2}=G^{(1)}_{i},R=A^{(1)}\cup B^{(1)},S=N_{a}^{(G^{(1)})}(k_{i})\cap B^{(1)},v=k_{i}^{(c)}
. The assumption of Proposition
F.8
is satisfied, since
|R|\leq 2C\lambda(G)
.
Thus, there exists a constant
C^{\prime}
such that

\Bigg\lvert\frac{\mathcal{P}_{i-1}^{\prime}}{\mathcal{P}_{i}}-1\Bigg\rvert\leq C^{\prime}\frac{|S|}{\lambda(G)^{2}}=C^{\prime\prime}\frac{|N_{a}^{(G^{(1)})}(k_{i})\cap B^{(1)}|}{\lambda(G)^{2}}\kern 5.0pt.\qed

The second lemma establishes that
\mathcal{P}_{i},\mathcal{P}_{i}^{\prime}
are multiplicatively close to each other.

Lemma F.16
.

For every
0\leq i\leq|A|+|B|
, the following holds

•

If
i\leq|A|
, then

\Bigg\lvert\frac{\mathcal{P}_{i}}{\mathcal{P}_{i}^{\prime}}-1\Bigg\rvert\leq C^{\prime}\frac{|N_{a}^{(G^{(1)})}(k_{i})\cap B^{(1)}|}{\lambda(G)^{2}}

•

If
i>|A|
, then

\Bigg\lvert\frac{\mathcal{P}_{i}}{\mathcal{P}_{i}^{\prime}}-1\Bigg\rvert\leq C^{\prime}\frac{|N_{a}^{(G^{(1)})}(l_{i-|A|})\cap A^{(1)}|}{\lambda(G)^{2}}

Proof.

As in Lemma
F.15
, the case analysis on the indices simply corresponds to analysing the copies in
A^{(1)}
and
B^{(1)}
, respectively. We focus on the case
i\leq|A|
for simplicity, the proof is identical for the other case.
Fix
k_{i}\in A^{(1)}
. The difference from
\mathcal{P}_{i}
to
\mathcal{P}_{i}^{\prime}
is that we are removing the parent
k_{i}
from all nodes in
N_{a}^{(G^{(1)})}(k_{i})\cap B^{(1)}
.
Thus, we can apply Proposition
F.8
with
H_{1}=(G_{i}^{(1)})^{\prime},H_{2}=G^{(1)}_{i},R=A^{(1)}\cup B^{(1)},S=N_{a}^{(G^{(1)})}(k_{i})\cap B^{(1)},v=k_{i}
. The assumption of Proposition
F.8
is satisfied, since
|R|\leq 2C\lambda(G)
.
Thus, there exists a constant
C^{\prime}
such that

\Bigg\lvert\frac{\mathcal{P}_{i}}{\mathcal{P}_{i}^{\prime}}-1\Bigg\rvert\leq C^{\prime}\frac{|S|}{\lambda(G)^{2}}=C^{\prime\prime}\frac{|N_{a}^{(G^{(1)})}(k_{i})\cap B^{(1)}|}{\lambda(G)^{2}}\kern 5.0pt.\qed

We can now combine the previous two lemmas to bound the multiplicative change in the probability of event
E_{A^{(1)}\cup B^{(1)}}
from
G^{(1)}
to
G^{(2)}
.

Lemma F.17
.

There exists a constant
C^{\prime}
depending only on
C
, such that

\Bigg\lvert\frac{\Pr_{#1}\lparen E_{A^{(1)}\cup B^{(1)}}\rparen}{\Pr_{#1}\lparen E_{A^{(1)}\cup B^{(1)}}\rparen}-1\Bigg\rvert\leq C^{\prime}\frac{F_{A,B}}{\lambda(G)^{2}}

(53)

Proof.

Notice that
G^{(1)}_{|A|+|B|}=G^{(2)}
by definition. By taking the convention
(G^{(1)}=G_{0}^{(1)})^{\prime}
, we can write the telescopic product

\Bigg\lvert\frac{\Pr_{#1}\lparen E_{A^{(1)}\cup B^{(1)}}\rparen}{\Pr_{#1}\lparen E_{A^{(1)}\cup B^{(1)}}\rparen}-1\Bigg\rvert=\Bigg\lvert\prod_{i=1}^{|A|+|B|}\Big\lparen\frac{\mathcal{P}_{i-1}^{\prime}}{\mathcal{P}_{i}}\cdot\frac{\mathcal{P}_{i}}{\mathcal{P}_{i}^{\prime}}\Big\rparen-1\Bigg\rvert

We can now use Lemma
F.6
with

a_{1,i}=\frac{\mathcal{P}_{i-1}^{\prime}}{\mathcal{P}_{i}}-1\qquad a_{2,i}=\frac{\mathcal{P}_{i}}{\mathcal{P}_{i}^{\prime}}-1\kern 5.0pt.

Indeed, by Lemmas
F.15
,
F.16
we get that, for a constant
C^{\prime}

\sum_{i=1}^{|I|}\lparen|a_{1,i}|+|a_{2,i}|\rparen\leq C^{\prime}\frac{\sum_{k\in A}|N_{a}^{(G^{(1)})}(k)\cap B^{(1)}|+\sum_{k\in B}|N_{a}^{(G^{(1)})}(k)\cap A^{(1)}|}{\lambda(G)^{2}}\leq C^{\prime\prime}\kern 5.0pt,

where we used the fact that each node in
A^{(1)}\cup B^{(1)}
has at most
d_{\textrm{max}}\leq C\lambda(G)
children, and
|A^{(1)}\cup B^{(1)}|=\mathcal{O}\lparen\lambda(G)\rparen
.
Thus, by Lemma
F.6
we get that for some constant
C^{\prime}

\displaystyle\Bigg\lvert\frac{\Pr_{#1}\lparen E_{A\cup B}\rparen}{\Pr_{#1}\lparen E_{A^{(1)}\cup B^{(1)}}\rparen}-1\Bigg\rvert

\displaystyle\leq C^{\prime}\sum_{i=1}^{|I|}\lparen|a_{1,i}|+|a_{2,i}|+|a_{3,i}|\rparen

\displaystyle\leq C^{\prime\prime}\frac{\sum_{k\in A}|N_{a}^{(G^{(1)})}(k)\cap B^{(1)}|+\sum_{k\in B}|N_{a}^{(G^{(1)})}(k)\cap A^{(1)}|}{\lambda(G)^{2}}\kern 5.0pt.

To prove the claim, it suffices to establish that

\sum_{k\in A}|N_{a}^{(G^{(1)})}(k)\cap B^{(1)}|+\sum_{k\in B}|N_{a}^{(G^{(1)})}(k)\cap A^{(1)}|=F_{A,B}\kern 5.0pt.

We show that the left hand side counts exactly the edges that contribute to
F_{A,B}
. Indeed, edges with both endpoints in

A\setminus B
or both endpoints in
B\setminus A
will not be altered in Phase I and thus will not be counted in the left hand side on Phase II. Also, edges
(u,v)
with both endpoints in
I
in
G
will be duplicated to two edges
(u_{A},v_{A})
and
(u_{B},v_{B})
, which lie inside
A^{(1)},B^{(1)}
respectively and thus will not be counted in the left hand side.
The remaining types of edges, which have the two endpoints in different subsets of the partition
A\setminus B,B\setminus A,A\cap B
can easily be shown to correspond to edges between
A^{(1)}
and
B^{(1)}
after Phase I. This concludes the proof.
∎

F.6
Correlation Lemma: Interpolation Phase III

Phase II removed all edges between
A^{(1)},B^{(1)}
. The only remaining correlations between
A^{(1)},B^{(1)}
is now coming from common parents of nodes in
A^{(1)},B^{(1)}
that do not belong to
A^{(1)}\cup B^{(1)}
.
Our goal in Phase III will be to remove these dependencies.
We now describe the final topology
G^{(3)}
that will be the end of interpolation. The topology of
G^{(3)}
can be constructed iteratively from
G^{(2)}
by the process that is described in Algorithm
8
.

Input:

Graph
G^{(2)}=(V^{(2)},E^{(2)})
, subsets
A^{(1)},B^{(1)}\subseteq V^{(2)}
.

Output:

Graph
G^{(3)}=(V^{(3)},E^{(3)})

V^{(3)}\leftarrow V^{(2)},E^{(3)}\leftarrow E^{(2)}

T_{A,B}\leftarrow\{k\notin A^{(1)}\cup B^{(1)}:N_{a}^{(G^{(2)})}(k)\cap A^{(1)}\neq\emptyset,N_{a}^{(G^{(2)})}(k)\cap B^{(1)}\neq\emptyset\}

Let
T_{A,B}=\{k_{1},\ldotp,k_{|R_{A,B}|}\}
be arbitrary ordering.

for

i=1
to
|T_{A,B}|

do

1

/*
Create two copies
k_{i,A}
and
k_{i,B}
of
k_{i}
*/

V^{(3)}\leftarrow(V^{(3)}\cup\{k_{i,A},k_{i,B}\})\setminus\{k_{i}\}

/*
Remove
k_{i}
and its edges from
G^{(2)}
*/

E^{(3)}\leftarrow E^{(3)}\setminus\{(k_{i},k^{\prime}):k^{\prime}\in N^{G^{(2)}}(k_{i})\}

/*
Connect
k_{i,A}
to the children of
k_{i}
in
A^{(1)}
and
k_{i,B}
to the children of
k_{i}
in
B^{(1)}
*/

E^{(3)}\leftarrow E^{(3)}\cup\{k_{i,A}\to k^{\prime}:k^{\prime}\in N_{a}^{(G^{(2)})}(k_{i})\cap A^{(1)}\}\cup\{k_{i,B}\to k^{\prime}:k^{\prime}\in N_{a}^{(G^{(2)})}(k_{i})\cap B^{(1)}\}

G_{i}^{(2)}\leftarrow(V^{(3)},E^{(3)})

Algorithm 8

Phase III Interpolation: from
G^{(2)}
to
G^{(3)}

Algorithm
8
selects one common parent
k_{i}\in T_{A,B}
in each iteration. It creates two copies
k_{i,A},k_{i,B}
, and connects
k_{i,A}
to the children of
k_{i}
in
A^{(1)}
and
k_{i,B}
with the children of
k_{i}
in
B^{(1)}
.
We call this topology
G^{(2)}_{i}
.
Pictorially, the steps of a single iteration are shown in Figure
23
.
An example of the Phase III transformation is given in Figure
20
.

\displaystyle k_{i}
\displaystyle N_{a}(k_{i})\cap A^{(1)}
\displaystyle N_{a}(k_{i})\cap B^{(1)}
\displaystyle A^{(1)}
\displaystyle B^{(1)}
\displaystyle k_{i,B}
\displaystyle k_{i,A}
\displaystyle N_{a}(k_{i})\cap A^{(1)}
\displaystyle N_{a}(k_{i})\cap B^{(1)}
\displaystyle A^{(1)}
\displaystyle B^{(1)}
\displaystyle G_{i}^{(2)}
\displaystyle G_{i-1}^{(2)}

Figure 23
:
Interpolation Phase III.

As in the previous phases, we establish that the marginals of
A^{(1)}
and
B^{(1)}
do not change from
G^{(2)}
to
G^{(3)}
.
In addition, we claim that events
E_{A^{(1)}}
and
E_{B^{(1)}}
are independent in
G^{(3)}
:

Lemma F.18
.

The events
E_{A}
and
E_{B}
are independent under the Conflict Graph Design given by the topological ordering of
G^{(3)}
:

\Pr_{#1}\Big\lparen E_{A^{(1)}\cup B^{(1)}}\Big\rparen=\Pr_{#1}\Big\lparen E_{A}\Big\rparen\cdot\Pr_{#1}\Big\lparen E_{B}\Big\rparen\kern 5.0pt.

Proof.

We have that

\displaystyle\Pr_{#1}\Big\lparen E_{A^{(1)}\cup B^{(1)}}\Big\rparen

\displaystyle=\Esym_{#1}\Bigg[\underbrace{\prod_{k\in A^{(1)}}\Big\lparen 1-\alpha_{k}\prod_{k^{\prime}\in N_{b}(k)}\beta_{k^{\prime}}\Big\rparen}_{Y_{A}}\cdot\underbrace{\prod_{k\in B^{(1)}}\Big\lparen 1-\alpha_{k}\prod_{k^{\prime}\in N_{b}(k)}\beta_{k^{\prime}}\Big\rparen}_{Y_{B}}\Bigg]

Notice that all variables that appear in the expression of random variable
Y_{A}
are independent from all variables that appear in
Y_{B}
. This follows from the property of
G^{(3)}
that
A^{(1)}\cap B^{(1)}=\emptyset
(because of Phase I), there are no edges between
A^{(1)}
and
B^{(1)}
(because of Phase II) and no-common parents between nodes in
A^{(1)}
and
B^{(1)}
(because of Phase III). Thus, the expectation can be broken into the product of expectations

\displaystyle\Pr_{#1}\Big\lparen E_{A^{(1)}\cup B^{(1)}}\Big\rparen

\displaystyle=\Esym_{#1}\Bigg[\prod_{k\in A^{(1)}}\Big\lparen 1-\alpha_{k}\prod_{k^{\prime}\in N_{b}(k)}\beta_{k^{\prime}}\Big\rparen\Bigg]

\displaystyle\qquad\cdot\Esym_{#1}\Bigg[\prod_{k\in B^{(1)}}\Big\lparen 1-\alpha_{k}\prod_{k^{\prime}\in N_{b}(k)}\beta_{k^{\prime}}\Big\rparen\Bigg]

\displaystyle=\Pr_{#1}\Big\lparen E_{A^{(1)}}\Big\rparen\cdot\Pr_{#1}\Big\lparen E_{B^{(1)}}\Big\rparen

To finish the proof, by Lemmas
F.9
and
F.14
, it suffices to prove that the marginals are the same as in
G^{(2)}
. Let’s focus on
A^{(1)}
, since the proof for
B^{(1)}
is identical. The strategy will be the same as in the proof of Lemma
F.14
. Let
\phi:V_{2}\mapsto V_{3}
be the mapping where every node
k\in T\subseteq V_{2}
is mapped to its copy
k_{i,A}\in T_{A}
. The rest of
\phi
is identity. Then, we can couple the executions of Algorithm
5
when run on
G^{(2)}
and
G^{(3)}
by using the same variable
U_{k}
for
k
and
\phi(k)
, for all
k\in V_{1}
.
It is straightforward to check using Algorithm
8
that for every
k\in A^{(1)}
, the sets
N_{b}^{(G^{(2)})}(k)
and
N_{b}^{(G^{(3)})}(\phi(k))
are in one-to-one correspondence using
\phi
. Thus, the outputs of the two executions will be identical for
A^{(1)}
and the proof is complete.
∎

As in the previous Phases, we analyze the change in
\Pr\lparen E_{A^{(1)}\cup B^{(1)}}\rparen
in one iteration of Algorithm
8
.
To do that, again we introduce the relevant notation

\mathcal{P}_{i}=\Pr_{#1}\lparen E_{A^{(1)}\cup B^{(1)}}\rparen\kern 5.0pt.

We also use the convention
\mathcal{P}_{0}=\Pr_{#1}\lparen E_{A^{(1)}\cup B^{(1)}}\rparen
.
The precise statement is given in the following Lemma.

Lemma F.19
.

For
i\leq|T_{A,B}|
, we have for some constant
C^{\prime}
that only depends on
C
that

\Bigg\lvert\frac{\mathcal{P}_{i-1}}{\mathcal{P}_{i}}-1\Bigg\rvert\leq C^{\prime}\frac{\Big\lvert N_{a}^{(G^{(2)})}(k_{i})\cap A^{(1)}\Big\rvert\cdot\Big\lvert N_{a}^{(G^{(2)})}(k_{i})\cap B^{(1)}\Big\rvert}{\lambda(G)^{3}}\kern 5.0pt.

Proof.

From
\mathcal{P}_{i-1}
to
\mathcal{P}_{i}
, we are removing
k_{i}
and its edges and are adding edges from
k_{i,A}
to all nodes in
N_{a}^{(G^{(2)})}(k_{i})\cap A^{(1)}
and from
k_{i,B}
to all nodes in
N_{a}^{(G^{(2)})}(k_{i})\cap B^{(1)}
.
Thus, we can couple the executions of the Conflict Graph design for
G^{(2)}_{i-1}
and
G^{(2)}_{i}
as follows: all nodes
k\neq k_{i}
in
G^{(2)}_{i-1}
are in one to one correspondence with all nodes
k\neq k_{i,A},k_{i,B}
in
G^{(2)}_{i}
, so we can use the same
U_{k}
random variable to sample them. For
k_{i},k_{i,A},k_{i,B}
we use random variables
U_{k_{i}}=U_{k_{i,A}}
and
U_{k_{i,B}}
, which are sampled independently.
Thus, with this coupling we can write

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{i-1}-\mathcal{P}_{i}

\displaystyle\qquad=\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{k\in N_{a}^{(G^{(2)})}(k_{i})\cap A^{(1)}}\Big\lparen 1-\alpha_{k}\cdot\beta_{k_{i}}\prod_{k^{\prime}\in N_{b}^{(G^{(2)}_{i-1})}(k)\setminus\{k_{i}\}}\beta_{k^{\prime}}\Big\rparen\right.

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\left.\cdot\prod_{k\in N_{a}^{(G^{(2)})}(k_{i})\cap B^{(1)}}\Big\lparen 1-\alpha_{k}\cdot\beta_{k_{i}}\prod_{k^{\prime}\in N_{b}^{(G^{(2)}_{i-1})}(k)\setminus\{k_{i}\}}\beta_{k^{\prime}}\Big\rparen\right.

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\cdot\left.\prod_{k\in A^{(1)}\cup B^{(1)}\setminus N_{a}^{(G^{(2)})}(k_{i})}\Big\lparen 1-\alpha_{k}\prod_{k^{\prime}\in N_{b}^{(G^{(2)}_{i-1})}(k)}\beta_{k^{\prime}}\Big\rparen\right]

\displaystyle\qquad-\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{k\in N_{a}^{(G^{(2)})}(k_{i})\cap A^{(1)}}\Big\lparen 1-\alpha_{k}\cdot\beta_{k_{i,A}}\prod_{k^{\prime}\in N_{b}^{(G^{(2)}_{i-1})}(k)\setminus\{k_{i}\}}\beta_{k^{\prime}}\Big\rparen\right.

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\left.\cdot\prod_{k\in N_{a}^{(G^{(2)})}(k_{i})\cap B^{(1)}}\Big\lparen 1-\alpha_{k}\cdot\beta_{k_{i,B}}\prod_{k^{\prime}\in N_{b}^{(G^{(2)}_{i-1})}(k)\setminus\{k_{i}\}}\beta_{k^{\prime}}\Big\rparen\right.

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\cdot\left.\prod_{k\in A^{(1)}\cup B^{(1)}\setminus N_{a}^{(G^{(2)})}(k_{i})}\Big\lparen 1-\alpha_{k}\prod_{k^{\prime}\in N_{b}^{(G^{(2)}_{i-1})}(k)}\beta_{k^{\prime}}\Big\rparen\right]

Notice that the only difference in the above two expressions is that the first has the same factor
\beta_{k_{i}}
for nodes in
N_{a}^{(G^{(2)})}(k_{i})\cap(A^{(1)}\cup B^{(1)})
, but the second expression has a factor
\beta_{k_{i,A}}
for nodes in
N_{a}^{(G^{(2)})}(k_{i})\cap A^{(1)}
and a factor
\beta_{k_{i,B}}
for nodes in
N_{a}^{(G^{(2)})}(k_{i})\cap B^{(1)}
.
Some simple algebra, using the facts that
\beta_{k_{i}}^{2}=\beta_{k_{i}},\beta_{k_{i}}=\beta_{k_{i,A}}
now shows that

\displaystyle\prod_{k\in N_{a}^{(G^{(2)})}(k_{i})\cap A^{(1)}}\Big\lparen 1-\alpha_{k}\cdot\beta_{k_{i}}\prod_{k^{\prime}\in N_{b}^{(G^{(2)}_{i-1})}(k)\setminus\{k_{i}\}}\beta_{k^{\prime}}\Big\rparen

\displaystyle\qquad\cdot\prod_{k\in N_{a}^{(G^{(2)})}(k_{i})\cap B^{(1)}}\Big\lparen 1-\alpha_{k}\cdot\beta_{k_{i}}\prod_{k^{\prime}\in N_{b}^{(G^{(2)}_{i-1})}(k)\setminus\{k_{i}\}}\beta_{k^{\prime}}\Big\rparen

\displaystyle\qquad-\prod_{k\in N_{a}^{(G^{(2)})}(k_{i})\cap A^{(1)}}\Big\lparen 1-\alpha_{k}\cdot\beta_{k_{i,A}}\prod_{k^{\prime}\in N_{b}^{(G^{(2)}_{i-1})}(k)\setminus\{k_{i}\}}\beta_{k^{\prime}}\Big\rparen

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\cdot\prod_{k\in N_{a}^{(G^{(2)})}(k_{i})\cap B^{(1)}}\Big\lparen 1-\alpha_{k}\cdot\beta_{k_{i,B}}\prod_{k^{\prime}\in N_{b}^{(G^{(2)}_{i-1})}(k)\setminus\{k_{i}\}}\beta_{k^{\prime}}\Big\rparen

\displaystyle=\big\lparen 1-\beta_{k_{i,B}}\big\rparen

\displaystyle\qquad\cdot\Bigg\lparen 1-\prod_{k\in N_{a}^{(G^{(2)})}(k_{i})\cap A^{(1)}}\Big\lparen 1-\alpha_{k}\cdot\beta_{k_{i}}\prod_{k^{\prime}\in N_{b}^{(G^{(2)}_{i-1})}(k)\setminus\{k_{i}\}}\beta_{k^{\prime}}\Big\rparen\Bigg\rparen

\displaystyle\qquad\cdot\Bigg\lparen 1-\prod_{k\in N_{a}^{(G^{(2)})}(k_{i})\cap B^{(1)}}\Big\lparen 1-\alpha_{k}\cdot\beta_{k_{i}}\prod_{k^{\prime}\in N_{b}^{(G^{(2)}_{i-1})}(k)\setminus\{k_{i}\}}\beta_{k^{\prime}}\Big\rparen\Bigg\rparen

Now, notice that the random variable
\big\lparen 1-\beta_{k_{i,B}}\big\rparen
is independent of everything else in the expectation, so we can write

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{i-1}-\mathcal{P}_{i}

\displaystyle\quad=\mathbb{E}\left[\big\lparen 1-\beta_{k_{i,B}}\big\rparen\right]\cdot

\displaystyle\qquad\cdot\mathbb{E}\left[\Bigg\lparen 1-\prod_{k\in N_{a}^{(G^{(2)})}(k_{i})\cap A^{(1)}}\Big\lparen 1-\alpha_{k}\cdot\beta_{k_{i}}\prod_{k^{\prime}\in N_{b}^{(G^{(2)}_{i-1})}(k)\setminus\{k_{i}\}}\beta_{k^{\prime}}\Big\rparen\Bigg\rparen\right.

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\left.\cdot\Bigg\lparen 1-\prod_{k\in N_{a}^{(G^{(2)})}(k_{i})\cap B^{(1)}}\Big\lparen 1-\alpha_{k}\cdot\beta_{k_{i}}\cdot\prod_{k^{\prime}\in N_{b}^{(G^{(2)}_{i-1})}(k)\setminus\{k_{i}\}}\beta_{k^{\prime}}\Big\rparen\Bigg\rparen\right.

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\left.\cdot\prod_{k\in A^{(1)}\cup B^{(1)}\setminus N_{a}^{(G^{(2)})}(k_{i})}\Big\lparen 1-\alpha_{k}\prod_{k^{\prime}\in N_{b}^{(G^{(2)}_{i-1})}(k)}\beta_{k^{\prime}}\Big\rparen\right]

\displaystyle\qquad=\frac{1}{\lambda(G)}\mathbb{P}_{G^{(2)}_{i-1}}\left[\Big\lparen\cup_{k\in N_{a}^{(G^{(2)})}(k_{i})\cap A^{(1)}}\{Z_{k}=1\}\Big\rparen\cap\Big\lparen\cup_{k\in N_{a}^{(G^{(2)})}(k_{i})\cap B^{(1)}}\{Z_{k}=1\}\Big\rparen\right.

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\left.\cap\Big\lparen\cup_{k\in(A^{(1)}\cup B^{(1)})\setminus N_{a}^{(G^{(2)})}(k_{i})}\{Z_{k}=0\}\Big\rparen\right]

by definition of the indicator events

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\frac{1}{\lambda(G)}\cdot

\displaystyle\qquad\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in N_{a}^{(G^{(2)})}(k_{i})\cap A^{(1)}\\
k^{\prime}\in N_{a}^{(G^{(2)})}(k_{i})\cap B^{(1)}\end{subarray}}\Pr_{#1}\Bigg\lparen\{Z_{k}=1\}\cap\{Z_{k^{\prime}}=1\}\cap\Big\lparen\cup_{k\in(A^{(1)}\cup B^{(1)})\setminus N_{a}^{(G^{(2)})}(k_{i})}\{Z_{k}=0\}\Big\rparen\Bigg\rparen

by union bound

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\frac{1}{\lambda(G)}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in N_{a}^{(G^{(2)})}(k_{i})\cap A^{(1)}\\
k^{\prime}\in N_{a}^{(G^{(2)})}(k_{i})\cap B^{(1)}\end{subarray}}\Pr_{#1}\Bigg\lparen\{U_{k}=1\}\cap\{U_{k^{\prime}}=1\}\Bigg\rparen

by monotonicity

\displaystyle\qquad=\frac{\big\lvert N_{a}^{(G^{(2)})}(k_{i})\cap A^{(1)}\big\rvert\cdot\big\lvert N_{a}^{(G^{(2)})}(k_{i})\cap B^{(1)}\big\rvert}{4\lambda(G)^{3}}

\displaystyle\qquad\leq C^{\prime}\frac{\big\lvert N_{a}^{(G^{(2)})}(k_{i})\cap A^{(1)}\big\rvert\cdot\big\lvert N_{a}^{(G^{(2)})}(k_{i})\cap B^{(1)}\big\rvert}{4\lambda(G)^{3}}\mathcal{P}_{i}\kern 5.0pt,

where we applied Lemma
F.7
to relate back to
\mathcal{P}_{i}
.
Rearranging the above gives the desired inequality.
∎

We are now ready to quantify the multiplicative difference of
\Pr_{#1}\lparen E_{A^{(1)}\cup B^{(1)}}\rparen
and
\Pr_{#1}\lparen E_{A^{(1)}\cup B^{(1)}}\rparen
.

Lemma F.20
.

There exists a constant
C^{\prime}
depending only on
C
, such that

\Bigg\lvert\frac{\Pr_{#1}\lparen E_{A^{(1)}\cup B^{(1)}}\rparen}{\Pr_{#1}\lparen E_{A^{(1)}\cup B^{(1)}}\rparen}-1\Bigg\rvert\leq C^{\prime}\frac{\sum_{k\in R_{A,B}}|N_{a}(k)\cap A|\cdot|N_{a}(k)\cap B|}{\lambda(G)^{3}}\kern 5.0pt.

(54)

Proof.

We can write the telescopic product

\Bigg\lvert\frac{\Pr_{#1}\lparen E_{A^{(1)}\cup B^{(1)}}\rparen}{\Pr_{#1}\lparen E_{A^{(1)}\cup B^{(1)}}\rparen}-1\Bigg\rvert=\Bigg\lvert\prod_{i=1}^{|T_{A,B}|}\frac{\mathcal{P}_{i-1}}{\mathcal{P}_{i}}-1\Bigg\rvert

We can now use Lemma
F.6
with

a_{i}=\frac{\mathcal{P}_{i-1}}{\mathcal{P}_{i}}-1\kern 5.0pt.

Indeed, by Lemma
F.19
we get that, for a constant
C^{\prime}

\sum_{i=1}^{|I|}\lparen|a_{i}|\rparen\leq C^{\prime}\frac{\sum_{k\in T_{A,B}}\big\lvert N_{a}^{(G^{(2)})}(k_{i})\cap A^{(1)}\big\rvert\cdot\big\lvert N_{a}^{(G^{(2)})}(k_{i})\cap B^{(1)}\big\rvert}{\lambda(G)^{3}}\leq C^{\prime\prime}\kern 5.0pt,

where we used the fact that each node in
A^{(1)}\cup B^{(1)}
has at most
\lambda(G)
parents, and
|A^{(1)}\cup B^{(1)}|=\mathcal{O}\lparen\lambda(G)\rparen
.
Thus, by Lemma
F.6
we get that for some constant
C^{\prime}

\displaystyle\Bigg\lvert\frac{\Pr_{#1}\lparen E_{A^{(1)}\cup B^{(1)}}\rparen}{\Pr_{#1}\lparen E_{A^{(1)}\cup B^{(1)}}\rparen}-1\Bigg\rvert

\displaystyle\leq C^{\prime}\sum_{i=1}^{|I|}\lparen|a_{1,i}|+|a_{2,i}|+|a_{3,i}|\rparen

\displaystyle\leq C^{\prime\prime}\frac{\sum_{k\in T_{A,B}}\big\lvert N_{a}^{(G^{(2)})}(k_{i})\cap A^{(1)}\big\rvert\cdot\big\lvert N_{a}^{(G^{(2)})}(k_{i})\cap B^{(1)}\big\rvert}{\lambda(G)^{3}}\kern 5.0pt.

The claim would follow if we can establish that

\sum_{k\in T_{A,B}}\big\lvert N_{a}^{(G^{(2)})}(k_{i})\cap A^{(1)}\big\rvert\cdot\big\lvert N_{a}^{(G^{(2)})}(k_{i})\cap B^{(1)}\big\rvert=\sum_{k\in R_{A,B}}|N_{a}(k)\cap A|\cdot|N_{a}(k)\cap B|

(55)

Indeed, it is easy to check that for any
k\notin A^{(1)}\cup B^{(1)}
,
N_{a}^{(G)}(k)\cap A^{(1)}
is in one-to-one corresponcence with
N_{a}^{(G^{(2)})}(k)
. This is the case because children of
k
that do not belong in the intersection
I=A^{(1)}\cap B^{(1)}
have not been altered, and if
k\to k^{\prime}
was an edge in
G
with
k^{\prime}\in I
, then
k\to k^{\prime}_{A}
will be an edge in
G^{(2)}
and vice versa. Similar claims hold for
B^{(1)}
. Thus, the sets
R_{A,B}
and
T_{A,B}
are the same and (
55
) holds.
∎

F.7
Proof of Correlation Lemma (Theorem
F.2
)

We now provide a proof of the Correlation Lemma (Theorem
F.2
).
For convenience, we restate the theorem here.

See
F.2

Proof.

We now combine the results of interpolation of the probability from the three different phases.
Indeed, using Lemmas
F.13
,
F.17
and
F.20
, together with Lemma
F.18
and Lemma
F.6
, we get that

\displaystyle C_{A,B}

\displaystyle=\Bigg\lvert\frac{\Pr_{#1}\lparen E_{A\cup B}\rparen}{\Pr_{#1}\lparen E_{A}\rparen\Pr_{#1}\lparen E_{B}\rparen}-1\Bigg\rvert

\displaystyle=\Bigg\lvert\frac{\Pr_{#1}\lparen E_{A\cup B}\rparen}{\Pr_{#1}\lparen E_{A^{(1)}\cup B^{(1)}}\rparen}\cdot\frac{\Pr_{#1}\lparen E_{A^{(1)}\cup B^{(1)}}\rparen}{\Pr_{#1}\lparen E_{A^{(1)}\cup B^{(1)}}\rparen}\cdot\frac{\Pr_{#1}\lparen E_{A^{(1)}\cup B^{(1)}}\rparen}{\Pr_{#1}\lparen E_{A^{(1)}\cup B^{(1)}}\rparen}-1\Bigg\rvert

\displaystyle\leq C^{\prime}\cdot\Bigg\lparen\frac{|I|}{\lambda(G)}+\frac{|F_{A,B}|}{\lambda(G)^{2}}+\frac{\sum_{k\in R_{A,B}}|N_{a}(k)\cap A|\cdot|N_{a}(k)\cap B|}{\lambda(G)^{3}}\Bigg\rparen\kern 5.0pt,

and the proof is complete.
∎
